1. Basics of C

Background / not directly examined

Foundation only — not asked as its own final-exam question, but every later topic depends on it.

Every C program is a set of instructions the compiler turns into a list of steps the CPU runs one after another. Before you can write any program you need four things: somewhere to put data (variables), a way to label their type (data types), a way to talk to the user (printf/scanf), and a way to combine values (operators). Everything else in this course — arrays, pointers, structures — is just a more organised way of holding the same variables.

Anatomy of a program

  • #include <stdio.h> — a preprocessor line. It copy-pastes the declarations for printf/scanf into your file before compiling.
  • int main() { ... } — every C program starts execution at main. The braces { } mark its scope: everything declared inside only exists inside those braces.
  • return 0; — tells the operating system the program finished without error. In lab/exam code you'll also see the older void main() style used in the slides; both are accepted here, but prefer int main() with return 0; unless told otherwise.

Data types you need this semester

TypeStoresFormat specifierExample
intwhole numbers%dint x = 103;
floatdecimals (single precision)%f / %.2ffloat y = 10.5;
doubledecimals (higher precision)%lf (scanf) / %f (printf)double z = 3.14159;
charone character%cchar c = 'a';

Variable naming: case-sensitive, letters/digits/underscore only, can't start with a digit, and can't be a reserved word (int, for, return, ...).

Taking input: scanf("%d", &x); — the & (address-of) is mandatory for ordinary variables because scanf needs the memory address to write the answer into, not the current value. (You'll meet & properly again in the Pointers section — this is the same operator.)

Arithmetic operators: + - * / % (the last is remainder, integer-only). Relational: == != > < >= <= each give 1 (true) or 0 (false). Logical: && (AND), || (OR), ! (NOT).

Printing with multiple format specifiers
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int courseCode = 103;
    int section = 2;
    printf("Welcome to EEE-%d Section-%d\n", courseCode, section);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int courseCode = 103;Declares an int variable named courseCode and immediately initializes it to 103.
int section = 2;Declares a second int variable, section, initialized to 2.
printf("Welcome to EEE-%d Section-%d\n", courseCode, section);The string is printed literally except for the two %d placeholders, which are replaced left-to-right by the values after the comma: courseCode fills the first %d, section fills the second. \n at the end moves the cursor to a new line.
return 0;Ends main and reports success (exit code 0) to the operating system.

int courseCode = 103; and int section = 2; declare two integer variables and give them initial values in the same statement.

printf("...%d Section-%d\n", courseCode, section) — each %d in the string is a placeholder. The values after the comma fill them in, left to right: courseCode replaces the first %d, section the second. \n moves the cursor to a new line; it does not print anything visible itself.

Output: Welcome to EEE-103 Section-2

What is wrong with scanf("%d", x); when x is an ordinary int variable?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

scanf writes the typed-in value into memory, so it must be told where to write — that's the job of &. Without it, the program usually crashes or silently misbehaves.

2. Control statements

Background / not directly examined

Foundation — used inside almost every solution, but not tested as a standalone final question.

Control statements decide which lines actually run. Without them a program is just a straight list of steps executed top to bottom every single time.

if — runs a block only when a condition is true.
if...else — exactly one of two blocks runs.
if...else if...else — conditions are tested top to bottom; the first true one runs and the rest are skipped, even if they'd also be true.
switch — compares one variable against a list of constant values; needs break; after every case or execution "falls through" into the next case.

Grade calculator using if / else if / else
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int marks;
    printf("Enter your marks: ");
    scanf("%d", &marks);

    if (marks >= 80) {
        printf("Grade: A\n");
    } else if (marks >= 60) {
        printf("Grade: B\n");
    } else if (marks >= 50) {
        printf("Grade: C\n");
    } else {
        printf("Grade: F\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int marks;Declares an int variable to hold the mark, with no value yet.
scanf("%d", &marks);Reads one integer typed by the user and stores it into marks. &marks gives scanf the address of marks so it knows where to write the value.
if (marks >= 80) { ... }First condition checked. If true, prints "Grade: A" and skips every else if/else below.
else if (marks >= 60) { ... }Only reached if the first condition was false. If this is true, prints "Grade: B" and skips the rest.
else if (marks >= 50) { ... }Only reached if both conditions above were false. If true, prints "Grade: C".
else { ... }Catches everything not matched above (marks below 50); prints "Grade: F".

The conditions are checked in order. For marks = 65: 65 >= 80 is false, so we drop to else if (marks >= 60) — 65 >= 60 is true, so "Grade: B" prints and every condition below it (>=50, the final else) is skipped entirely, even though marks >= 50 is also technically true.

This "first match wins, then stop checking" behaviour is the single most common source of logic mistakes in exam tracing questions — always ask "is this the first condition that matched?", not "is this condition true?".

In an if...else if...else chain, if the second condition and the third condition are both true, which block executes?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

C stops checking as soon as one condition is true. Later else ifs and the final else are simply skipped.

3. Loops

Core topic

Core mechanism — every array, sorting, DMA and string program in this course is built on a loop.

A loop repeats a block of code while a condition holds. There are three shapes; all three can solve the same problem, but each communicates a slightly different intent.

LoopChecks conditionRuns at least once?Best for
while(cond){...}before each passNounknown number of repeats
do{...}while(cond);after each passYes, alwaysmenus / "ask at least once"
for(init;cond;step){...}before each passNoa known counted repeat
for loop — print 0 to 9
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        printf("%d ", i);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int i;Declares the loop counter i (no value yet — it's given one by the loop's init clause next).
for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {Three clauses in one line: i = 0 runs once before the loop starts; i < 10 is the condition checked before every pass; i++ runs after every pass, before the condition is checked again.
printf("%d ", i);The loop body: prints the current value of i followed by a space. This runs once per pass, for i = 0,1,2,…,9.
return 0;Runs once, after the loop has fully finished (once i < 10 becomes false at i = 10).

A for loop packs three things onto one line, always executed in this order: (1) i = 0 runs once, before anything else. (2) i < 10 is checked before every pass through the body — if false, the loop ends immediately. (3) the body runs. (4) i++ runs, then we jump back to step 2. So the actual order is init → check → body → step → check → body → step → ... until the check fails.

do...while — the trap: runs even when the condition is already false
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int i = 10;
    do {
        printf("%d ", i);
        i++;
    } while (i < 10);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int i = 10;Declares i and initializes it to 10 — already at the value the condition will check against.
do {Marks the start of the loop body. Unlike while, this body is guaranteed to run at least once, because the condition isn't checked until the end.
printf("%d ", i);Prints the current value of i (10, on the only pass this loop makes).
i++;Increments i from 10 to 11, still inside the body, before the condition is checked.
} while (i < 10);Now checked for the first time: 11 < 10 is false, so the loop stops here — it never loops back.

i starts at 10. A while loop with condition i < 10 would never run its body at all. But do...while checks the condition after the body, so the body runs once unconditionally first: it prints 10, increments i to 11, then checks 11 < 10 → false → stops. Final output: just 10. This "runs once no matter what" behaviour is exactly why do...while is used for input-validation menus.

For int i = 5; while (i < 5) { printf("%d", i); i++; }, what prints?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

while checks the condition before the first pass. 5 < 5 is false immediately, so the body never runs.

4. Functions

Background / not directly examined

Optional tool, not a graded question type this semester — use it wherever it shortens your code.

Your faculty's rule Functions will not be demanded as their own question type in the final. But you are explicitly welcome to use a user-defined function inside any problem-solving question if it makes your code shorter and cleaner — e.g. a isPrime(int) helper inside a bigger array question. This tab teaches the mechanism so you can use it confidently as a tool, not because it will be examined on its own.

A function is a named, reusable block of code. It has three parts: a declaration/prototype (tells the compiler the name, return type and parameter types before it's used), a definition (the actual body), and a call (where you actually run it).

A function that returns a value
#include <stdio.h>

int square(int n) {          // declaration + definition combined
    return n * n;
}

int main() {
    int r = square(6);        // function call
    printf("%d\n", r);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int square(int n) {int (before the name) is the return type; square is the function's name; (int n) declares one parameter, n, a local variable that will hold a copy of whatever value is passed in when the function is called.
return n * n;Computes n multiplied by itself and immediately sends that value back to wherever square(...) was called from; the function ends here.
int r = square(6);Calls square with the argument 6 (a copy of 6 is placed into n inside the function), then stores whatever square returns (36) into the new variable r.
printf("%d\n", r);Prints the value now stored in r, i.e. 36.

int square(int n) — int before the name is the return type (what kind of value comes back); (int n) is the parameter, a local variable that receives a copy of whatever is passed in.

return n * n; computes the result and immediately hands it back to whoever called the function.

int r = square(6); — C copies 6 into n inside square (this is called pass-by-value: the function gets its own copy, so changing n inside square would never affect anything in main). The returned value 36 is stored in r.

Local vs global variables: a variable declared inside a function only exists while that function is running and is invisible to other functions. A variable declared outside every function (a global) is visible everywhere and lives for the whole program.

5. Arrays

Final exam topic

Final exam: combined with DMA for 13–14 marks.

An array is a block of variables of the same type sitting in contiguous (back-to-back) memory, all sharing one name and told apart by an index. Instead of int a, b, c, d, e; you write int arr[5]; — and, critically, you can now visit every element with a loop instead of writing five separate lines.

Declaration: int marks[10]; (10 uninitialised ints).
Initialisation:

  • Full: int nums[5] = {1,2,3,4,5};
  • Partial (rest become 0): int nums[5] = {1,2}; → {1,2,0,0,0}
  • Auto-size: int nums[] = {1,2,3,4,5}; — size is inferred as 5
  • All zero: int arr[10] = {0};

Indexing always starts at 0. For int arr[5], valid indices are arr[0] through arr[4]. Writing to arr[5] is out-of-bounds — C does not stop you, it just corrupts whatever memory happens to sit next to the array. This is the single most common silent bug in array questions: always double-check your loop condition is i < n, not i <= n.

The pattern behind almost every array question: one for loop to read n values in, then one or more loops to process them (sum, max/min, search, reverse, count, filter). Learn this input-loop once and reuse it everywhere:

The universal 'read n numbers into an array' pattern
int arr[n], i;
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    scanf("%d", &arr[i]);
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int arr[n], i;Declares an array of n ints (all currently uninitialised) and a loop counter i.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {Visits every valid index exactly once: starts at 0, stops before reaching n (so the last index touched is n-1, never n itself).
scanf("%d", &arr[i]);Reads one integer typed by the user and stores it into arr[i]. &arr[i] is the memory address of that specific slot; without &, scanf would have no idea where to write the value.

&arr[i] — exactly like &x for an ordinary variable, scanf needs the address of the box it's about to fill. arr[i] is that box; &arr[i] is where that box lives in memory.

Summing all elements
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {2, 4, 6, 8, 10};
    int sum = 0;
    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        sum = sum + arr[i];
    }
    printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int arr[5] = {2, 4, 6, 8, 10};Declares and fully initialises a 5-element array in one line; arr[0] is 2, arr[4] is 10.
int sum = 0;The running total, seeded at 0 so the first addition doesn't change anything unexpected.
for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {Visits index 0 through 4, once each.
sum = sum + arr[i];Adds the current element onto the running total and immediately stores the new total back into sum — this is the standard "accumulator" pattern.
printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);Runs once, after the loop, printing the final accumulated value.

sum starts at 0 (the "identity" value for addition — adding 0 changes nothing, so it's a safe starting point). The loop visits every index from 0 to 4 and adds that element into the running total. After the loop, sum holds 2+4+6+8+10 = 30.

Finding max and min in one pass
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {5, 12, 3, 27, 9};
    int max = arr[0];
    int min = arr[0];
    int i;
    for (i = 1; i < 5; i++) {
        if (arr[i] > max) max = arr[i];
        if (arr[i] < min) min = arr[i];
    }
    printf("Max = %d\n", max);
    printf("Min = %d\n", min);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int max = arr[0];Seeds max with the first element, not with 0 — a real starting candidate, since 0 could wrongly "win" if every value were negative.
int min = arr[0];Seeds min the same way, from the same first element.
for (i = 1; i < 5; i++) {Starts at index 1, not 0 — index 0 is already accounted for by the seeding above, so re-checking it would be redundant.
if (arr[i] > max) max = arr[i];If the current element beats the best-so-far maximum, it becomes the new maximum.
if (arr[i] < min) min = arr[i];A completely separate check (not else if) — every element is tested against both max and min independently.

Both max and min are seeded with arr[0] before the loop starts — never seed them with 0, because if every element were negative, 0 would incorrectly "win" as the maximum. The loop then starts from index 1 (index 0 is already accounted for) and, for every element, asks two independent questions: "is this bigger than the current max?" and "is this smaller than the current min?" — both checks run on every element, they are not else-linked.

int arr[4] = {1,2,3}; What is arr[3]?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

When you give fewer initial values than the declared size, C fills every remaining element with 0 — but only if you provide the braces {...} at all. An array declared with no initialiser (int arr[4];) instead holds garbage.

What is the most common bug when looping over int arr[n]?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

Valid indices run 0 .. n-1. i <= n lets the loop reach arr[n], which is one element past the end of the array — undefined behaviour that the compiler will not warn you about.

6. Bubble & selection sort

Core topic

On the syllabus; expect it folded into an array-based question rather than asked alone.

Both algorithms sort in place (no second array needed) using two nested loops — the outer loop counts passes, the inner loop does the comparing. They are on the syllabus as their own topic, but in practice a final exam question is more likely to ask you to sort an array as one step inside a bigger array/DMA question (e.g. "store n numbers, then print them sorted") than to ask for bubble sort in isolation — so learn both well enough to drop straight into any array question.

Bubble sort
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {5, 3, 8, 4, 2};
    int n = 5, i, j, temp;

    for (i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
        for (j = 0; j < n - i - 1; j++) {
            if (arr[j] > arr[j + 1]) {
                temp = arr[j];
                arr[j] = arr[j + 1];
                arr[j + 1] = temp;
            }
        }
    }

    printf("Sorted: ");
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {Counts passes. Only n-1 passes are ever needed — once every element but the last has been bubbled into place, the last one is automatically correct too.
for (j = 0; j < n - i - 1; j++) {The inner sweep for this pass. It shrinks by one each outer pass (-i) because the last i elements are already sorted into their final spots and don't need re-comparing.
if (arr[j] > arr[j + 1]) {Compares two side-by-side elements. If the left one is bigger than its right neighbour, they're in the wrong order.
temp = arr[j]; arr[j] = arr[j+1]; arr[j+1] = temp;The standard 3-line swap: save the left value in temp first, overwrite it with the right value, then write the saved temp into the right slot.

Bubble sort repeatedly compares neighbours and swaps them if they're in the wrong order, so on every full inner-loop pass the largest remaining value "bubbles" to the end. for (i = 0; i < n-1; i++) counts passes; for (j = 0; j < n-i-1; j++) is the inner sweep, and it gets shorter by one each pass (-i) because the last i elements are already correctly placed at the end and don't need re-checking. if (arr[j] > arr[j+1]) compares two neighbours; if the left one is bigger, the three-line swap using temp exchanges them (you always need a temporary variable to swap two values — otherwise the first assignment overwrites the value you still needed).

Selection sort
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {5, 3, 8, 4, 2};
    int n = 5, i, j, min_idx, temp;

    for (i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
        min_idx = i;
        for (j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
            if (arr[j] < arr[min_idx]) {
                min_idx = j;
            }
        }
        if (min_idx != i) {
            temp = arr[i];
            arr[i] = arr[min_idx];
            arr[min_idx] = temp;
        }
    }

    printf("Sorted: ");
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
min_idx = i;Assumes, for now, that the current outer position i holds the smallest remaining value — this assumption gets corrected below if it's wrong.
for (j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {Scans every element after position i (not before — everything before i is already sorted from previous passes).
if (arr[j] < arr[min_idx]) { min_idx = j; }Whenever a smaller value is found, min_idx is updated to remember its index (not its value) — so at the end of the loop, min_idx points at the smallest element in the unsorted remainder.
if (min_idx != i) { ... swap ... }Only swap if the smallest value wasn't already sitting at position i — avoids a pointless self-swap.

Selection sort instead finds the index of the smallest remaining value in one inner pass, then swaps it into place — at most one swap per outer pass, versus bubble sort's potentially many. min_idx = i assumes the current position already holds the smallest value; the inner loop (j = i+1 onward) then checks every remaining element and updates min_idx whenever it finds something smaller. Only after the whole inner loop finishes do we swap — and only if (min_idx != i), skipping a pointless self-swap when the current position was already the smallest.

Why does bubble sort's inner loop use j < n - i - 1 instead of just j < n - 1?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

After each outer pass, the largest unsorted value has bubbled to its correct final position at the end. Shrinking the inner loop by one avoids wasted comparisons — it's an optimisation, not a requirement for correctness.

7. Strings

Final exam topic

Final exam: bonus question, 3–4 marks — but the manual-loop technique here also appears inside DMA questions.

A C string is simply a char array with one extra rule: it must end with the invisible null character '\0'. That single character is how every string function knows where the text stops — there is no separate "length" stored anywhere.

char str[6] = "Hello"; actually stores 6 bytes: 'H','e','l','l','o','\0' — the literal "Hello" has 5 visible characters but the compiler silently appends the null terminator, so the array must be at least one byte bigger than the visible text.

Input rules: scanf("%s", str); needs no & (an array name already is an address — more on this in Pointers) and stops reading at the first space, so it can't read a full sentence. fgets(str, sizeof(str), stdin); reads a whole line including spaces and is the safe choice whenever the input might contain multiple words — but it also keeps the trailing '\n' in the string, which you often need to strip or account for.

No built-in string functions in this course: strlen, strcpy, strcat, strcmp exist in <string.h>, but every lab/assignment in this course hand-rolls them with a loop against '\0' instead — that is exactly what the exam expects too.

Counting characters (hand-rolled strlen)
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100];
    int count = 0;
    printf("Enter a string: ");
    scanf("%s", str);
    while (str[count] != '\0') {
        count++;
    }
    printf("Total characters: %d\n", count);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
char str[100];Reserves room for up to 100 characters (including the null terminator) to hold the input string.
int count = 0;The running character tally, starting at 0.
scanf("%s", str);Reads one whitespace-terminated word into str. No & before str — an array name already refers to its own address.
while (str[count] != '\0') {Keeps looping as long as the character at position count is not the null terminator.
count++;Advances to the next character. This runs once per real character, so when the loop stops, count equals the string's length.

count starts at 0. while (str[count] != '\0') keeps advancing through the array one index at a time, stopping the instant it hits the null terminator — at that point count equals exactly the number of real characters before it, because it was incremented once for every character it stepped over.

Reversing a string
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100];
    int len = 0, i;
    printf("Enter a string: ");
    scanf("%s", str);
    while (str[len] != '\0') len++;
    for (i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        printf("%c", str[i]);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
while (str[len] != '\0') len++;The same length-finding loop as the previous example, written compactly on one line: keeps advancing len until it hits the null terminator.
for (i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) {Starts at the last real character (len - 1, since str[len] itself is the null terminator, not a real character) and walks backwards down to index 0.
printf("%c", str[i]);Prints one character at a time (%c, not %s, since we're printing a single char value here), in reverse order.

First we find the length exactly as above (len). Then for (i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) walks backwards from the last real character (index len-1 — note: not len, which is the null terminator itself) down to index 0, printing each character as it goes. Because we print instead of storing, no second array is needed.

char s[10] = "Hi"; How many bytes actually hold meaningful data (including the terminator)?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

The visible text is 2 characters, plus the compiler-added null terminator = 3 bytes used. The remaining 7 bytes in the 10-byte array exist but hold unused / leftover memory.

Why can't scanf("%s", str) read the sentence "hello world" into one string?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

%s with scanf treats a space as the end of input. Use fgets to capture a full line including spaces.

8. Pointers

Final exam topic

Final exam: tracing question, 7–8 marks. This is the single highest-value topic to master for tracing tables.

A pointer is a variable whose value is a memory address, not an ordinary number. Two operators do all the work:

  • & (address-of) — "give me the address where this variable lives".
  • * (dereference) — "go to the address stored here and give me / set the value sitting there". * also appears in the declaration int *p;, where it just means "p is a pointer to an int" — two different jobs for the same symbol, and exam tracing questions love to exploit exactly this dual meaning.

Declaring & assigning correctly:

int x = 10;
int *p;      // p is a pointer to int (currently pointing nowhere useful)
p = &x;      // correct: store the ADDRESS of x inside p

p = 10;      // WRONG: this makes p "point" at raw address 10 — almost certainly a crash
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *p;Declares p as a pointer to int. Here * is part of the type ("p is a pointer-to-int"), not a dereference — p itself doesn't point anywhere useful yet.
p = &x;Correct assignment: &x produces the address of x, which is exactly the kind of value a pointer is meant to hold.
p = 10;Incorrect: this stores the raw number 10 as if it were a memory address. Dereferencing p afterwards would try to read/write whatever happens to live at address 10 — not x, almost certainly a crash.

A pointer must always be assigned an address (something produced by &, another pointer, or an array name), never a raw value like 10. This is the single most common beginner mistake with pointers.

Reading and writing through a pointer
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int x = 10;
    int *p = &x;

    printf("%p\n", p);    // prints the address stored in p
    printf("%d\n", *p);   // prints the value at that address: 10

    *p = 20;               // changes x indirectly, through p
    printf("%d\n", x);    // x is now 20

    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int x = 10;An ordinary int variable, holding 10.
int *p = &x;Declares p as a pointer-to-int in the same statement it's assigned x's address — p now "points at" x.
printf("%p\n", p);%p prints the raw address value stored in p (i.e. where x lives in memory) — not x's value.
printf("%d\n", *p);*p dereferences: "go to the address in p and read the int there", which is x's current value, 10.
*p = 20;Writes through the pointer: walks to the address p holds and overwrites the value sitting there. Since that address is x's address, this changes x itself to 20.
printf("%d\n", x);Prints x directly by name — confirms it really is 20 now, even though the previous line never wrote to x by name.

int *p = &x; declares p as a pointer-to-int and immediately stores x's address inside it. *p from here on means "the int living at the address stored in p" — i.e. x itself, just accessed indirectly. So *p = 20; does not change p; it walks to the address p holds and overwrites the value there, which is x. That's why the final printf("%d", x) prints 20, even though the line never mentioned x by name.

Arrays and pointers are the same address underneath
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int data[5] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};
    int *ptr = data;      // array name = address of data[0]
    int i;

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        printf("Element %d: %d\n", i, *(ptr + i));
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int data[5] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};An ordinary 5-element array.
int *ptr = data;data used by itself decays to the address of data[0] — so this is exactly equivalent to writing &data[0].
for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {Visits every valid index once.
printf("Element %d: %d\n", i, *(ptr + i));ptr + i moves i whole elements forward from where ptr points (automatically scaled by sizeof(int) bytes, not literally i bytes); *(...) then reads the value sitting there — identical to data[i].

An array name, used by itself, decays into the address of its first element — so int *ptr = data; is exactly equivalent to int *ptr = &data[0];. *(ptr + i) means "move i whole ints forward from where ptr points, then dereference" — this is pointer arithmetic: ptr + i automatically advances by i * sizeof(int) bytes, not literally i bytes, because the compiler knows ptr's type. *(ptr + i) and ptr[i] and arr[i] all mean exactly the same thing.

Professor's explanation

Pointer arithmetic cheat-sheet: ptr + n moves n elements forward; ptr - n moves n elements backward; ptr++ moves to the next element; ptr-- moves to the previous one. All of these move by n * sizeof(type) bytes, never n raw bytes.

int x = 5; int *p = &x; *p = *p + 1; After this, what is x?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

*p is not a copy of x — it *is* x's memory, accessed via the address stored in p. Any read or write through *p directly reads/writes x.

Given int arr[5] = {10,20,30,40,50}; int *p = arr;, what does *(p + 2) give?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

p points at arr[0]; p + 2 moves 2 elements forward to arr[2], and * dereferences it to get the value 30.

9. Dynamic memory allocation

Final exam topic

Final exam: combined with arrays for 13–14 marks.

A normal array like int arr[5]; has its size fixed the moment you compile the program — you cannot make it bigger later. Dynamic memory allocation requests memory while the program is running, sized exactly to what the user asked for, from a separate region called the heap. Because malloc hands back an address, the result must always be stored in a pointer — DMA and pointers are inseparable.

The four functions you need (header: <stdlib.h>):

FunctionDoesReturns
malloc(size)allocates size bytes, uninitialisedpointer to the block, or NULL on failure
calloc(n, size)allocates n blocks, zero-initialisedpointer, or NULL
realloc(ptr, newsize)resizes a previous block, keeping existing datapointer (may be a new address!), or NULL
free(ptr)releases the block back to the systemnothing

The standard pattern: always cast the result to the right pointer type, always request n * sizeof(type) bytes (never a hard-coded number), and always check for NULL before using the pointer.

Dynamically allocate n ints, fill them, sum them, free the memory
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("How many numbers? ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *ptr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (ptr == NULL) {
        printf("Allocation failed\n");
        return 1;
    }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        scanf("%d", &ptr[i]);
    }

    int sum = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        sum = sum + ptr[i];
    }
    printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);

    free(ptr);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *ptr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));Requests enough heap memory for n ints back-to-back. sizeof(int) is the byte-size of one int on this machine; (int *) casts the generic pointer malloc returns into an int-pointer.
if (ptr == NULL) { ... return 1; }Checks whether the allocation actually succeeded. If the system had no memory available, malloc returns NULL instead of crashing outright — this check must come before ptr is used for anything.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) { scanf("%d", &ptr[i]); }Fills the allocated block one element at a time, exactly like filling an ordinary array — ptr[i] behaves identically to arr[i].
sum = sum + ptr[i];The same accumulator pattern as any array-summing loop, just reading from ptr[i] instead of arr[i].
free(ptr);Releases the heap block back to the system once it's no longer needed. Skipping this is a memory leak, and typically costs a mark in an exam answer.

(int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int)) — sizeof(int) is the number of bytes one int takes on this machine (normally 4); multiplying by n requests exactly enough room for n integers back-to-back, exactly like a real array. The (int *) in front is a cast: malloc technically returns a generic pointer, and casting tells the compiler "treat this block as an array of ints".

if (ptr == NULL) — if the system had no memory left, malloc returns NULL instead of crashing; a program that skips this check and uses ptr anyway will crash later with no clear cause, so this check is graded as a required line, not decoration.

From here on, ptr[i] behaves exactly like arr[i] would — that's the whole point of DMA: same indexing syntax, but the size was decided at run time.

free(ptr); — releases the heap memory. Forgetting this in a real program causes a memory leak; in an exam answer, forgetting it usually costs you a mark even if the numeric output is correct.

Growing an array at run time with realloc()
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, extra, i;
    printf("How many numbers initially? ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *ptr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (ptr == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) scanf("%d", &ptr[i]);

    printf("Add more numbers? How many: ");
    scanf("%d", &extra);

    ptr = (int *)realloc(ptr, (n + extra) * sizeof(int));
    if (ptr == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = n; i < n + extra; i++) scanf("%d", &ptr[i]);

    int total = n + extra;
    printf("All values: ");
    for (i = 0; i < total; i++) printf("%d ", ptr[i]);

    free(ptr);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
ptr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));Allocates the initial block, exactly as in the previous example.
printf("Add more numbers? How many: "); scanf("%d", &extra);Reads how many additional elements the user wants to add.
ptr = (int *)realloc(ptr, (n + extra) * sizeof(int));Resizes the existing block to hold n + extra ints total, keeping the first n values already stored. The result is reassigned back into ptr itself — required, since realloc may have moved the data to a new address.
if (ptr == NULL) { ... }Same safety check as malloc — realloc can also fail and return NULL.
for (i = n; i < n + extra; i++) scanf("%d", &ptr[i]);Fills only the newly added slots: the loop starts at n (the first previously-unused index), not 0, so the original n values are left untouched.

We first allocate room for n ints and fill them exactly as before. When the user wants extra more, realloc(ptr, (n+extra)*sizeof(int)) asks the system to resize the existing block: it keeps all the values already stored, and either extends the block in place or — if there isn't room right there — copies everything to a brand-new, bigger block and returns that address instead. This is precisely why the result must always be re-assigned: ptr = realloc(ptr, ...), never used as a separate variable, otherwise you could be left holding the old, now-invalid address.

The second input loop deliberately starts at i = n (not 0) and runs to n + extra, so it only fills the newly added slots without overwriting the values already stored in the first n slots.

Why is the result of malloc almost always checked against NULL before use?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

If the system cannot supply the requested memory, malloc signals this by returning NULL instead of a real address. Dereferencing a NULL pointer is undefined behaviour — typically an immediate crash.

After ptr = realloc(ptr, newsize);, why must you always reassign the result back into ptr?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

If the existing block can't simply be extended in place, realloc copies the data elsewhere and returns the new address. Not capturing that new address leaves you pointing at memory that may no longer be valid.

10. Structures

Final exam topic

Final exam: the single biggest question, 14 marks (1 set question, 2 parts or single part).

An array groups many values of the same type. A structure groups values of different types that describe one real-world "thing" — a student, a course, a reading — under a single name, so you can pass the whole bundle around as one unit instead of juggling five separate parallel arrays.

Always use the typedef form (the slides call this the recommended syntax, and it's what you should default to in the exam):

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float cgpa;
} Student;          // "Student" is now usable exactly like int/float
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
typedef struct {Begins a struct blueprint, and typedef means the name given at the very end will become a usable type name on its own.
int id; char name[50]; float cgpa;The three members — each an ordinary variable declaration, just written inside the struct's braces instead of inside a function.
} Student;Closes the blueprint and names the new type Student. No object exists yet — this line alone doesn't reserve any memory.

This defines a brand-new type called Student. It doesn't create any variable yet — it's a blueprint. From this point on, Student can be used anywhere a type name is expected, e.g. Student s1;, Student list[10];, or as a function parameter.

Creating and printing one struct object
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float cgpa;
} Student;

int main() {
    Student s1 = {101, "Alice", 3.85};

    printf("ID: %d\n", s1.id);
    printf("Name: %s\n", s1.name);
    printf("CGPA: %.2f\n", s1.cgpa);

    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
Student s1 = {101, "Alice", 3.85};Creates one actual Student object named s1, filling its members in declaration order: id gets 101, name gets "Alice", cgpa gets 3.85.
printf("ID: %d\n", s1.id);s1.id (dot operator) reads the id member of the object s1; printed with %d since it's an int.
printf("Name: %s\n", s1.name);s1.name is a char[] member, so it's printed with %s like any string.
printf("CGPA: %.2f\n", s1.cgpa);s1.cgpa is a float member, printed with %.2f to show 2 decimal places.

Student s1 = {101, "Alice", 3.85}; creates one object of type Student and fills its three members in declaration order: id=101, name="Alice", cgpa=3.85.

Dot operator: s1.id reads/writes the id member of the object s1. Use the dot whenever you have the object itself (not a pointer to it).

Note the format specifiers still match the member's own type: %d for the int member, %s for the char[] member, and %.2f for the float member.

Accessing the same struct through a pointer
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float cgpa;
} Student;

int main() {
    Student s1 = {101, "Alice", 3.85};
    Student *ptr = &s1;

    printf("ID: %d\n", ptr->id);
    printf("Name: %s\n", ptr->name);
    printf("CGPA: %.2f\n", ptr->cgpa);

    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
Student *ptr = &s1;Declares ptr as a pointer to a Student struct, and immediately points it at s1.
ptr->idThe arrow operator: "go to the struct ptr points at, then read its id member" — used because we now hold a pointer to the struct, not the struct itself.
ptr->name, ptr->cgpaSame arrow-access pattern for the other two members — every . from the previous example becomes -> here.

Student *ptr = &s1; stores the address of the whole struct object. When you have a pointer to a struct instead of the object itself, you must use the arrow operator -> instead of the dot: ptr->id means "go to the struct that ptr points to, then read its id member" — it is shorthand for the more clumsy (*ptr).id. If you ever need scanf on a member through a pointer, you still need &: scanf("%d", &ptr->id);.

You haveUseExample
the object itself.s1.id
a pointer to the object->ptr->id
An array of structures — the shape almost every exam question uses
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float cgpa;
} Student;

int main() {
    Student students[3] = {
        {101, "Alice", 3.85},
        {102, "Bob", 3.60},
        {103, "Charlie", 3.92}
    };
    int i;

    for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
        printf("Student %d: %s (%.2f)\n",
               students[i].id, students[i].name, students[i].cgpa);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
Student students[3] = { {...}, {...}, {...} };Declares an array of 3 Student objects; each inner {...} initialises one complete object, in order: students[0], students[1], students[2].
for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) {Visits every record in the array once.
students[i].id, students[i].name, students[i].cgpaCombines array indexing (students[i] — which record) with dot-access (.id/.name/.cgpa — which member of that record). Read right to left: "the id member of students[i]".

Student students[3] = { {...}, {...}, {...} }; declares 3 Student objects back to back, each initialised with its own set of braces inside the outer braces. This is the workhorse pattern for the 14-mark structure question: N records of related data, stored as one array, processed with one loop.

Inside the loop, students[i].id combines array indexing (which record?) with the dot operator (which member of that record?) — read it right to left: "the id member of students[i]".

Typical exam task shape: take N records as input, then filter/print only the ones matching some condition (e.g. "credits > 35 AND some digit of the ID is even") — this always reduces to: one loop to fill the array of structs, one loop (with an if inside) to check each record and print the matches.

You have Student s; (not a pointer). How do you access its cgpa member?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

The dot operator is used when you hold the struct object itself. The arrow -> is only for when you hold a pointer to the struct.

Student *p = &s; Which line correctly reads an integer into p's id member using scanf?

✓ Correct.

✗ Not quite — the correct answer is highlighted above.

scanf always needs an address to write into. p->id gives you the *value*, so you still need & in front of the whole expression: &p->id (the arrow binds tighter than the address-of).

Contents
  • Digit extraction toolkit
  • Structures
  • Dynamic Memory
  • Pointers & tracing
  • Strings (no built-ins)

Exam-Ready Theory

This is not the full course syllabus — it is only the conventions, syntax and reusable "recipes" that the official practice sheet (Tab 4, now including its new Part D pointer-tracing problems) and therefore the final exam actually draw on. Every rule below is here because at least one problem in Tab 4 needs it. Read this once, then go solve problems in Tab 4 — come back here only to look something up.

1. Digit extraction — the toolkit behind almost every Structures question

Every Part A question hides 2–4 fields inside one integer (a registration number, a batch number, an employee code, an account number). There are exactly three extraction moves you need — everything else is just combining them.

Move 1 — a field flush against the right end (the last k digits)

field = number % (10k)

Example: last digit → n % 10. Last 3 digits → n % 1000.

Move 2 — a field flush against the left end (the first k digits)

field = number / (10(total digits − k))

Example: an 8-digit number's first 2 digits → divide by 106 (1,000,000), because 6 digits remain to the right that need to be pushed off.

Move 3 — a field in the middle (not touching either end)

field = (number / 10(digits to its right)) % 10(its own width)

First divide to drop whatever sits to the right of the field, then % by the field's own width to drop whatever is left on the left side too. Example (A1): department code is 3 digits, with 3 digits still to its right, out of an 8-digit code → (regNum / 1000) % 1000.

LayoutFieldFormula
YY DDD SSS (8 digits)YY (year, left)n / 1000000
YY DDD SSSDDD (middle)(n / 1000) % 1000
YY DDD SSSSSS (right)n % 1000
YY MM SS (6 digits)YY (left)n / 10000
YY MM SSMM (middle)(n / 100) % 100
YY GG (last digit is grade)GG (right, 1 digit)n % 10

Move 4 — you need every digit (checksums / validation, A4-style)

When a rule needs to look at all the digits (sum them, weight them, alternate signs), no fixed division/modulo works — peel digits one at a time from the right with a while loop:

while (n > 0) { digit = n % 10; /* use digit */ n = n / 10; }

Handy trick: if you need "sum of all but the last digit", it's often easier to sum all digits (one clean loop) and then subtract the last digit back out, rather than writing a loop that stops one digit early.

Combining two dates into one comparable number (A2-style)

totalMonths = year * 12 + month

Converts a (year, month) pair into a single linear count. Subtracting two such totals gives the age/difference directly — no manual handling of "borrowing" across a year boundary. The same idea generalises to hours+minutes (hour*60+minute) or any two-part unit.

2. Structures — syntax and the one loop-shape you always reuse

Declaring with typedef (always use this form on the exam)

typedef struct { char name[30]; int code; float value; } TypeName;

After this, TypeName works like a built-in type. Never bother with the anonymous or non-typedef forms — typedef is what every model solution on this sheet uses.

Array of structs, initialised inline

TypeName arr[5] = { {"Name1", 22101045, 3.71}, {"Name2", 23102187, 3.25} };

Accessing members

You haveSyntax
a plain struct variable ss.member
a pointer to a struct pp->member (equivalent to (*p).member)
scanf into a memberscanf("%d", &s.id); — still needs &

The universal loop shape for every Part A question

for (i = 0; i < N; i++) { /* 1. extract any packed digit-fields from arr[i].code */ /* 2. test the filter condition(s) */ if (condition) { printf(...); /* 3. print/act on qualifying rows */ tally++; /* 4. keep any running counts */ } } printf("count summary...");

Every A1–A4 solution is this exact shape. What changes between questions is only what gets extracted and what the condition is.

Copying a string manually (no strcpy)

int k = 0; while (src[k] != '\0') { dst[k] = src[k]; k++; } dst[k] = '\0';

Needed whenever you must "remember the best name seen so far" (A3-style) — you cannot write dst = src; for whole char arrays in C.

3. Dynamic Memory — malloc / calloc / realloc / free

FunctionSyntaxNotes
mallocT *p = (T*)malloc(n * sizeof(T));uninitialised (garbage) memory
callocT *p = (T*)calloc(n, sizeof(T));count and size are separate args; memory is zeroed
reallocp = (T*)realloc(p, newN * sizeof(T));keeps existing data (up to the smaller size); may move the block
freefree(p);always call once per malloc/calloc/realloc chain, at the end

Always check if (p == NULL) right after allocating, on the exam this is an easy, expected line even if the professor doesn't always deduct for skipping it.

Recipe A — you already know exactly how many items (B1, B2, B4)

scanf("%d", &n); T *arr = (T*)malloc(n * sizeof(T)); for (i = 0; i < n; i++) scanf(..., &arr[i]);

If more items get added later and you're told exactly how many (M), resize once: arr = realloc(arr, (n+m) * sizeof(T)); then fill only the new region, for (i = n; i < n+m; i++).

Recipe B — you don't know the count in advance, a sentinel stops input (B3)

int cap = 5, count = 0; int *arr = (int*)malloc(cap * sizeof(int)); int value; scanf("%d", &value); while (value != -1) { if (count == cap) { /* check BEFORE storing */ cap += 5; arr = (int*)realloc(arr, cap * sizeof(int)); printf("[capacity increased to %d]\n", cap); } arr[count] = value; count++; scanf("%d", &value); /* read NEXT value LAST */ }

The one detail examiners love to test: read one value before the loop, check/grow capacity before storing, and read the next value at the very end of the loop body. Get this ordering wrong and either the sentinel gets stored, or the array overflows by one.

Slab / tiered-rate calculations (B1 fare, B2 tax)

Build the branches from the lowest bracket up, each one adding only the charge for the portion inside that bracket, on top of the full charge of every bracket below it:

if (x <= b1) fare = rate0; else if (x <= b2) fare = full0 + rate1*(x-b1); else fare = full0 + full1 + rate2*(x-b2);

With ≥4 brackets, prefer early returns (see B2) — it avoids deeply nested elses and repeated subtraction terms. Always cap a middle bracket's own width before charging it (a slab that isn't the last/open one can never contribute more than its own size).

4. Pointers — syntax, and the scoping rules tracing questions test

ActionSyntaxMeaning
declareint *p;p will hold the address of an int
assignp = &x;p now points to x (never p = x; a value)
dereference (read/write)*p"the value at the address p holds" — acts exactly like x itself
array ↔ pointerint *p = arr;array name is already the address of element 0
arithmetic*(p+i) or p[i]moves i whole elements forward, not i bytes

Pass-by-value vs pass-by-pointer (the single most-tested contrast)

void byValue(int a) { a = a + 5; } /* copy — caller's variable UNCHANGED */ void byPointer(int *b){ *b = *b + 5; } /* real address — caller's variable CHANGES */

Call as byValue(x) vs byPointer(&x). This is exactly Assignment 2 Q4's trap, and its own standalone tracing programs (Tab 3).

The scoping rule that decides every tracing question

  • A global variable is declared outside every function.
  • A local variable (or a function parameter) with the same name as a global always wins inside that function — the global becomes invisible there, right down to the end of that function's braces.
  • Two different functions with a local/parameter of the same name (e.g. two functions both using x) are completely unrelated variables, each with its own storage, even though they print the same way.
  • If a function does not declare its own local/parameter with that name, any plain use of that name inside it refers to the global.

How to trace safely, step by step

  1. List every variable name that appears anywhere, and next to each occurrence write which variable it actually is (global? local to main? a parameter of some other function?). Do this before tracing values.
  2. Draw a table: one row per statement executed, one column per real variable (using a distinct label per variable if the same name is reused, e.g. x_main, x_modify).
  3. For any statement with a pointer dereference, track "what p currently points to" as its own column — *p's meaning can change mid-program if p is reassigned.
  4. C evaluates the right-hand side first, entirely with old values, then assigns. On a line like c = *p + b; where p points to c itself, the old value of c is used, not a value it hasn't been assigned yet.
  5. A function call happens exactly at the point it's written — step into it, finish tracing its body completely (with its own fresh local/parameter copies), note its printf output in-place, then step back out before continuing the caller's next line.

5. Strings — every C1–C9 primitive, with zero built-in functions

No built-in string functions are allowed anywhere in Part C (no strlen, strcpy, strcat, strcmp, strrev, etc). Every one of the following hand-rolled primitives replaces a library function, and nearly every C-question on the exam is one or two of these combined.

Primitive 1 — find the length

int len = 0; while (str[len] != '\0') len++;

Primitive 2 — walk backward (reverse, without a second array)

for (i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) printf("%c", str[i]);

Last real character is at len-1, not len (that's the terminator).

Primitive 3 — classify a character (letter / vowel / digit / space)

if ((c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')) { /* letter */ } else if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') { /* digit */ } else if (c == ' ') { /* space */ } else { /* other */ }

Use one if / else if ladder so every character lands in exactly one bucket. Nest the vowel-check inside the letter branch.

Primitive 4 — the word-start test (memorise this exactly)

if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i-1] == ' ')) { /* this IS the first letter of a word */ }

Handles leading spaces, trailing spaces and multiple spaces between words all at once — the i==0 check exists purely so you never read str[-1].

Primitive 5 — toggle / force letter case (the ±32 trick)

if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') c = c - 32; /* lower -> UPPER */ if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32; /* UPPER -> lower */

Write these as mutually exclusive branches (if / else if) when toggling both directions in the same pass; digits/spaces simply match neither branch and pass through untouched — don't bother writing an explicit "leave alone" else.

Primitive 6 — filter-then-check (build a cleaned copy first)

char cleaned[50]; int k = 0; for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) { char c = str[i]; if (c == ' ') continue; /* skip what should be ignored */ if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32; /* normalise */ cleaned[k++] = c; } cleaned[k] = '\0';

Use this whenever a question says "ignoring spaces/case" and the cleaned result needs to be reused afterward (e.g. a palindrome check). If the filtered result is only ever printed once and never reused, skip the second buffer and just continue; past what should be skipped (Primitive 7).

Primitive 7 — print selectively without a second buffer

for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) { if (isVowel(str[i])) continue; /* skip printing this one */ printf("%c", str[i]); }

Primitive 8 — two-pointer palindrome check

int left = 0, right = k - 1, isPalin = 1; while (left < right) { if (cleaned[left] != cleaned[right]) { isPalin = 0; break; } left++; right--; }

Always run this on an already-cleaned copy (Primitive 6), never on the raw string with spaces/case still in it.

Reading a single character safely

scanf(" %c", &ch); /* the LEADING SPACE skips any leftover newline */

Forgetting the leading space is the single most common silent bug when a %c read follows any earlier scanf call.

Averages: always cast before dividing

float avg = (float)wordCount / sentenceCount;
