L3 — Basics of C

L3 — Q1

Write a program to convert feet to centimeters (1 foot = 30.48 cm).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float feet, cm;
    printf("Enter length in feet: ");
    scanf("%f", &feet);
    cm = feet * 30.48;
    printf("%.2f feet = %.2f cm\n", feet, cm);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float feet, cm;Declares two float variables for the input and result.
scanf("%f", &feet);Reads a decimal value into feet; & is required since feet is an ordinary variable.
cm = feet * 30.48;Multiplies by the fixed conversion factor.
printf("%.2f feet = %.2f cm\n", feet, cm);Prints both values, each rounded to 2 decimal places for display.

A straight unit-conversion: multiply the input by the fixed conversion factor 30.48. %.2f rounds the printed output to 2 decimal places without changing the value stored in memory.

L3 — Q2

Write a program to calculate the sum of two numbers entered by the user.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float a, b, sum;
    printf("Enter two numbers: ");
    scanf("%f %f", &a, &b);
    sum = a + b;
    printf("Sum = %.2f\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
scanf("%f %f", &a, &b);One call reads both numbers; the space between the two %fs tells scanf to skip any whitespace between the two typed values.
sum = a + b;Ordinary addition, stored in a third variable.

scanf("%f %f", &a, &b) reads two values separated by whitespace (space, tab or newline all work) in a single call — you don't need two separate scanf lines.

L3 — Q3

Write a program to find the average of three numbers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float a, b, c, avg;
    printf("Enter three numbers: ");
    scanf("%f %f %f", &a, &b, &c);
    avg = (a + b + c) / 3;
    printf("Average = %.2f\n", avg);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
avg = (a + b + c) / 3;Parentheses force all three additions to happen before the division; without them, precedence rules would divide only c by 3 first, giving the wrong total.

The parentheses in (a + b + c) / 3 matter: without them, / would run before the additions due to operator precedence and give the wrong result.

L3 — Q4

Write a program to convert temperature from Celsius to Fahrenheit.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float celsius, fahrenheit;
    printf("Enter temperature in Celsius: ");
    scanf("%f", &celsius);
    fahrenheit = (celsius * 9 / 5) + 32;
    printf("%.2f C = %.2f F\n", celsius, fahrenheit);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
fahrenheit = (celsius * 9 / 5) + 32;Evaluated left to right within the parentheses: celsius * 9 first (a float result, since celsius is a float), then / 5 — by this point it's already floating-point division, so no precision is lost.

9 / 5 is written as celsius * 9 / 5 rather than celsius * (9/5). If 9/5 were evaluated on its own using int division it would truncate to 1 and silently give a wrong answer — multiplying celsius (a float) first forces the whole expression into floating-point arithmetic.

L3 — Q5

Write a program to calculate the area of a circle (Area = π × r²).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float r, area;
    float pi = 3.14159;
    printf("Enter radius: ");
    scanf("%f", &r);
    area = pi * r * r;
    printf("Area = %.2f\n", area);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float pi = 3.14159;A local constant, declared with an initial value.
area = pi * r * r;Computes πr² directly via two multiplications instead of calling a power function.

r * r is used instead of a power function since built-in library functions like pow() aren't used in this course — for a fixed small power like squaring, direct multiplication is simplest.

L4 — Control Statements

L4 — Q1

Write a program to check if a number is divisible (by 5), (by 5 or 7), (by 5 and 7 both).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n;
    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    if (n % 5 == 0) printf("Divisible by 5\n");
    else printf("Not divisible by 5\n");

    if (n % 5 == 0 || n % 7 == 0) printf("Divisible by 5 or 7\n");
    else printf("Not divisible by 5 or 7\n");

    if (n % 5 == 0 && n % 7 == 0) printf("Divisible by 5 and 7\n");
    else printf("Not divisible by both\n");

    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (n % 5 == 0) ... else ...First, independent question: is n divisible by 5 alone?
if (n % 5 == 0 || n % 7 == 0) ...Second, independent question: uses || since "5 or 7" means at least one must hold.
if (n % 5 == 0 && n % 7 == 0) ...Third, independent question: uses && since "5 and 7" means both must hold at once.

Three independent checks, so three separate if...else blocks are used (not else if, since all three questions need answering regardless of the others). || means "at least one is true"; && means "both must be true".

L4 — Q2

Write a program to find the largest of two numbers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a, b;
    printf("Enter two numbers: ");
    scanf("%d %d", &a, &b);
    if (a > b) printf("Largest = %d\n", a);
    else printf("Largest = %d\n", b);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (a > b) printf(...a...); else printf(...b...);A single comparison covers both possibilities: strictly-greater goes to the if branch, everything else (including equality) goes to else.

Only one comparison is needed for two numbers: if a isn't strictly bigger than b, then b must be the larger one (or they're equal, in which case either answer is correct).

L4 — Q3

Write a program to check if a year is a leap year (multiple of 400, or multiple of 4 and not a multiple of 100).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int year;
    printf("Enter a year: ");
    scanf("%d", &year);

    if (year % 400 == 0 || (year % 4 == 0 && year % 100 != 0)) {
        printf("%d is a leap year\n", year);
    } else {
        printf("%d is not a leap year\n", year);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
year % 400 == 0First half of the OR: true for years like 2000, 2400.
year % 4 == 0 && year % 100 != 0Second half: divisible by 4 but not by 100 (excludes century years like 1900, which are NOT leap years unless also divisible by 400).

The condition is copied straight from the rule: "multiple of 400" OR "multiple of 4 AND not a multiple of 100". The parentheses around the AND part matter, because && binds tighter than || anyway in C — but writing them explicitly makes the logic unambiguous to read.

L4 — Q4

Write a program to display the month name using switch (1–12).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int month;
    printf("Enter month number (1-12): ");
    scanf("%d", &month);

    switch (month) {
        case 1: printf("January\n"); break;
        case 2: printf("February\n"); break;
        case 3: printf("March\n"); break;
        case 4: printf("April\n"); break;
        case 5: printf("May\n"); break;
        case 6: printf("June\n"); break;
        case 7: printf("July\n"); break;
        case 8: printf("August\n"); break;
        case 9: printf("September\n"); break;
        case 10: printf("October\n"); break;
        case 11: printf("November\n"); break;
        case 12: printf("December\n"); break;
        default: printf("Invalid month\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
switch (month) {Compares month against each case value in turn.
case 1: printf("January\n"); break;If month == 1, prints and then break exits the switch immediately.
default: printf("Invalid month\n");Runs only if month matched none of the 12 cases.

Each case ends with break; so execution exits the switch immediately after printing the matching month — without it, every case below the match would also run ("fall-through").

L4 — Q5

Write a program to calculate an electricity bill based on units consumed.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int units;
    float bill;
    printf("Enter units consumed: ");
    scanf("%d", &units);

    if (units <= 100) {
        bill = units * 5.0;
    } else if (units <= 300) {
        bill = 100 * 5.0 + (units - 100) * 7.5;
    } else {
        bill = 100 * 5.0 + 200 * 7.5 + (units - 300) * 10.0;
    }

    printf("Bill = %.2f Taka\n", bill);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (units <= 100) { bill = units * 5.0; }Simplest case: all units are in the first tier.
else if (units <= 300) { bill = 100*5.0 + (units-100)*7.5; }Charges the first 100 units at the tier-1 rate, then only the remaining units (units - 100) at the tier-2 rate.
else { bill = 100*5.0 + 200*7.5 + (units-300)*10.0; }Charges the first 100 at tier 1, the next 200 at tier 2 (both now full brackets), and only whatever's left beyond 300 at tier 3.

A tiered/slab bill: the first 100 units are cheap, the next 200 cost more per unit, and anything beyond that costs the most. Each bracket only charges the extra units at that bracket's rate, adding on top of the full charge already computed for the brackets below it — you must add the lower brackets' totals in, not just charge the whole amount at the top rate.

L4 — Q6

Write a program to create a simple calculator using switch (+, -, *, /). Use char for the operation.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float a, b, result;
    char op;
    printf("Enter first number, operator, second number: ");
    scanf("%f %c %f", &a, &op, &b);

    switch (op) {
        case '+': result = a + b; break;
        case '-': result = a - b; break;
        case '*': result = a * b; break;
        case '/':
            if (b != 0) result = a / b;
            else { printf("Error: division by zero\n"); return 1; }
            break;
        default:
            printf("Invalid operator\n");
            return 1;
    }
    printf("Result = %.2f\n", result);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
scanf("%f %c %f", &a, &op, &b);Reads a float, then a single character (the operator symbol), then another float — all three on one input line.
switch (op) { case '+': ... }Character constants like '+' work fine as switch cases, matched against op's value.
case '/': if (b != 0) ... else { ... return 1; } break;Division gets an extra guard: only divides if b isn't zero, otherwise reports an error and exits early.

switch works perfectly with char as well as int (just not with float or strings). Division needs an extra safety check for b != 0 before dividing, since dividing by zero is undefined for integers and gives inf/nan for floats.

L4 — Q7

Write a program to check if a character is a vowel or consonant (using ASCII).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char ch;
    printf("Enter a character: ");
    scanf("%c", &ch);

    if (ch == 'a' || ch == 'e' || ch == 'i' || ch == 'o' || ch == 'u' ||
        ch == 'A' || ch == 'E' || ch == 'I' || ch == 'O' || ch == 'U') {
        printf("%c is a vowel\n", ch);
    } else if ((ch >= 'a' && ch <= 'z') || (ch >= 'A' && ch <= 'Z')) {
        printf("%c is a consonant\n", ch);
    } else {
        printf("%c is not a letter\n", ch);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (ch == 'a' || ... || ch == 'U') {Checks all 10 vowel possibilities (5 lowercase, 5 uppercase) with one long OR-chain.
else if ((ch >= 'a' && ch <= 'z') || (ch >= 'A' && ch <= 'Z')) {Only reached if it wasn't a vowel; checks whether it's any letter at all using the ASCII-range trick — if so, it must be a consonant.
else { printf("%c is not a letter\n", ch); }Anything that's neither a vowel nor a letter-range match (digits, punctuation, etc.).

Characters in C are really small integers under the hood (their ASCII codes), so ch >= 'a' && ch <= 'z' is a valid and common way to test "is this a lowercase letter" — it relies on lowercase letters being stored as a contiguous block of ASCII codes.

L4 — Q8

Write a program to display menu options and perform actions using switch (like a mobile-banking *121# menu).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int choice;
    printf("1. Check Balance\n2. Send Money\n3. Buy Airtime\n4. Exit\n");
    printf("Enter choice: ");
    scanf("%d", &choice);

    switch (choice) {
        case 1: printf("Your balance is Tk 500.00\n"); break;
        case 2: printf("Enter amount and recipient to send money.\n"); break;
        case 3: printf("Enter amount to buy airtime.\n"); break;
        case 4: printf("Goodbye!\n"); break;
        default: printf("Invalid option\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
switch (choice) { case 1: ...; break; ... default: ...; }Identical shape to the month-name switch: one case per menu option, each ending in break, with default catching invalid input.

This is the same switch pattern as Q4, just with menu text instead of month names — recognising that most "menu" problems are really just switch in a costume is the key exam skill here.

L5 — Loops

L5 — Q1

Print even numbers between 1 and N.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        if (i % 2 == 0) printf("%d ", i);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {Visits every integer from 1 to N inclusive.
if (i % 2 == 0) printf("%d ", i);Only prints when the remainder after dividing by 2 is 0, i.e. when i is even.

The loop visits every number from 1 to N; the if inside filters out the ones that aren't even. This "loop + filter if" shape is reused constantly.

L5 — Q2

Print all numbers between 1 and N divisible by 3 (or 5).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        if (i % 3 == 0 || i % 5 == 0) printf("%d ", i);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (i % 3 == 0 || i % 5 == 0) printf("%d ", i);Prints if either remainder is 0 — || only needs one side to be true.

Same shape as Q1, but the filter condition now uses ||: a number qualifies if it's divisible by 3, by 5, or both.

L5 — Q3

Sum of first N numbers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, sum = 0;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        sum = sum + i;
    }
    printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int n, i, sum = 0;sum starts at 0, the safe starting value for addition.
sum = sum + i;Adds the current loop value onto the running total, once per pass, for i = 1 through N.

sum is initialised to 0 before the loop (the identity value for addition). Each pass adds the current i onto the running total.

L5 — Q4

Product of first N numbers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i;
    long product = 1;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        product = product * i;
    }
    printf("Product = %ld\n", product);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
long product = 1;Seeded at 1 (the identity value for multiplication), not 0.
product = product * i;Multiplies the running product by the current loop value, once per pass.

The accumulator starts at 1, not 0 — multiplying by 0 would instantly zero out the whole product. This is structurally identical to a factorial (see L5-Q6 / L6-Q1).

L5 — Q5

Print the multiplication table for a given number.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    for (i = 1; i <= 10; i++) {
        printf("%d x %d = %d\n", n, i, n * i);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 1; i <= 10; i++) {Always exactly 10 passes, regardless of n.
printf("%d x %d = %d\n", n, i, n * i);Prints the fixed number, the current multiplier, and their product.

The loop always runs a fixed 10 times (i from 1 to 10); only the printed values change as i increases.

L5 — Q6

Factorial of N.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i;
    long fact = 1;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        fact = fact * i;
    }
    printf("%d! = %ld\n", n, fact);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
fact = fact * i;Same accumulator pattern as Q4's product — factorial is exactly the product of 1 through N.

Identical logic to Q4 (product of first N numbers) — factorial is the product of first N numbers.

L5 — Q7

Count numbers between 1 and 50 divisible by 3 and 5.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int i, count = 0;
    for (i = 1; i <= 50; i++) {
        if (i % 3 == 0 && i % 5 == 0) {
            count++;
        }
    }
    printf("Count = %d\n", count);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (i % 3 == 0 && i % 5 == 0) { count++; }Both remainders must be 0 at once (&&), unlike Q2's "3 or 5" which used ||.

"Divisible by 3 and 5" (both, together) means && — not || like Q2. count++ increments the tally each time a qualifying number is found.

L5 — Q8

Determine if a number is prime.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, isPrime = 1;
    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    if (n <= 1) {
        isPrime = 0;
    } else {
        for (i = 2; i < n; i++) {
            if (n % i == 0) {
                isPrime = 0;
                break;
            }
        }
    }

    if (isPrime) printf("%d is prime\n", n);
    else printf("%d is not prime\n", n);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int n, i, isPrime = 1;The flag starts true (1); it's only ever changed to false if a divisor is actually found.
if (n <= 1) { isPrime = 0; }Handles the special case: 0, 1 and negatives are not prime by definition.
for (i = 2; i < n; i++) { if (n % i == 0) { isPrime = 0; break; } }Tries every candidate divisor from 2 up to (not including) n; the moment one divides evenly, the flag flips to false and the loop exits immediately — no need to keep checking further divisors.

isPrime is a flag, assumed true (1) until proven otherwise. The loop tries every possible divisor from 2 up to (but not including) n; the moment any of them divides evenly, we know n isn't prime, so we set the flag to 0 and break immediately — no need to keep checking.

L6 — Functions

Not tested as its own final question — shown here because it's a listed slide practice set, and because writing small helper functions like these is exactly what your faculty encourages you to reach for inside bigger problems.

L6 — Q1 Optional tool — not its own final exam question

Write a function that returns the factorial of a number.

#include <stdio.h>

long factorial(int n) {
    long fact = 1;
    int i;
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) fact = fact * i;
    return fact;
}

int main() {
    int n;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    printf("%d! = %ld\n", n, factorial(n));
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
long factorial(int n) {long return type (factorials grow fast), one parameter n.
return fact;Sends the computed value back to the caller; the function ends here.
printf("%d! = %ld\n", n, factorial(n));The function call factorial(n) is used directly inside printf's argument list — its return value fills the %ld.

Exactly the same loop as L5-Q6, just wrapped inside a function so it can be reused. return fact; sends the computed value back to main, which uses it directly inside the printf call.

L6 — Q2 Optional tool

Write a function to convert Celsius to Fahrenheit.

#include <stdio.h>

float toFahrenheit(float c) {
    return (c * 9 / 5) + 32;
}

int main() {
    float celsius;
    printf("Enter Celsius: ");
    scanf("%f", &celsius);
    printf("Fahrenheit = %.2f\n", toFahrenheit(celsius));
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float toFahrenheit(float c) { return (c * 9 / 5) + 32; }The whole computation is returned directly, with no intermediate variable needed.

A one-line function body is fine — return can directly return the result of an expression without storing it in a variable first.

L6 — Q3 Optional tool

Write a void function that prints the sum of the first N numbers.

#include <stdio.h>

void printSum(int n) {
    int i, sum = 0;
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) sum = sum + i;
    printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);
}

int main() {
    int n;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    printSum(n);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
void printSum(int n) {void return type: this function does something (prints) but hands nothing back.
printSum(n);Called as a standalone statement — there's no value to capture, so it isn't assigned to anything.

void means the function performs an action (here, printing) but hands nothing back to the caller — there is no return value, and calling it is a statement on its own, not something you'd assign to a variable.

L6 — Q4 Optional tool

Write a function that checks whether a number is prime.

#include <stdio.h>

int isPrime(int n) {
    int i;
    if (n <= 1) return 0;
    for (i = 2; i < n; i++) {
        if (n % i == 0) return 0;
    }
    return 1;
}

int main() {
    int n;
    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    if (isPrime(n)) printf("Prime\n");
    else printf("Not prime\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (n <= 1) return 0;Immediately exits with "not prime" for 0, 1 and negatives.
if (n % i == 0) return 0;The instant a divisor is found, the function exits right there with 0 — acting exactly like break would, but for the whole function, not just the loop.
return 1;Only reached if the loop finished without ever returning early — meaning no divisor was found.

Instead of a flag variable, this version returns immediately the moment it finds a divisor — returning inside a loop exits the function on the spot, so no separate break is needed.

L6 — Q5 Optional tool

Write a function to compute power(a, b) without using pow().

#include <stdio.h>

long power(int a, int b) {
    long result = 1;
    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < b; i++) {
        result = result * a;
    }
    return result;
}

int main() {
    int a, b;
    printf("Enter base and exponent: ");
    scanf("%d %d", &a, &b);
    printf("%d^%d = %ld\n", a, b, power(a, b));
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
long result = 1;Seeded at 1, the multiplication identity.
for (i = 0; i < b; i++) { result = result * a; }Runs exactly b times, multiplying a in each time — that's the definition of "a to the power b" via repeated multiplication.

a raised to b just means "multiply a by itself b times", so the loop runs exactly b times, each time multiplying a into the running result (started at 1, same reasoning as the factorial accumulator).

L7 — Arrays

L7 — Q1

Write a program to read 10 integers and print them in reverse order.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[10], i;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    printf("Reversed: ");
    for (i = 9; i >= 0; i--) {
        printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);Fills the array forward, index 0 through 9.
for (i = 9; i >= 0; i--) { printf("%d ", arr[i]); }Reads the same array backward, from the last index down to the first — nothing is moved, only the read order changes.

The input loop fills the array forward (i from 0 to 9). The output loop simply walks backward (i from 9 down to 0) over the same array — no data is actually moved or swapped, we just read it in the opposite order.

L7 — Q2

Write a program to count the number of even and odd numbers in an array.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[10], i, evenCount = 0, oddCount = 0;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        if (arr[i] % 2 == 0) evenCount++;
        else oddCount++;
    }
    printf("Even: %d, Odd: %d\n", evenCount, oddCount);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int evenCount = 0, oddCount = 0;Two independent tallies, both starting at 0.
if (arr[i] % 2 == 0) evenCount++; else oddCount++;Every element increments exactly one counter — if/else guarantees never both, never neither.

Two counters, both starting at 0. The second loop visits every element exactly once and increments exactly one of the two counters per element, using if/else since a number can never be both.

L7 — Q3

Write a program to find the average of elements in an array.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[10], i, sum = 0;
    float avg;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) sum = sum + arr[i];
    avg = (float)sum / 10;
    printf("Average = %.2f\n", avg);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) sum = sum + arr[i];Standard accumulator loop, summing all 10 elements.
avg = (float)sum / 10;(float)sum converts sum to a float before the division happens, so the division itself is floating-point (keeps decimals) instead of integer (truncates).

(float)sum is a cast: without it, sum / 10 would be integer division (dropping the decimal part) even though the result is stored in a float. Casting sum to float first forces the whole division to happen in floating point.

L7 — Q4

Write a program to check if a given number exists in the array (linear search).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[10], i, key, found = 0;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    printf("Enter number to search: ");
    scanf("%d", &key);

    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        if (arr[i] == key) {
            found = 1;
            printf("Found at index %d\n", i);
            break;
        }
    }
    if (!found) printf("Not found\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (arr[i] == key) { found = 1; printf(...); break; }The moment a match is found: the flag is set, the position is printed immediately, and break exits the loop — no point checking the remaining elements.
if (!found) printf("Not found\n");Only runs if the loop finished without ever setting found to 1.

Linear search checks each element one by one until it finds a match (or runs out of elements). break stops early the moment a match is found, since there's no point checking further; found tracks whether that ever happened so the right message prints after the loop ends.

L7 — Q5

Write a program to copy one array into another array.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int source[10], dest[10], i;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &source[i]);

    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        dest[i] = source[i];
    }

    printf("Copied array: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) printf("%d ", dest[i]);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
dest[i] = source[i];Copies one element per pass; after 10 passes every slot in dest matches the corresponding slot in source.

Arrays cannot be copied with a single = in C (dest = source; is not allowed) — you must copy element by element with a loop, exactly like this.

L7 — Q6

Write a program to find the second largest number in an array.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[10], i, largest, secondLargest;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    if (arr[0] > arr[1]) { largest = arr[0]; secondLargest = arr[1]; }
    else { largest = arr[1]; secondLargest = arr[0]; }

    for (i = 2; i < 10; i++) {
        if (arr[i] > largest) {
            secondLargest = largest;
            largest = arr[i];
        } else if (arr[i] > secondLargest) {
            secondLargest = arr[i];
        }
    }
    printf("Largest = %d, Second Largest = %d\n", largest, secondLargest);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (arr[0] > arr[1]) {...} else {...}Seeds both trackers from the first two elements, in the correct order, before the loop begins.
for (i = 2; i < 10; i++) {Starts at index 2, since 0 and 1 are already used up by the seeding step.
if (arr[i] > largest) { secondLargest = largest; largest = arr[i]; }A new overall largest bumps the old largest down to second place first.
else if (arr[i] > secondLargest) { secondLargest = arr[i]; }Only checked when the element didn't beat largest; updates just the second slot if it beats that instead.

Seed largest and secondLargest correctly from the first two elements before the loop starts (not from index 0 alone, since we need two seeds). Then for every later element: if it beats the current largest, the old largest slides down into second place before the new value takes the top spot; otherwise, if it merely beats the current second-largest, it only replaces that.

L8P2 — Strings

L8P2 — Q1

Convert a string to uppercase.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100];
    int i;
    printf("Enter a string: ");
    scanf("%s", str);

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] >= 'a' && str[i] <= 'z') {
            str[i] = str[i] - 32;
        }
    }
    printf("Uppercase: %s\n", str);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string one character at a time until the null terminator.
if (str[i] >= 'a' && str[i] <= 'z') {Only enters the block for characters that are currently lowercase letters.
str[i] = str[i] - 32;Subtracting 32 from a lowercase letter's ASCII code gives exactly its uppercase equivalent.

ASCII lowercase letters are exactly 32 higher than their uppercase counterpart ('a' is 97, 'A' is 65). So subtracting 32 from any lowercase letter converts it to uppercase; the if makes sure we only touch characters that actually are lowercase letters.

L8P2 — Q2

Count how many times a given word appears in a sentence.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char sentence[200], word[50];
    int i, j, k, count = 0, match;

    printf("Enter a sentence: ");
    fgets(sentence, sizeof(sentence), stdin);
    printf("Enter word to count: ");
    scanf("%s", word);

    for (i = 0; sentence[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        match = 1;
        for (j = 0; word[j] != '\0'; j++) {
            if (sentence[i + j] != word[j]) { match = 0; break; }
        }
        /* also make sure it's a whole word: char before/after is space, start or end */
        if (match) {
            int before_ok = (i == 0 || sentence[i - 1] == ' ');
            int after_ok = (sentence[i + j] == ' ' || sentence[i + j] == '\0' || sentence[i + j] == '\n');
            if (before_ok && after_ok) count++;
        }
    }
    printf("Occurrences of \"%s\": %d\n", word, count);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; sentence[i] != '\0'; i++) { match = 1;Tries every possible starting position in the sentence; match is assumed true until proven otherwise for this position.
for (j = 0; word[j] != '\0'; j++) { if (sentence[i+j] != word[j]) { match=0; break; } }Compares word against the sentence starting at i, character by character; any mismatch sets the flag false and stops early.
int before_ok = (i == 0 || sentence[i-1] == ' ');Confirms the character just before the match is either the very start of the sentence or a space — i.e. this isn't the tail end of a longer word.
if (before_ok && after_ok) count++;Only counts the match if it's a genuine whole word, not a substring buried inside a longer one.

For every starting position i in the sentence, we try to match word character by character starting there (j). If every character matches, match stays 1. We additionally check the characters immediately before and after the match are a space/boundary, so "test" inside "testing" is not wrongly counted as a whole-word match.

L8P2 — Q3

Check whether one string is a substring of another.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char text[200], sub[100];
    int i, j, found = 0;

    printf("Enter main string: ");
    scanf("%s", text);
    printf("Enter substring to check: ");
    scanf("%s", sub);

    for (i = 0; text[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        j = 0;
        while (sub[j] != '\0' && text[i + j] == sub[j]) {
            j++;
        }
        if (sub[j] == '\0') { found = 1; break; }
    }

    if (found) printf("Substring found\n");
    else printf("Substring not found\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
while (sub[j] != '\0' && text[i + j] == sub[j]) { j++; }Advances j as long as we haven't reached the end of sub AND the characters still match; stops the instant either fails.
if (sub[j] == '\0') { found = 1; break; }If the walk stopped because we reached sub's terminator (not because of a mismatch), every character matched — the substring was found starting here.

For each starting index i in text, we try to walk through sub matching character by character. If we manage to walk all the way to sub's null terminator (sub[j] == '\0') without a mismatch, every character of sub matched — it was found starting at position i.

L8P2 — Q4

Extract a substring given a start index and length.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100], sub[100];
    int start, len, i;

    printf("Enter a string: ");
    scanf("%s", str);
    printf("Enter start index and length: ");
    scanf("%d %d", &start, &len);

    for (i = 0; i < len; i++) {
        sub[i] = str[start + i];
    }
    sub[len] = '\0';

    printf("Substring: %s\n", sub);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < len; i++) { sub[i] = str[start + i]; }Copies len characters, reading from str starting at start but writing into sub starting at 0.
sub[len] = '\0';Manually terminates the new string right after the last copied character — without this, sub has no defined end.

The loop copies exactly len characters starting from index start into a fresh array. sub[len] = '\0'; is essential — without manually placing the null terminator, sub wouldn't be a valid string and %s would print garbage past the intended text.

L8P2 — Q5

Count vowels and consonants in a string.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100];
    int i, vowels = 0, consonants = 0;
    printf("Enter a string: ");
    scanf("%s", str);

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char c = str[i];
        if (c == 'a' || c == 'e' || c == 'i' || c == 'o' || c == 'u' ||
            c == 'A' || c == 'E' || c == 'I' || c == 'O' || c == 'U') {
            vowels++;
        } else if ((c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')) {
            consonants++;
        }
    }
    printf("Vowels = %d, Consonants = %d\n", vowels, consonants);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
char c = str[i];Copies the current character for readability.
if (c == 'a' || ... ) { vowels++; }Same 10-way vowel check as L4-Q7.
else if ((c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')) { consonants++; }Only reached if it wasn't a vowel; counts it as a consonant only if it's still a letter — digits and punctuation fall through uncounted.

Same vowel-checking condition as L4-Q7, now run once per character while walking the string until '\0'. The else if only counts a consonant when the character is a letter but not a vowel — digits/punctuation count as neither.

L8P2 — Q6

Check whether a word is a palindrome.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100];
    int len = 0, i, isPalin = 1;
    printf("Enter a word: ");
    scanf("%s", str);

    while (str[len] != '\0') len++;

    for (i = 0; i < len / 2; i++) {
        if (str[i] != str[len - 1 - i]) {
            isPalin = 0;
            break;
        }
    }

    if (isPalin) printf("Palindrome\n");
    else printf("Not a palindrome\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
while (str[len] != '\0') len++;Standard length-finding loop.
for (i = 0; i < len / 2; i++) {Only needs to check half the string — each pass compares one symmetric pair.
if (str[i] != str[len - 1 - i]) { isPalin = 0; break; }Compares the character at i from the front against its mirror at len-1-i from the back; any disagreement disproves the palindrome immediately.

First find the length (loop to '\0', as always). Then compare characters from both ends moving inward: index i against index len-1-i. We only need to check half the string (i < len/2) because by the time we reach the middle, every pair has already been checked once from the other side.

L8P2 — Q7

Remove all spaces from a string.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[200], result[200];
    int i, j = 0;
    printf("Enter a string: ");
    fgets(str, sizeof(str), stdin);

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] != ' ' && str[i] != '\n') {
            result[j] = str[i];
            j++;
        }
    }
    result[j] = '\0';

    printf("Without spaces: %s\n", result);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (str[i] != ' ' && str[i] != '\n') { result[j] = str[i]; j++; }Only copies (and advances the write index j) for characters that aren't a space or newline; matching characters are simply skipped, so i keeps advancing while j doesn't.
result[j] = '\0';Terminates the new, shorter string right after the last character actually copied.

j is a separate "write index" into result, moving forward only when a non-space character is copied — i (reading) and j (writing) drift apart exactly by however many spaces have been skipped so far. Terminating with result[j] = '\0'; after the loop is what makes result a valid, correctly-shortened string.

L8P2 — Q8

Reverse the order of words in a sentence.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char sentence[200];
    char words[50][50];
    int wordCount = 0, i, j, k;

    printf("Enter a sentence: ");
    fgets(sentence, sizeof(sentence), stdin);

    /* split into words */
    i = 0; k = 0;
    while (sentence[i] != '\0') {
        if (sentence[i] == ' ' || sentence[i] == '\n') {
            words[wordCount][k] = '\0';
            if (k > 0) wordCount++;
            k = 0;
        } else {
            words[wordCount][k] = sentence[i];
            k++;
        }
        i++;
    }
    if (k > 0) { words[wordCount][k] = '\0'; wordCount++; }

    /* print in reverse order */
    for (i = wordCount - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        printf("%s ", words[i]);
    }
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
char words[50][50];A 2D array: up to 50 words, each up to 50 characters — think of it as an array of strings.
if (sentence[i] == ' ' || sentence[i] == '\n') { words[wordCount][k]='\0'; if(k>0) wordCount++; k=0; }Hitting a space/newline closes off the current word (terminates it), then moves on to the next row of words, resetting the within-word position k.
else { words[wordCount][k] = sentence[i]; k++; }Otherwise, copies the current character into the current word's current position.
for (i = wordCount - 1; i >= 0; i--) { printf("%s ", words[i]); }Once every word is stored as its own string, printing them from the last row back to the first reverses their order.

This builds on the substring/tokenising idea: walk through sentence once, and every time a space (or the newline from fgets) is hit, that marks the end of one word — close it off with '\0' and move to the next row of the words 2D array. Once every word is stored separately, printing them from wordCount-1 down to 0 reverses their order.

L9 — Pointers

L9 — Q1

Write a program to swap two numbers using pointers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a = 5, b = 10, temp;
    int *p1 = &a, *p2 = &b;

    temp = *p1;
    *p1 = *p2;
    *p2 = temp;

    printf("a = %d, b = %d\n", a, b);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *p1 = &a, *p2 = &b;Two pointers, holding the addresses of a and b respectively.
temp = *p1; *p1 = *p2; *p2 = temp;The standard 3-line swap, performed through the pointers: reading/writing *p1 reads/writes a directly, and likewise for *p2/b.

p1 and p2 hold the addresses of a and b. Swapping *p1 and *p2 (the values at those addresses) using the standard three-line swap directly swaps a and b themselves — there's no difference between "swap *p1 and *p2" and "swap a and b", because they're the same memory.

L9 — Q2

Find the largest element in an array using pointers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {12, 45, 3, 67, 21};
    int *ptr = arr;
    int max = *ptr;
    int i;

    for (i = 1; i < 5; i++) {
        if (*(ptr + i) > max) {
            max = *(ptr + i);
        }
    }
    printf("Largest = %d\n", max);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *ptr = arr;ptr now points at arr[0] (an array name decays to the address of its first element).
int max = *ptr;Seeds from the first element, read via dereference.
if (*(ptr + i) > max) { max = *(ptr + i); }*(ptr + i) is exactly arr[i], just written in pointer-arithmetic form.

Identical logic to the ordinary array max-finder, just written with pointer arithmetic: *(ptr + i) is exactly arr[i]. max = *ptr seeds from the first element (*(ptr+0)).

L9 — Q3

Reverse an array using pointer arithmetic.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5};
    int *start = arr;
    int *end = arr + 4;
    int temp;

    while (start < end) {
        temp = *start;
        *start = *end;
        *end = temp;
        start++;
        end--;
    }

    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *start = arr; int *end = arr + 4;Two pointers seeded at opposite ends of the array (index 0 and index 4, the last valid index for a 5-element array).
while (start < end) {Continues only while the two pointers haven't met or crossed each other.
temp = *start; *start = *end; *end = temp;Swaps the values the two pointers currently point at, using the standard 3-line swap.
start++; end--;Moves the pointers one element closer to each other for the next pass.

Two pointers walk toward each other from opposite ends: start begins at arr[0], end at arr[4]. Each loop pass swaps the values they currently point to, then start++ moves it one element forward and end-- moves the other one element backward. The loop stops once they meet or cross (start < end becomes false), which correctly handles both even- and odd-length arrays.

L9 — Q4 Uses pointers + functions together

Implement a function that takes a pointer and modifies the value it points to.

#include <stdio.h>

void doubleValue(int *p) {
    *p = *p * 2;
}

int main() {
    int x = 7;
    doubleValue(&x);
    printf("x = %d\n", x);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
void doubleValue(int *p) {The parameter is a pointer, not an ordinary int — it will receive an address, not a copy of a value.
*p = *p * 2;Writes through the pointer, doubling whatever value sits at the address p holds.
doubleValue(&x);Passes x's address, so the function can modify x itself — unlike ordinary pass-by-value, where a copy would be modified instead.

Passing &x gives the function x's real address, so *p = *p * 2; inside doubleValue changes x itself — this is called pass-by-reference (via a pointer), and is the only way a function can permanently change a variable that belongs to its caller.

L9 — Q5

Calculate the sum of array elements using pointers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int arr[5] = {2, 4, 6, 8, 10};
    int *ptr = arr;
    int sum = 0, i;

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        sum = sum + *(ptr + i);
    }
    printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
sum = sum + *(ptr + i);Same accumulator pattern as every array-sum question; *(ptr + i) reads the value exactly like arr[i] would.

Same accumulation pattern as every other "sum of array" question, just reading each element via *(ptr + i) instead of arr[i].

L10 — Dynamic Memory Allocation

L10 — Dynamic Sum Calculator (Basic)

Dynamically allocate memory for n integers, take input, and print the sum.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, i, sum = 0;
    printf("Enter n: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *arr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (arr == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    printf("Enter %d numbers: ", n);
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) sum = sum + arr[i];
    printf("Sum = %d\n", sum);

    free(arr);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *arr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));Allocates room for exactly n ints on the heap.
if (arr == NULL) { ...; return 1; }Required safety check before using arr for anything.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) sum = sum + arr[i];The usual accumulator loop, reading from the dynamically-allocated block exactly like an ordinary array.
free(arr);Releases the heap memory once it's no longer needed.

This is the DMA-input pattern from Tab 1, applied directly: allocate exactly n ints, check for NULL, fill them, sum them with the usual accumulator loop, then free. It's the base template you should be able to write from memory in under a minute.

L10 — Even Numbers Storage (Intermediate)

Dynamically allocate memory for 10 integers, and store only the even numbers entered by the user.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int input, count = 0, i;
    int *evens = (int *)malloc(10 * sizeof(int));
    if (evens == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        scanf("%d", &input);
        if (input % 2 == 0) {
            evens[count] = input;
            count++;
        }
    }

    printf("Even numbers stored: ");
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%d ", evens[i]);

    free(evens);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int input, count = 0, i;input is a throwaway variable for whatever was just typed; count tracks how many even numbers have been stored so far.
scanf("%d", &input); if (input % 2 == 0) { evens[count] = input; count++; }Every typed number is checked; only even ones get copied into evens, at the next free slot (count), which then advances by one.
for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%d ", evens[i]);Prints only up to count (the number of evens actually stored), not up to 10 — the rest of the allocated array was never filled.

input is a throwaway variable that receives every typed number; only when it's even does it get copied into evens[count], and only then does count advance. So count ends up meaning two things at once: how many even numbers were stored, and the next free slot to write into — which is also exactly how far the print loop should go, not all the way to 10.

L10 — Dynamic Array with realloc() (Advanced)

(1) Allocate memory for 3 integers. (2) Take input. (3) If the user wants to enter more numbers, resize using realloc(). (4) Display all entered numbers.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int i, choice, more;
    int *arr = (int *)malloc(3 * sizeof(int));
    if (arr == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    printf("Enter 3 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    int total = 3;
    printf("Enter more numbers? (1 = Yes, 0 = No): ");
    scanf("%d", &choice);

    while (choice == 1) {
        printf("How many more: ");
        scanf("%d", &more);

        arr = (int *)realloc(arr, (total + more) * sizeof(int));
        if (arr == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }

        printf("Enter %d more numbers: ", more);
        for (i = total; i < total + more; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);
        total = total + more;

        printf("Enter more numbers? (1 = Yes, 0 = No): ");
        scanf("%d", &choice);
    }

    printf("All numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < total; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);

    free(arr);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *arr = (int *)malloc(3 * sizeof(int));Initial allocation for exactly 3 ints.
while (choice == 1) {Repeats the whole expansion process as many times as the user wants more numbers.
arr = (int *)realloc(arr, (total + more) * sizeof(int));Resizes to total + more — the old size plus however many new numbers are being added this round.
for (i = total; i < total + more; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);Fills only the newly added slots, starting right after the last previously-filled index.
total = total + more;Only updated after the new numbers are safely read in, so it always reflects exactly how many valid values exist.

total tracks exactly how many valid integers are currently stored, and it's what everything else is built around: the input loop for new numbers starts at total (so it doesn't overwrite existing data) and runs to total + more; realloc is asked to resize to exactly that new size; and only after the new numbers are safely read in does total get updated to include them. The while (choice == 1) loop lets the user repeat this expansion as many times as they like.

L11 — Structures

L11 — Simple Student Structure (Basic)

Create a struct named student that stores student id, name and CGPA. Create one object and print all details.

#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float cgpa;
} student;

int main() {
    student s1;

    printf("Enter ID: ");
    scanf("%d", &s1.id);
    printf("Enter name: ");
    scanf("%s", s1.name);
    printf("Enter CGPA: ");
    scanf("%f", &s1.cgpa);

    printf("\nID: %d\nName: %s\nCGPA: %.2f\n", s1.id, s1.name, s1.cgpa);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
student s1;Creates one student object with all members uninitialised, ready to be filled by scanf.
scanf("%d", &s1.id);&s1.id is the address of the id member — needed since it's an ordinary int.
scanf("%s", s1.name);No & here: s1.name is a char[] member, and array members decay to an address just like standalone arrays do.
printf("...", s1.id, s1.name, s1.cgpa);Dot-access for every member when reading their values back for printing.

Note scanf("%s", s1.name) has no & — s1.name is itself an array, and array names already decay to an address (same rule as ordinary string input). But &s1.id and &s1.cgpa DO need &, since int and float members are ordinary values, not arrays.

L11 — Pointer to Struct (Basic)

Modify the previous task so the student object is stored via a pointer, and the pointer is used to print the details.

#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float cgpa;
} student;

int main() {
    student s1;
    student *ptr = &s1;

    printf("Enter ID: ");
    scanf("%d", &ptr->id);
    printf("Enter name: ");
    scanf("%s", ptr->name);
    printf("Enter CGPA: ");
    scanf("%f", &ptr->cgpa);

    printf("\nID: %d\nName: %s\nCGPA: %.2f\n", ptr->id, ptr->name, ptr->cgpa);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
student *ptr = &s1;ptr holds the address of the whole s1 object.
scanf("%d", &ptr->id);The arrow reads the member; & in front still supplies the address scanf needs, since id is an ordinary int, not an array.
scanf("%s", ptr->name);No & needed here either — same array-decay rule as before, just accessed via -> instead of ..

Every . from Q1 becomes -> since we now hold a pointer to the struct rather than the struct itself. &ptr->id reads as "the address of the id member of the struct that ptr points to" — needed because scanf always wants an address, and the arrow doesn't change that rule for non-array members.

L11 — CGPA Calculator with Functions (Advanced) Simplified to the course's actual final-exam ceiling — the full assignment version (5 quizzes, best-3-of-5, multi-course CGPA) is solved in Tab 3 → Assignment 2, Q3, and is explicitly harder than anything expected on the final.

Struct stores id, name, 4 quiz marks (array) and cgpa. Take input for id, name and 4 quizzes (assume all 3-credit courses). Using a function, determine each course's GPA, then the overall CGPA. Grading: 80+ → 4.0, 60+ → 3.0, 50+ → 2.0, else 0.0.

#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    int quizzes[4];
    float cgpa;
} student;

float getGPA(int marks) {
    if (marks >= 80) return 4.0;
    else if (marks >= 60) return 3.0;
    else if (marks >= 50) return 2.0;
    else return 0.0;
}

int main() {
    student s;
    int i, total = 0;

    printf("Enter ID: ");
    scanf("%d", &s.id);
    printf("Enter name: ");
    scanf("%s", s.name);

    printf("Enter 4 quiz marks: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        scanf("%d", &s.quizzes[i]);
        total = total + s.quizzes[i];
    }

    float avgMarks = (float)total / 4;
    s.cgpa = getGPA((int)avgMarks);

    printf("\nID: %d\nName: %s\nCGPA: %.2f\n", s.id, s.name, s.cgpa);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int quizzes[4];An array declared as a struct member — works exactly like a standalone array, just living inside the struct.
float getGPA(int marks) { if (marks >= 80) return 4.0; ... }A standalone helper function, kept separate from main so the grading rule is written once and reused.
scanf("%d", &s.quizzes[i]); total += s.quizzes[i];Combines dot-access (s's member) with array indexing ([i]) to read into and accumulate from one quiz slot at a time.
float avgMarks = (float)total / 4;Casts before dividing, so the average keeps its decimal part instead of truncating.
s.cgpa = getGPA((int)avgMarks);Calls the helper function, passing the computed average, and stores the returned grade point directly into the struct's cgpa member.

The struct now holds an array as a member (quizzes[4]) alongside ordinary members — access it with s.quizzes[i], combining dot-access with array indexing. The getGPA function is a small user-defined helper exactly of the kind your faculty encourages: it takes one course's average mark and converts it to a grade point using the same if/else-if/else-if/else ladder from Tab 1, kept separate from main so the grading rule only has to be written once.
