Lab 3A — Conditional Statements

Lab 3A — Q1

Take an input voltage level (volts) and classify it: 0–5V “Logic Level Voltage”; >5V up to 230V “AC Mains Voltage”; >230V “High Voltage – Dangerous!”

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float v;
    printf("Enter voltage: ");
    scanf("%f", &v);

    if (v >= 0 && v <= 5) {
        printf("Logic Level Voltage\n");
    } else if (v > 5 && v <= 230) {
        printf("AC Mains Voltage\n");
    } else {
        printf("High Voltage - Dangerous!\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (v >= 0 && v <= 5) {First range: both bounds checked together with &&.
else if (v > 5 && v <= 230) {Only reached if the first range failed; checks the next band.
else { ... }Catches everything not covered above — v > 230 (and, harmlessly, negative inputs too).

Three ranges, three branches. The final else quietly catches every remaining case (v > 230, and also any negative input, which physically shouldn't happen but the code doesn't crash on it either).

Lab 3A — Q2

Given three-phase voltages Va, Vb, Vc: report balanced (all 220–240V), undervoltage (any phase <220V), overvoltage (any phase >240V), or phase failure (any phase = 0V).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float va, vb, vc;
    printf("Enter Va, Vb, Vc: ");
    scanf("%f %f %f", &va, &vb, &vc);

    if (va == 0 || vb == 0 || vc == 0) {
        printf("Phase failure detected.\n");
    } else if (va < 220 || vb < 220 || vc < 220) {
        printf("Phase undervoltage detected.\n");
    } else if (va > 240 || vb > 240 || vc > 240) {
        printf("Phase overvoltage detected.\n");
    } else {
        printf("System is balanced.\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (va == 0 || vb == 0 || vc == 0) {Checked first, since a 0V failure would otherwise also satisfy the undervoltage check below it.
else if (va < 220 || vb < 220 || vc < 220) {Only reached once failure is ruled out; catches any phase below the normal band.
else if (va > 240 || vb > 240 || vc > 240) {Only reached once both failure and undervoltage are ruled out.
else { printf("System is balanced.\n"); }Only reached if none of the three problem conditions matched — every phase must be within 220–240V.

Order matters here: a failed phase (0V) is technically also “below 220V”, so the failure check must come first in the chain, otherwise it would incorrectly get reported as undervoltage instead. Whenever two conditions can overlap, put the more specific/urgent one earlier in the if...else if chain.

Lab 3A — Q3

Given motor temperature and supply voltage, report normal running, overheating warning, shutdown (overheated), or shutdown (abnormal voltage).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float temp, voltage;
    printf("Enter motor temperature and supply voltage: ");
    scanf("%f %f", &temp, &voltage);

    if (temp > 90) {
        printf("Motor shutdown - Overheated!\n");
    } else if (voltage < 210 || voltage > 250) {
        printf("Voltage abnormal - Motor shutdown!\n");
    } else if (temp > 70 && temp < 90) {
        printf("Warning: Motor overheating!\n");
    } else if (temp < 70 && voltage >= 210 && voltage <= 230) {
        printf("Motor running normally.\n");
    } else {
        printf("Readings do not clearly match any listed state.\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (temp > 90) {Checked first: the most severe condition, regardless of voltage.
else if (voltage < 210 || voltage > 250) {Second priority: an abnormal voltage, only checked once extreme overheating is ruled out.
else if (temp > 70 && temp < 90) {A milder warning band, only reached once both shutdown conditions are ruled out.
else if (temp < 70 && voltage >= 210 && voltage <= 230) {The "all clear" condition — needs all three sub-conditions true at once.
else { printf("Readings do not clearly match any listed state.\n"); }A safety-net default for combinations the four rules don't explicitly cover.

The most dangerous condition (temperature already past 90°C) is checked first, before the voltage check, so an overheating shutdown is never masked by an unrelated voltage message. The final else is a safety net for readings the four given rules simply don't describe (e.g. 75°C with 235V) — a real controller needs some default branch; it should never be silently skipped.

Lab 3A — Q4

Given voltage and load resistance, compute the current and print the appropriate wire gauge.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float voltage, resistance, current;
    printf("Enter voltage (V) and load resistance (ohms): ");
    scanf("%f %f", &voltage, &resistance);

    current = voltage / resistance;
    printf("Current = %.2f A\n", current);

    if (current <= 5) {
        printf("Use 1mm^2 wire.\n");
    } else if (current <= 15) {
        printf("Use 2.5mm^2 wire.\n");
    } else if (current <= 30) {
        printf("Use 4mm^2 wire.\n");
    } else {
        printf("Use 6mm^2 or higher gauge wire.\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
current = voltage / resistance;Ohm's law: current equals voltage divided by resistance.
if (current <= 5) ... else if (current <= 15) ... else if (current <= 30) ... else ...A tiered ladder, exactly like the electricity-bill pattern — each branch only needs its upper bound, since failing every branch above already ruled out the lower bound.

Ohm's law (I = V / R) is computed first and stored, then plugged straight into the same tiered if...else if ladder pattern used in the electricity-bill question (L4-Q5) — each new else if only needs to check the upper bound, since the lower bound was already ruled out by every branch above it failing.

Lab 3B — Loop-related problems (quick set)

Lab 3B (quick) — Q1

Count and display all numbers between a and b (a < b) divisible by both 3 and 5.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a, b, i, count = 0;
    printf("Input lower limit (a): ");
    scanf("%d", &a);
    printf("Input upper limit (b): ");
    scanf("%d", &b);

    printf("Numbers divisible by both 3 and 5 are: ");
    for (i = a; i <= b; i++) {
        if (i % 3 == 0 && i % 5 == 0) {
            printf("%d ", i);
            count++;
        }
    }
    printf("\nTotal count = %d\n", count);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = a; i <= b; i++) {Starts at the user's lower limit, not a fixed value — stops at b inclusive.
if (i % 3 == 0 && i % 5 == 0) { printf("%d ", i); count++; }Both printing and counting happen together, in the same branch, so they always stay consistent with each other.

The loop runs from a to b inclusive; every qualifying number is both printed immediately and tallied in count, so the final count is always consistent with what was printed above it.

Lab 3B (quick) — Q2

Display nn for every number from 1 up to n.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j;
    long power;
    printf("Input number of terms: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        power = 1;
        for (j = 0; j < i; j++) {
            power = power * i;
        }
        printf("%d^%d = %ld\n", i, i, power);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) { power = 1;Reset every outer pass, so each number's power calculation starts fresh.
for (j = 0; j < i; j++) { power = power * i; }Multiplies by i exactly i times — the inner loop count grows with the outer loop, since we want i to the power i.

power is reset to 1 at the start of every outer pass, then the inner loop multiplies it by i, exactly i times — that's how you compute i raised to the power i using only multiplication, one term at a time.

Lab 3B (quick) — Q3

Print an n×n square pattern using *.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j;
    printf("Input number of rows (n): ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        for (j = 0; j < n; j++) {
            printf("* ");
        }
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {Counts rows, 0 through n-1.
for (j = 0; j < n; j++) { printf("* "); }Prints exactly n stars, unconditionally, for every row.

Two nested loops of the same length n: the outer counts rows, the inner prints exactly n stars per row before the row-ending \n.

Lab 3B (quick) — Q4

Print a right-aligned right-angled triangle of increasing numbers per row.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j, k;
    printf("Input number of rows (n): ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (k = 0; k < n - i; k++) {
            printf(" ");
        }
        for (j = 1; j <= i; j++) {
            printf("%d", j);
        }
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (k = 0; k < n - i; k++) { printf(" "); }Leading spaces: shrinks as i grows, which is what pushes the numbers toward the right edge.
for (j = 1; j <= i; j++) { printf("%d", j); }Prints the digits 1 through i for this row, right after the spaces.

Every pattern-printing question splits into the same two jobs per row: print the right number of leading spaces first (here, n - i, which shrinks as i grows — that's what pushes the triangle to the right), then print the actual content (1 to i).

Experiment 3B — Loops (full lab sheet)

Experiment 3B — Task 1

Read a positive integer N. Compute the sum of all even numbers and the sum of all odd numbers from 1 to N, separately.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, evenSum = 0, oddSum = 0;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        if (i % 2 == 0) evenSum += i;
        else oddSum += i;
    }

    printf("Sum of even numbers = %d\n", evenSum);
    printf("Sum of odd numbers = %d\n", oddSum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (i % 2 == 0) evenSum += i; else oddSum += i;Each number adds into exactly one of the two totals, decided by its own remainder.

One loop, one if/else that routes every number into exactly one of two independent accumulators. For N=10: even → 2+4+6+8+10=30, odd → 1+3+5+7+9=25, matching the sample.

Experiment 3B — Task 2

Read N. Using % and /, find how many digits N has, and how many of those digits are smaller than 5.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    long n;
    int totalDigits = 0, smallCount = 0, digit;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%ld", &n);

    while (n > 0) {
        digit = n % 10;
        totalDigits++;
        if (digit < 5) smallCount++;
        n = n / 10;
    }

    printf("Total digits = %d\n", totalDigits);
    printf("Digits smaller than 5 = %d\n", smallCount);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
while (n > 0) {Continues until every digit has been peeled off and n reaches 0.
digit = n % 10;The remainder after dividing by 10 is exactly the last digit.
totalDigits++; if (digit < 5) smallCount++;Every digit is counted once; separately, it's also checked against the <5 threshold.
n = n / 10;Integer division drops the last digit, shifting everything else one place to the right for the next pass.

The classic digit-peeling loop: n % 10 peels off the last digit, n = n / 10 drops it, and the loop naturally ends once n reaches 0. Every peeled digit is counted once, and separately checked against <5. For 8241937 the digits are 7,3,9,1,4,2,8 (in peeling order) — 7 digits total, and 1,3,4,2 are the four smaller than 5, matching the sample.

Experiment 3B — Task 3

Approximate ex using the truncated series 1 + x + x2/2! + … + xN/N!, computing each power and factorial with their own inner loops (no pow()).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float x, sum = 0, term, power, fact;
    int n, k, j;
    printf("Enter x and N: ");
    scanf("%f %d", &x, &n);

    for (k = 0; k <= n; k++) {
        power = 1;
        for (j = 0; j < k; j++) power = power * x;

        fact = 1;
        for (j = 1; j <= k; j++) fact = fact * j;

        term = power / fact;
        sum = sum + term;
    }

    printf("Series approximation = %f\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (k = 0; k <= n; k++) {One pass per term in the series, k=0 through N.
power = 1; for (j = 0; j < k; j++) power = power * x;Builds x to the power k by multiplying x into power, exactly k times.
fact = 1; for (j = 1; j <= k; j++) fact = fact * j;A second, independent inner loop builds k! the same repeated-multiplication way.
term = power / fact; sum = sum + term;Combines this term and adds it into the running approximation.

The outer loop counts terms k = 0 to N. For each term, one inner loop builds xk by repeated multiplication, a second, independent inner loop builds k! the same way — then term = power / fact and it's added to the running sum. Keeping the two inner loops separate (rather than trying to combine them) keeps each one simple and easy to get right.

Experiment 3B — Task 4

Read N (1–9) and compute S = 11 + 22 + … + NN, printing every term and the running sum as it progresses.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, k, j;
    long term, sum = 0;
    printf("Enter N (1-9): ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (k = 1; k <= n; k++) {
        term = 1;
        for (j = 0; j < k; j++) {
            term = term * k;
        }
        sum = sum + term;
        printf("k = %d : term = %ld    sum = %ld\n", k, term, sum);
    }

    printf("Final sum S = %ld\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
term = 1; for (j = 0; j < k; j++) { term = term * k; }Computes k to the power k by repeated multiplication, reset to 1 at the start of every outer pass.
sum = sum + term;Adds this term into the running total — sum is declared once, outside the loop, so it correctly accumulates across every k.
printf("k = %d : term = %ld sum = %ld\n", k, term, sum);Prints the current term and the running sum together, once per outer pass.

Exactly the nn pattern from Lab 3B (quick)-Q2, but now each term is immediately added into a running sum and both are printed on the same line before moving to the next k. This exact program (with the bug of using addition instead of multiplication, and no reset of the accumulator) is what Assignment 1 Q11 asks you to debug — see Assignment 1 below.

Experiment 3B — Task 5

Read n (2–26) and print an inverted triangle of the first n capital letters, dropping the last letter each row.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j;
    printf("Enter n: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = n; i >= 1; i--) {
        for (j = 0; j < i; j++) {
            printf("%c ", 65 + j);
        }
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = n; i >= 1; i--) {Counts down from n to 1, so each successive row is shorter than the last.
for (j = 0; j < i; j++) { printf("%c ", 65 + j); }Prints letters A onward, i of them, using 65 + j to compute each letter's ASCII code directly.

Characters are small integers in C, so 65 + j (65 is ASCII for 'A') computes the j-th letter directly, printed with %c. The outer loop counts down from n to 1, so each row simply prints one fewer letter than the last, starting from A every time.

Experiment 3B — Task 6

Read n (≥3) and print (a) a hollow square (stars on the border only), then (b) a rhombus of n rows shifted progressively left going down.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j, k;
    printf("Enter n: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    /* (a) hollow square */
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (j = 1; j <= n; j++) {
            if (i == 1 || i == n || j == 1 || j == n) printf("*");
            else printf(" ");
        }
        printf("\n");
    }

    /* (b) rhombus */
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (k = 0; k < n - i; k++) printf(" ");
        for (j = 0; j < n; j++) printf("*");
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (i == 1 || i == n || j == 1 || j == n) printf("*"); else printf(" ");A star only on the border rows/columns; everywhere else, a space — that's what leaves the middle hollow.
for (k = 0; k < n - i; k++) printf(" ");Leading spaces that shrink as i grows, shifting each row one step further left than the one above it.
for (j = 0; j < n; j++) printf("*");Always exactly n stars per row, regardless of the leading spaces.

(a) A star is printed only on the first/last row or first/last column (i==1 || i==n || j==1 || j==n); everywhere else prints a space, leaving the middle hollow. (b) Reuses the leading-spaces trick from Lab 3B (quick)-Q4: row i gets n - i leading spaces before its n stars, so the top row is pushed furthest right and each row below shifts one step to the left.

Experiment 3B — Task 7

Read n and print a diamond: the upper half has n rows, row i showing 2i−1 centred stars; the lower half mirrors the upper half.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j, k;
    printf("Enter n: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    /* upper half, including the widest row */
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (k = 0; k < n - i; k++) printf(" ");
        for (j = 0; j < 2 * i - 1; j++) printf("*");
        printf("\n");
    }
    /* lower half mirrors the upper half, widest row already printed once */
    for (i = n - 1; i >= 1; i--) {
        for (k = 0; k < n - i; k++) printf(" ");
        for (j = 0; j < 2 * i - 1; j++) printf("*");
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) { ... }Upper half: grows from 1 star (row 1) up to the widest row (row n).
for (j = 0; j < 2 * i - 1; j++) printf("*");2*i - 1 gives 1, 3, 5, 7... as i increases — each row 2 stars wider than the last.
for (i = n - 1; i >= 1; i--) { ... }Lower half: starts at n-1 (not n, to avoid repeating the widest row) and counts back down to 1, mirroring the upper half.

The star count 2*i - 1 is what makes each row grow by exactly 2 stars (1, 3, 5, 7, …), which is what "centres" the triangle when combined with n - i leading spaces on each side implicitly (the spaces shrink at exactly the rate the stars grow, keeping everything centred). The second loop simply replays rows n-1 down to 1 to mirror the shape without repeating the widest row twice.

Lab 5 — Arrays & Strings

Lab 5 — Task 1

Read 10 numbers into an array. Read an index (0–9) and print the value at that index.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a[10], i, index;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &a[i]);

    printf("Enter an index (0-9): ");
    scanf("%d", &index);
    printf("a[%d] = %d\n", index, a[index]);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &a[i]);Fills all 10 slots.
printf("a[%d] = %d\n", index, a[index]);Direct lookup at whatever index the user typed — no loop or search needed for this part.

The array is filled once with the standard input loop; after that, a[index] directly retrieves whichever element the user asks for — array indexing is just a lookup, no searching required.

Lab 5 — Task 2

Read 5 numbers and print the sum, followed by all the numbers.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a[5], i, sum = 0;
    printf("Enter 5 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        scanf("%d", &a[i]);
        sum = sum + a[i];
    }

    printf("Sum: %d\n", sum);
    printf("Numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        printf("%d", a[i]);
        if (i < 4) printf(", ");
    }
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
scanf("%d", &a[i]); sum = sum + a[i];Reading and accumulating happen together in one loop — no separate summing pass needed.
if (i < 4) printf(", ");Only prints a comma between numbers, not after the last one.

The sum is accumulated while reading the input (one combined loop), so a second pass isn't needed just to total the values — only the final comma-separated printout needs its own loop, since it needs to know when to stop printing commas (i < 4, i.e. not after the last number).

Lab 5 — Task 3

Read 5 numbers and print them in reverse order.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a[5], i;
    printf("Enter 5 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) scanf("%d", &a[i]);

    for (i = 4; i >= 0; i--) {
        printf("%d ", a[i]);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 4; i >= 0; i--) { printf("%d ", a[i]); }Starts at the last valid index and counts down to 0, printing as it goes.

Reading backwards needs no extra storage — just start the print loop at the last valid index (4, not 5) and count down to 0.

Lab 5 — Task 4

Read 10 numbers and print only the even ones, in reverse order.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a[10], i;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &a[i]);

    for (i = 9; i >= 0; i--) {
        if (a[i] % 2 == 0) printf("%d ", a[i]);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 9; i >= 0; i--) { if (a[i] % 2 == 0) printf("%d ", a[i]); }Walks backward (index 9 down to 0); the if only filters what gets printed, it doesn't affect the loop's direction.

Combines Task 3's backward loop with the even-number filter from L5-Q1 (Tab 2) — the if only decides whether to print each element, it never changes the direction of the loop itself.

Lab 5 — Task 5

Read 10 numbers, then a search number; print “Yes found at index X” or “No”.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a[10], i, key, found = 0;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &a[i]);

    printf("Enter number to search: ");
    scanf("%d", &key);

    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        if (a[i] == key) {
            printf("Yes found at index %d\n", i);
            found = 1;
            break;
        }
    }
    if (!found) printf("No\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (a[i] == key) { printf(...); found = 1; break; }Prints immediately on match, sets the flag, and exits the loop right away.
if (!found) printf("No\n");Only runs if the loop completed without ever matching.

Standard linear search. break stops the moment a match is found (both to avoid wasted work and so the message only prints once); the found flag lets the code print “No” after the loop only if no match was ever hit.

Lab 5 — Task 6

Repeat Task 1, but validate the index input: keep asking while it's outside 0–9.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a[10], i, index;
    printf("Enter 10 numbers: ");
    for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) scanf("%d", &a[i]);

    printf("Enter a number between 0-9: ");
    scanf("%d", &index);
    while (index < 0 || index > 9) {
        printf("Enter a number between 0-9\n");
        scanf("%d", &index);
    }

    printf("a[%d] = %d\n", index, a[index]);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
scanf("%d", &index);One attempt is read in before the loop even starts.
while (index < 0 || index > 9) { ...; scanf("%d", &index); }Keeps re-asking only while the value is out of range; the moment a valid index arrives the condition fails and the loop exits.

This is the textbook use for a while loop over a do...while: we already have one attempt read in before the loop, and the loop's only job is to keep re-asking while the value is still invalid — the moment a valid index arrives, the loop condition fails and we fall straight through to use it.

Lab 5 — Task 7

Concatenate string a with string b manually (e.g. “foot” + “ball” = “football”).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char a[50], b[50], c[100];
    int i = 0, j = 0, k = 0;

    printf("Enter first string: ");
    scanf("%s", a);
    printf("Enter second string: ");
    scanf("%s", b);

    while (a[i] != '\0') { c[k] = a[i]; i++; k++; }
    while (b[j] != '\0') { c[k] = b[j]; j++; k++; }
    c[k] = '\0';

    printf("Concatenated string: %s\n", c);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
while (a[i] != '\0') { c[k] = a[i]; i++; k++; }Copies all of a into c, advancing both the read index i and the write index k.
while (b[j] != '\0') { c[k] = b[j]; j++; k++; }Copies all of b into c, continuing from wherever k left off — so it's appended, not overwritten.
c[k] = '\0';Terminates the combined string once both copies are done.

k tracks the next free slot in c across both copy loops — it keeps counting up from where the first loop left it, so the second string is appended right after the first, not overwriting it. The final c[k] = '\0'; after both loops is what makes c a valid, complete string.

Lab 5 — Task 8 Same technique as Tab 2 → L8P2-Q6

Determine whether a word is a palindrome.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[100];
    int len = 0, i, isPalin = 1;
    printf("Enter a word: ");
    scanf("%s", str);

    while (str[len] != '\0') len++;

    for (i = 0; i < len / 2; i++) {
        if (str[i] != str[len - 1 - i]) {
            isPalin = 0;
            break;
        }
    }

    if (isPalin) printf("Palindrome\n");
    else printf("Not a palindrome\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
while (str[len] != '\0') len++;Finds the length first.
if (str[i] != str[len - 1 - i]) { isPalin = 0; break; }Compares each front/back pair; any mismatch disproves the palindrome immediately.

Same technique already taught in Tab 2 (L8P2-Q6): find the length, then compare symmetric pairs from both ends moving inward, stopping as soon as any pair disagrees.

Lab 5 — Task 9 Same technique as Tab 2 → L8P2-Q2

Count how many times a given word appears in a sentence.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char sentence[200], word[50];
    int i, j, count = 0, match;

    printf("Enter a sentence: ");
    fgets(sentence, sizeof(sentence), stdin);
    printf("Enter word to count: ");
    scanf("%s", word);

    for (i = 0; sentence[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        match = 1;
        for (j = 0; word[j] != '\0'; j++) {
            if (sentence[i + j] != word[j]) { match = 0; break; }
        }
        if (match) count++;
    }
    printf("Occurrences of \"%s\": %d\n", word, count);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (j = 0; word[j] != '\0'; j++) { if (sentence[i+j] != word[j]) {...} }Tries to match word starting at position i; match stays 1 only if every character lines up.
if (match) count++;Tallies a match; note this simplified version doesn't check word boundaries, so it also matches inside longer words.

Same idea already used in Tab 2 (L8P2-Q2): try to match word starting at every position in sentence, counting each full match. (This simplified version counts overlapping/partial matches inside longer words too; the whole-word-boundary check shown in Tab 2's version is the stricter form if the question demands exact word matches only.)

Experiment 6 — Pointers & Dynamic Memory Allocation

Experiment 6 — Task 1: Digit-Position Power Sum Using Pointers

Calculate the sum of each digit raised to the power of its position (counted from the rightmost digit, starting at 1), using pointers throughout.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    long n, *pn;
    long sum, *psum;
    int position;
    int digit;

    n = 0;
    pn = &n;
    sum = 0;
    psum = &sum;

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%ld", pn);

    position = 1;
    while (*pn > 0) {
        digit = *pn % 10;

        long p = 1, k;
        for (k = 0; k < position; k++) p = p * digit;

        *psum = *psum + p;
        *pn = *pn / 10;
        position++;
    }

    printf("Sum = %ld\n", *psum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
long n, *pn; long sum, *psum;Every ordinary variable the algorithm needs (n, sum) gets a matching pointer (pn, psum) pointing at it.
pn = &n; psum = &sum;The pointers are pointed at their variables up front, once.
scanf("%ld", pn);No & needed — pn already holds n's address.
digit = *pn % 10;Peels the last digit off the number currently stored at the address pn points to.
for (k = 0; k < position; k++) p = p * digit;Raises digit to the current position by multiplying it into p, position times.
*psum = *psum + p;Adds this digit's contribution into the running total, written through the pointer.
*pn = *pn / 10; position++;Drops the digit just processed and advances to the next position, both via the pointer for n.

pn and psum hold the addresses of n and sum respectively, so every place the algorithm would normally say n or sum instead says *pn or *psum — that's literally the only change from an ordinary version. scanf("%ld", pn) needs no extra &, because pn already is an address.

The algorithm itself: peel the last digit off with %10, raise it to the current position using a small repeated-multiplication loop, add that into the running *psum, then drop the digit with /10 and advance position. For 5321: rightmost digit 1 at position 1 (11=1), then 2 at position 2 (22=4), then 3 at position 3 (33=27), then 5 at position 4 (54=625). 1+4+27+625 = 657, matching the sample.

Experiment 6 — Task 2: Checking the Frequency of a Number Using Pointers

Given n integers, a search value and a minimum occurrence count, print “Good Number” if the value occurs more than the minimum, else “Bad Number” — using pointers throughout.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, a[100], i, *arr, *p;
    int searchVal, minOcc, count = 0;

    printf("Enter the number of elements: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);
    arr = a;   /* arr now points at the first element of a[] */

    printf("Enter %d numbers: ", n);
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        scanf("%d", (arr + i));
    }

    printf("Enter the value to search: ");
    scanf("%d", &searchVal);
    printf("Enter the minimum occurrence: ");
    scanf("%d", &minOcc);

    for (p = arr; p < arr + n; p++) {
        if (*p == searchVal) count++;
    }

    printf("%d appears %d time(s).\n", searchVal, count);
    if (count > minOcc) printf("Good Number\n");
    else printf("Bad Number\n");

    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
arr = a;Points arr at the first element of the backing array a.
scanf("%d", (arr + i));arr + i is the address of the i-th element — no & needed since this is already an address.
for (p = arr; p < arr + n; p++) { if (*p == searchVal) count++; }Pure pointer-walk version of the counting loop: p starts at arr and advances one element at a time until it passes the last valid element (arr + n).

arr = a; makes arr point at a[0]; from then on arr + i is the address of a[i], so scanf("%d", (arr + i)) reads directly into it with no & needed. The counting loop is written purely in pointer form: p starts at arr and walks forward (p++) until it reaches arr + n (one past the last element) — the array-index style for(i=0;i<n;i++) and this pointer style are mechanically identical, just written differently.

For the sample (n=7, array 2 5 2 7 2 9 4, search 2, min 2): the value 2 occurs 3 times, and 3 > 2, so the program reports Good Number, matching the sample.

Experiment 6 — Task 3: Perfect String Using Dynamic Memory Allocation

Dynamically allocate storage for a single alphabetic word, count vowels and consonants (case-insensitive), and report “Perfect String” if they're equal.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int length, i;
    char *word;
    int vowels = 0, consonants = 0;
    char c;

    printf("Enter the length of the word: ");
    scanf("%d", &length);

    word = (char *)malloc((length + 1) * sizeof(char));
    if (word == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    printf("Enter the word: ");
    scanf("%s", word);

    for (i = 0; word[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        c = word[i];
        if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32;   /* normalise to lowercase */

        if (c == 'a' || c == 'e' || c == 'i' || c == 'o' || c == 'u') {
            vowels++;
        } else {
            consonants++;
        }
    }

    printf("Vowels = %d\n", vowels);
    printf("Consonants = %d\n", consonants);

    if (vowels == consonants) printf("Perfect String\n");
    else printf("Imperfection Present\n");

    free(word);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
word = (char *)malloc((length + 1) * sizeof(char));Allocates exactly enough room for the word plus its null terminator (+1).
c = word[i]; if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32;Normalises each character to lowercase first (only if it's currently uppercase), so the vowel check works regardless of the original case.
if (c == 'a' || ... ) vowels++; else consonants++;Every letter is either a vowel or a consonant — with input restricted to letters only, else is safe to use here.
if (vowels == consonants) printf("Perfect String\n"); else ...Compares the two final tallies once the whole word has been scanned.

malloc((length + 1) * sizeof(char)) — the +1 reserves room for the null terminator on top of the length visible characters, exactly like a normal string array must be one byte bigger than its visible text. Each character is normalised to lowercase (+32, the same ASCII trick as L8P2-Q1 but in reverse) before checking whether it's a vowel, so the check works regardless of the case the user typed.

For “BaNaNa”: lowercased it's b-a-n-a-n-a — 3 vowels (a,a,a), 3 consonants (b,n,n) → equal → Perfect String, matching the sample exactly.

Experiment 6 — Task 4: Dynamic Array Expansion, Average and Sorting

Allocate memory for an initial set of numbers, compute the average, optionally grow the array with realloc() and take more numbers, recompute the average, then sort everything ascending.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, extra, choice, i, j, temp, total;
    float sum = 0, avg;

    printf("Enter number of values: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *arr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (arr == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    printf("Enter %d numbers: ", n);
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        scanf("%d", &arr[i]);
        sum += arr[i];
    }
    avg = sum / n;
    printf("Average = %.2f\n", avg);

    total = n;
    printf("Do you want to add more numbers? (1 for Yes / 0 for No): ");
    scanf("%d", &choice);

    if (choice == 1) {
        printf("How many additional numbers: ");
        scanf("%d", &extra);

        arr = (int *)realloc(arr, (n + extra) * sizeof(int));
        if (arr == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }

        printf("Enter %d additional numbers: ", extra);
        for (i = n; i < n + extra; i++) {
            scanf("%d", &arr[i]);
            sum += arr[i];
        }
        total = n + extra;
        avg = sum / total;
        printf("Updated Average = %.2f\n", avg);
    }

    /* bubble sort ascending */
    for (i = 0; i < total - 1; i++) {
        for (j = 0; j < total - i - 1; j++) {
            if (arr[j] > arr[j + 1]) {
                temp = arr[j];
                arr[j] = arr[j + 1];
                arr[j + 1] = temp;
            }
        }
    }

    printf("Numbers in ascending order:\n");
    for (i = 0; i < total; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    printf("\n");

    free(arr);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
sum += arr[i];Accumulated while reading the initial input.
avg = sum / n;First average, computed once initial input is done.
if (choice == 1) { ... arr = realloc(...); ... }Growth only happens if the user opts in; realloc resizes to n + extra.
for (i = n; i < n + extra; i++) { scanf(...); sum += arr[i]; }Fills only the new slots (starting at the old size n) and keeps accumulating into the same sum.
total = n + extra; avg = sum / total;Recomputes the average using the updated element count and the updated sum.
for (i...) for (j...) { if (arr[j] > arr[j+1]) swap; }Standard bubble sort, run over total elements (whether or not growth happened).

total is the single source of truth for “how many valid numbers are in arr right now” — it starts at n, and only becomes n + extra once the additional numbers have actually been read in. Everything downstream (the updated-average division, and the final sort/print loops) uses total, never a hard-coded size, which is exactly what makes the array genuinely resizable rather than just “two separate fixed arrays glued together”.

Trace against the sample: n=4, values 10 25 15 20 → sum=70, average 17.50. User adds 3 more: 5 30 18 → sum=123, total=7, updated average 123/7=17.5714… → 17.57. Bubble-sorting {10,25,15,20,5,30,18} ascending gives 5 10 15 18 20 25 30 — matching the sample exactly.

Assignment 1

Assignment 1 — Q1

Develop a flowchart to determine the result of the series 1 + 1/23 + 1/33 + … + 1/n3.

Start
↓
Read n
↓
sum = 1, i = 2
↓
i ≤ n ?
↓ Yes
term = 1 / (i × i × i)
sum = sum + term
i = i + 1
↻ loops back up to the "i ≤ n ?" check
↓ No
Print sum
↓
End
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i;
    float sum = 1, term;

    printf("Enter n: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 2; i <= n; i++) {
        term = 1.0 / (i * i * i);
        sum = sum + term;
    }

    printf("Sum = %f\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float sum = 1, term;sum starts at the series' first term (1/1³ = 1), so the loop only has to add the remaining terms.
for (i = 2; i <= n; i++) {Starts at 2 since term 1 is already accounted for by the seed value.
term = 1.0 / (i * i * i);1.0 (a float literal, not 1) forces floating-point division — with a plain 1, integer division would truncate every term to 0.
sum = sum + term;Accumulates each term into the running total.

The flowchart's decision diamond and the C for loop's condition are the same check, just drawn two different ways: i ≤ n controls whether we go around again. Notice sum is seeded at 1 before the loop (that's the series' first term, 1/13), so the loop itself only needs to add terms starting from i = 2.

Assignment 1 — Q2

Determine whether a triangle is right-angled, given its three side lengths.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float a, b, c, largest, s1, s2;
    printf("Enter three side lengths: ");
    scanf("%f %f %f", &a, &b, &c);

    if (a >= b && a >= c)      { largest = a; s1 = b; s2 = c; }
    else if (b >= a && b >= c) { largest = b; s1 = a; s2 = c; }
    else                       { largest = c; s1 = a; s2 = b; }

    if (largest * largest == s1 * s1 + s2 * s2) {
        printf("Right-angled triangle\n");
    } else {
        printf("Not a right-angled triangle\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
if (a >= b && a >= c) { largest=a; s1=b; s2=c; }Identifies a as largest only if it's at least as big as both others; the remaining two go into s1/s2.
else if (b >= a && b >= c) {...} else {...}Same idea for b; if neither a nor b won, c must be largest by elimination.
if (largest * largest == s1 * s1 + s2 * s2) {Pythagoras checked in the one direction that matters, now that we know which side is the (potential) hypotenuse.

Because the hint says to find the largest side first, three if/else branches identify which of a, b, c is largest and label the other two as s1/s2. Pythagoras is then checked in one direction only: largest² == s1² + s2² — there's no need to separately test all three possible “hypotenuse” positions once you know which side is actually the largest.

Assignment 1 — Q3

Grade a sample of steel (A–F) from hardness, carbon content and tensile strength.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float hardness, carbon, tensile;
    int cond1, cond2, cond3, total;

    printf("Enter hardness, carbon content, tensile strength: ");
    scanf("%f %f %f", &hardness, &carbon, &tensile);

    cond1 = (hardness > 30);
    cond2 = (carbon < 0.5);
    cond3 = (tensile > 2600);
    total = cond1 + cond2 + cond3;

    if (cond1 && cond2 && cond3) {
        printf("Grade A\n");
    } else if (cond1 && cond2) {
        printf("Grade B\n");
    } else if (cond2 && cond3) {
        printf("Grade C\n");
    } else if (cond1 && cond3) {
        printf("Grade D\n");
    } else if (total == 1) {
        printf("Grade E\n");
    } else {
        printf("Grade F\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
cond1 = (hardness > 30);Stores the 0/1 result of the comparison in a variable, so it can be reused without re-checking.
total = cond1 + cond2 + cond3;Adding three 0/1 flags gives exactly how many conditions were true (0 through 3).
if (cond1 && cond2 && cond3) { Grade A }Checked first: catches the all-three-true case before any "any two" branch could wrongly claim it.
else if (cond1 && cond2) { Grade B } ... else if (total == 1) { Grade E }Each subsequent branch only needs to check its own combination, since Grade A already ruled out the all-three case.

Each condition is stored once as a 0/1 flag (cond1, cond2, cond3) so it doesn't need to be re-evaluated repeatedly, and total simply adds the three flags together (a true int adds as 1) to know how many conditions passed overall. Grade A is checked first: since every “any two” combination (B/C/D) is only reached once we already know not all three are true, there's no risk of a three-condition case accidentally being reported as Grade B/C/D.

Print the multiplication table for a user-entered number.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {

Professor's explanation

Assignment 1 — Q5

Find the factorials of every number between n and m (n < m, both user inputs).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, m, i, j;
    long fact;
    printf("Enter n and m (n < m): ");
    scanf("%d %d", &n, &m);

    for (i = n; i <= m; i++) {
        fact = 1;
        for (j = 1; j <= i; j++) fact = fact * j;
        printf("%d! = %ld\n", i, fact);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = n; i <= m; i++) { fact = 1;Resets the accumulator at the start of every outer pass, so each number's factorial is computed independently.
for (j = 1; j <= i; j++) fact = fact * j;Standard factorial accumulation, run fresh for the current i.

The outer loop walks through every number from n to m; for each one, an independent inner loop computes that number's factorial from scratch (fact is reset to 1 at the start of every outer pass, exactly like the nn pattern seen earlier).

Assignment 1 — Q6

Find the sum of the series x/1! − x3/3! + x5/5! − x7/7! + … + xn/n! (x and n are user inputs).

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    float x, sum = 0, term, power, fact;
    int n, k, j, sign;

    printf("Enter x and n: ");
    scanf("%f %d", &x, &n);

    sign = 1;
    for (k = 1; k <= n; k += 2) {
        power = 1;
        for (j = 0; j < k; j++) power = power * x;

        fact = 1;
        for (j = 1; j <= k; j++) fact = fact * j;

        term = power / fact;
        sum = sum + sign * term;
        sign = -sign;
    }

    printf("Sum = %.4f\n", sum);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (k = 1; k <= n; k += 2) {Steps by 2, so k only ever takes the odd values 1, 3, 5, 7, … needed by this series.
power = 1; for (j = 0; j < k; j++) power = power * x;Builds x to the power k by repeated multiplication.
fact = 1; for (j = 1; j <= k; j++) fact = fact * j;Builds k! the same way, independently.
sum = sum + sign * term; sign = -sign;Adds the term with its current sign, then flips the sign for next time — alternates + and - without needing an if.

The exponents involved are only the odd numbers (1, 3, 5, 7, …), so the outer loop steps by 2 (k += 2) instead of 1. sign starts at +1 and is flipped (sign = -sign;) after every term, which is a clean way to alternate + and − without an if. Each term's power and factorial are built exactly as in Task 3 of Experiment 3B.

Assignment 1 — Q7

Print the pattern: for input 5 → A / BA / CBA / DCBA / EDCBA.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int n, i, j;
    printf("Enter n: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        for (j = i - 1; j >= 0; j--) {
            printf("%c", 'A' + j);
        }
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (j = i - 1; j >= 0; j--) {Counts backward from i-1 to 0, so the letters print in descending order.
printf("%c", 'A' + j);Computes each letter's ASCII code directly from j.

Row i needs the letters from the i-th letter down to A, so the inner loop counts j backwards from i-1 to 0 and prints 'A' + j each time. For row 3 (i=3): j goes 2,1,0 → prints C, B, A → “CBA”, matching the sample.

Assignment 1 — Q8 (tracing) 
#include <stdio.h>
int main()
{
    int i = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i < 8; i++)
    {
        if(i == 2) { break; }
        for (int j = 1; j < 3; j++) {
            if (j == 2) continue;
            printf("%d ", i);
        }
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}

Find the output. Use a proper tracing table.

Output:
1
Professor's explanation
Outer ii<8?i==2 → break?Inner jj==2 → continue?Printed
1yesno1no"1 "
1——2yes (skip printf, j++ still runs)(nothing)
1——3 → j<3 fails, inner loop ends—then outer prints "\n"
2yesyes → break——outer loop exits immediately

The outer int i = 1; inside the for creates a new i that shadows the outer int i = 0; declared just above it — that outer i is never used again. On the very first pass (i=1), the inner loop prints 1 once (at j=1), skips printing at j=2 because of continue (note: continue skips the rest of that iteration's body, but j++ in the loop header still runs), and stops once j=3 fails j<3. Then printf("\n") runs, ending that line. On the second outer pass (i=2), if(i==2) is true, so break exits the outer loop immediately — before the inner loop or the trailing printf("\n") for this pass ever run.

Final output is exactly one line: 1 followed by a newline — nothing else prints.

Assignment 1 — Q9 (tracing) 
#include <stdio.h>
int main()
{
    int n = 1729;
    int count = 0, digit;
    while (n > 0)
    {
        digit = n % 10;
        if (digit == 6 && digit == 7) { count++; }
        n = n / 10;
    }
    printf("n=%d, sum=%d, digit=%d", n, count, digit);
    return 0;
}

Find the output. Use a proper tracing table.

Output:
n=0, sum=0, digit=1
Professor's explanation
Passn (before)digit = n%10digit==6 && digit==7 ?n = n/10 (after)
117299false172
21722false17
3177false1
411false0

The condition digit == 6 && digit == 7 can never be true — a single variable cannot simultaneously equal 6 and equal 7. This looks like it might be intended to check "digit is 6 or 7", which would need ||, not && — but as written, count stays 0 forever. The loop still runs to completion, peeling off every digit until n reaches 0 (which is also when the loop stops, so the printed n is 0). The digit printed is whatever it was set to on the last pass (digit=1, from n=1 in the 4th pass) since it's never reset afterwards. Also note the format string mislabels count as sum — that's just a cosmetic label mismatch, not a logic bug; the value printed there is still count.

Condition checkedabResult

Assignment 1 — Q11 (find &amp; fix logical errors)

The code below is expected to evaluate 11 + 22 + 33 + 44 (= 288). Trace it, find every logical error, and fix them (no major restructuring allowed):

#include <stdio.h>
int main()
{
    int n = 3, p, q, i, j;
    q = 0;
    p = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++);
    {
        for (j = 0; j < i; j++);
        {
            q = q + i;
        }
        p = p * q;
    }
    printf("Ans: %d\n", p);
    return 0;
}
#include <stdio.h>
int main()
{
    int n = 4, p, q, i, j;
    p = 0;
    for (i = 1; i <= n; i++)
    {
        q = 1;
        for (j = 0; j < i; j++)
        {
            q = q * i;
        }
        p = p + q;
    }
    printf("Ans: %d\n", p);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation

Every bug found:

  1. for (i = 0; i < n; i++); and for (j = 0; j < i; j++); each end with a stray ;. That semicolon is the loop body (an empty statement) — so the { ... } block written below each loop is NOT actually repeated by the loop at all; it just runs once, after the loop finishes. Fix: delete both semicolons.
  2. n = 3 only produces 3 terms; the target series needs 4 terms (11..44). Fix: n = 4.
  3. The outer loop runs i from 0, but 00 isn't part of the series — we want i to represent the bases 1,2,3,4. Fix: start at i = 1 and use i <= n.
  4. q is only initialised to 0 once, before the outer loop, so it's never reset between bases — but each base's power needs to start fresh. Fix: set q = 1 at the start of every outer pass (moved inside the outer loop, before the inner loop).
  5. The inner loop uses q = q + i; (addition). Repeated addition of i, i times only builds i×i (a square) — it can never build ii for i>2. To raise i to its own power via repeated multiplication, each pass must multiply, not add. Fix: q = q * i;.
  6. p = p * q; multiplies the running total, but p starts at 0 — anything times 0 stays 0 forever, so the answer would always print 0. We want a running sum of the terms. Fix: p = p + q;.
iq resets toq after inner loop (i¹ multiplications)p = p + q
1111 = 10+1 = 1
2122 = 41+4 = 5
3133 = 275+27 = 32
4144 = 25632+256 = 288

Final answer: Ans: 288, matching the target series exactly.

Assignment 2

Assignment 2 — Q1

Using dynamic memory allocation, store N integers. Reverse the array in place by swapping first↔last, second↔second-last, and so on. Then grow the allocation by one element and store 103 in the new last position. Print the final array.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, i, temp;
    printf("Enter N: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *arr = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (arr == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    printf("Enter %d numbers: ", n);
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);

    /* swap first with last, second with second-last, ... */
    for (i = 0; i < n / 2; i++) {
        temp = arr[i];
        arr[i] = arr[n - 1 - i];
        arr[n - 1 - i] = temp;
    }

    /* grow the block by one and store 103 at the new last position */
    arr = (int *)realloc(arr, (n + 1) * sizeof(int));
    if (arr == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }
    arr[n] = 103;
    n = n + 1;

    printf("Modified array: ");
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    printf("\n");

    free(arr);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
for (i = 0; i < n / 2; i++) {Only needs to run half the array's length — each pass handles one mirrored pair.
temp = arr[i]; arr[i] = arr[n-1-i]; arr[n-1-i] = temp;Standard 3-line swap between index i and its mirror n-1-i.
arr = (int *)realloc(arr, (n + 1) * sizeof(int));Grows the block by exactly one element.
arr[n] = 103; n = n + 1;Writes into the first unused slot (arr[n], before n is updated), then updates n so later loops use the new size.

The swap loop only needs to run n/2 times — each pass swaps one mirrored pair (i with n-1-i), and once i reaches the middle, every pair has already been swapped once (this is the exact same two-pointer idea as reversing an array with pointers in Tab 1, just written with array indices). Growing the array is the standard realloc pattern: resize to n+1, write the new value at index n (the first unused slot), then update n itself so every later loop uses the new, correct size.

Assignment 2 — Q2

Given int marks[] = {3, 5, 90, 2, 1};, write a function findIndex that returns the index of a given number in the array, or −1 if it isn't present.

#include <stdio.h>

int findIndex(int arr[], int size, int key) {
    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        if (arr[i] == key) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

int main() {
    int marks[] = {3, 5, 90, 2, 1};
    int num, idx;

    printf("Enter a number to find: ");
    scanf("%d", &num);

    idx = findIndex(marks, 5, num);

    if (idx != -1) {
        printf("The number %d is at index %d.\n", num, idx);
    } else {
        printf("The number %d is not in the array.\n", num);
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int findIndex(int arr[], int size, int key) {Takes the array, its size, and the value being searched for — all as parameters, so the function works for any array/key combination.
if (arr[i] == key) return i;Returns the index immediately on a match, exiting the function on the spot.
return -1;Only reached if the loop finished without ever matching.
idx = findIndex(marks, 5, num);Calls the function with the specific array, its size (5), and the number to search for.

findIndex is a plain linear search wrapped as a reusable function: it returns the index the instant it finds a match (exiting the function immediately, no break needed), and falls through to return -1; only if the loop finishes without ever matching. Searching for 90 returns index 2; searching for 7 (absent from the array) returns -1, matching the expected samples.

Assignment 2 — Q3

Design a Course struct (course code, credit hours, attendance, assignment, 5 quiz marks, midterm, final exam). Using functions, keep only the best 3 of 5 quizzes, compute each course's total mark and GPA, then compute overall CGPA across all courses entered.

This question is explicitly harder than the final exam standard your faculty described (lab-manual / struct-sheet / pointer-tracing level). It's solved here in full for completeness since it was assigned, but the 14-mark final exam structure question is expected to look like the simpler array-of-structs pattern in Tab 1 §10 and the struct worked example further down this tab — not this multi-course CGPA pipeline.
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char code[20];
    float credit;
    float gpa;
    float attendance;   /* out of 10 */
    float assignment;   /* out of 20 */
    float quizzes[5];   /* each out of 20 */
    float midterm;      /* out of 20 */
    float finalExam;    /* out of 30 */
    float total;        /* out of 100 */
} Course;

/* sum of the best 3 of 5 quizzes, converted to a mark out of 20 */
float bestThreeQuizzes(float q[5]) {
    float sorted[5];
    int i, j, minIdx;
    float temp;

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) sorted[i] = q[i];

    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        minIdx = i;
        for (j = i + 1; j < 5; j++) {
            if (sorted[j] < sorted[minIdx]) minIdx = j;
        }
        temp = sorted[i]; sorted[i] = sorted[minIdx]; sorted[minIdx] = temp;
    }

    float bestSum = sorted[2] + sorted[3] + sorted[4];   /* three largest, out of 60 */
    return (bestSum / 60.0) * 20.0;                      /* scaled to out of 20 */
}

float calcGPA(float marks) {   /* marks out of 100 */
    if (marks >= 80) return 4.00;
    else if (marks >= 75) return 3.70;
    else if (marks >= 70) return 3.30;
    else if (marks >= 65) return 3.00;
    else if (marks >= 60) return 2.70;
    else if (marks >= 55) return 2.30;
    else if (marks >= 50) return 2.00;
    else if (marks >= 45) return 1.70;
    else if (marks >= 40) return 1.30;
    else return 0.00;
}

int main() {
    int n, i, j;
    printf("Enter number of courses: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    Course courses[20];   /* assumes n <= 20 */

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("\nCourse %d code: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%s", courses[i].code);
        printf("Credit hours: ");
        scanf("%f", &courses[i].credit);
        printf("Attendance (/10): ");
        scanf("%f", &courses[i].attendance);
        printf("Assignment (/20): ");
        scanf("%f", &courses[i].assignment);
        printf("5 quiz marks (/20 each): ");
        for (j = 0; j < 5; j++) scanf("%f", &courses[i].quizzes[j]);
        printf("Midterm (/20): ");
        scanf("%f", &courses[i].midterm);
        printf("Final exam (/30): ");
        scanf("%f", &courses[i].finalExam);
    }

    float totalPoints = 0, totalCredits = 0, earnedCredits = 0;

    printf("\n");
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        float quizMark = bestThreeQuizzes(courses[i].quizzes);
        courses[i].total = courses[i].attendance + courses[i].assignment +
                            quizMark + courses[i].midterm + courses[i].finalExam;
        courses[i].gpa = calcGPA(courses[i].total);

        printf("%s - %.1f credits - GPA %.2f\n", courses[i].code, courses[i].credit, courses[i].gpa);

        totalPoints += courses[i].gpa * courses[i].credit;
        totalCredits += courses[i].credit;
        if (courses[i].gpa > 0.00) earnedCredits += courses[i].credit;
    }

    float cgpa = totalPoints / totalCredits;
    printf("================================\n");
    printf("Earned: %.1f credits, CGPA: %.2f\n", earnedCredits, cgpa);

    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float sorted[5]; for (i=0;i<5;i++) sorted[i] = q[i];Copies the 5 quiz marks into a scratch array first, so sorting doesn't disturb the original data in the struct.
selection sort ascending on sorted[]Same selection-sort pattern as Tab 1 — finds the smallest remaining value each pass and swaps it into place.
float bestSum = sorted[2] + sorted[3] + sorted[4];After ascending sort, the 3 largest values sit in the last 3 slots.
return (bestSum / 60.0) * 20.0;Rescales from “out of 60” (3 quizzes × 20 each) down to “out of 20”.
float quizMark = bestThreeQuizzes(courses[i].quizzes);Calls the helper function, passing this course's quiz array.
courses[i].total = attendance + assignment + quizMark + midterm + finalExam;Sums every graded component into the course's overall mark out of 100.
totalPoints += courses[i].gpa * courses[i].credit; totalCredits += courses[i].credit;Accumulates credit-weighted grade points and total credits, across every course, for the final CGPA calculation.

bestThreeQuizzes copies the 5 marks into a scratch array so the original data isn't disturbed, selection-sorts that copy ascending, then adds the last three (the three largest) and rescales from “out of 60” to “out of 20”. calcGPA is a straightforward if/else-if ladder identical in spirit to the grade calculator from Tab 1. main then loops over every course, calls both helper functions, and accumulates a credit-weighted CGPA (∑ gpa×credit / ∑ credit) exactly the way a real transcript is computed.

Assignment 2 — Q4 (tracing: globals, pointers &amp; functions)

Trace the program below and give its complete output. Pay close attention to which x and which total each line actually refers to.

#include <stdio.h>
int x = 3;
int total = 0;

void modify(int *p, int x) {
    int temp = *p;
    int total = 5;
    *p = *p + x;
    x = x + 1;
    total = total + *p;
    printf("%d,%d,%d\n", x, *p, total);
    *p = temp;
}

int process(int x) {
    int y = x;
    y = y + 2;
    return y;
}

void main() {
    int i;
    int x = 10;
    int y = 4;
    int *ptr = &y;

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        if (i == 2 || i == 4) {
            total = total + x;
        }
        y = y + i;
        modify(ptr, x);
        x = process(x);
        if (i % 2 == 1) {
            *ptr = *ptr + total;
        }
        printf("%d:%d:%d:%d\n", i, x, y, total);
    }
    printf("Final values:\n");
    printf("%d:%d\n", x, y);
    printf("total = %d\n", total);
}
#include <stdio.h>
int x = 3;
int total = 0;

void modify(int *p, int x) {
    int temp = *p;
    int total = 5;
    *p = *p + x;
    x = x + 1;
    total = total + *p;
    printf("%d,%d,%d\n", x, *p, total);
    *p = temp;
}

int process(int x) {
    int y = x;
    y = y + 2;
    return y;
}

void main() {
    int i;
    int x = 10;
    int y = 4;
    int *ptr = &y;

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        if (i == 2 || i == 4) {
            total = total + x;
        }
        y = y + i;
        modify(ptr, x);
        x = process(x);
        if (i % 2 == 1) {
            *ptr = *ptr + total;
        }
        printf("%d:%d:%d:%d\n", i, x, y, total);
    }
    printf("Final values:\n");
    printf("%d:%d\n", x, y);
    printf("total = %d\n", total);
}
Professor's explanation

Scoping first — this is the whole puzzle:

  • There is a global x (=3) and a global total (=0).
  • main declares its own local x (=10) and y (=4) — from here on, every plain x inside main refers to this local copy, never the global. main does not declare its own total, so every total inside main IS the global one.
  • modify(int *p, int x) has its own parameter x (a fresh copy every call) and its own local total = 5 (reset to 5 every call). Neither ever touches main's x/total or the globals.
  • process(int x) has yet another separate parameter x, and its own local y — completely unrelated to any other x or y in the program.
  • ptr always points at main's y and never changes where it points, only what value sits there.

Because every function that mentions x has its own local/parameter named x, the global x = 3 is never actually read or printed anywhere — it's a decoy. The global total, by contrast, genuinely accumulates across iterations because only main touches it directly (by name), and main has no local shadowing it.

itotal after if(i==2||i==4)y after y+=iInside modify() printsx after process()i%2==1 → *ptr+=total?y at end of iterMain prints
00 (unchanged)411,14,1912no4 (modify restores it)0:12:4:0
10 (unchanged)513,17,2214yes, but total=0 so y unchanged51:14:5:0
214 (0 + x=14 at that point)715,21,2616no72:16:7:14
314 (unchanged)1017,26,3118yes: y = 10+14 = 24243:18:24:14
432 (14 + x=18 at that point)2819,46,5120no284:20:28:32

Key mechanics per iteration: inside modify, *p is temporarily changed (*p = *p + x) so its local printf sees the bumped-up value — but the very last line, *p = temp;, always restores y back to what it was before the call. So modify never permanently changes y by itself; the only lines in main that permanently change y are y = y + i; and the conditional *ptr = *ptr + total; when i is odd.

Full program output, in order:

11,14,19
0:12:4:0
13,17,22
1:14:5:0
15,21,26
2:16:7:14
17,26,31
3:18:24:14
19,46,51
4:20:28:32
Final values:
20:28
total = 32

Struct question — final-exam standard worked example

Struct worked example (this is the final-exam standard)

Develop a struct named Student that stores Student ID, Student Name, and Credits Completed. Given the records:

IDNameCredits
1234ABC32.0
5678DEF35.5
9101GHI36.0
1213IJK33.0

(1) Store this data using an array of structures. (2) Identify and print the ID and Name of every student who has completed more than 35 credits and whose 3rd digit from the last of the ID (the hundreds digit) is even.

#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[20];
    float credits;
} Student;

int main() {
    Student students[4] = {
        {1234, "ABC", 32.0},
        {5678, "DEF", 35.5},
        {9101, "GHI", 36.0},
        {1213, "IJK", 33.0}
    };
    int i;

    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        int hundredsDigit = (students[i].id / 100) % 10;
        if (students[i].credits > 35 && hundredsDigit % 2 == 0) {
            printf("ID: %d, Name: %s\n", students[i].id, students[i].name);
        }
    }
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
Student students[4] = { {1234,"ABC",32.0}, ... };Declares and initialises the whole array of given records in one statement.
int hundredsDigit = (students[i].id / 100) % 10;Extracts the hundreds digit of this record's ID: dividing by 100 drops the last two digits, then % 10 keeps only the new last digit.
if (students[i].credits > 35 && hundredsDigit % 2 == 0) {Both conditions checked together: enough credits and an even hundreds digit.
printf("ID: %d, Name: %s\n", students[i].id, students[i].name);Only reached for qualifying records; prints just the two requested fields.

This is the exact shape a 14-mark structure question takes: an array of structs holding fixed given data, then one loop that checks each record against a compound condition. (students[i].id / 100) % 10 extracts the hundreds digit of any number: dividing by 100 removes the last two digits, and % 10 then keeps only the last digit of what's left — for 5678, 5678/100 = 56, 56 % 10 = 6.

IDCredits > 35?Hundreds digitEven?Qualifies?
123432.0 → no2yesNo (fails credits)
567835.5 → yes6yesYes
910136.0 → yes1noNo (fails digit parity)
121333.0 → no1noNo

Only ID 5678, DEF satisfies both conditions at once, so that's the program's only line of output: ID: 5678, Name: DEF.

Pointer tracing — final-exam standard worked examples

Pointer tracing — Program 1 
#include 
int main()
{
    int x = 5;
    int y = 10;
    int *p = &x;

    x = x + 2;
    *p = *p + y;
    p = &y;
    *p = *p - 3;
    x = x + *p;

    printf("%d %d\n", x, y);
    return 0;
}

Find the output.

Output:
24 7
Professor's explanation
LineWhat happensxyp points to
int x=5; int y=10;init510—
int *p = &x;p now holds x's address510x
x = x + 2;ordinary update via the name x710x
*p = *p + y;*p means x (p still points to x): x = x + y = 7+101710x
p = &y;p is redirected to point at y instead1710y
*p = *p - 3;*p now means y: y = y - 3 = 10-3177y
x = x + *p;*p still means y (=7): x = 17 + 7247y

The trap: p is reassigned partway through (p = &y;), so every *p after that line means “y”, not “x” — even though the variable is still called p. Final output: 24 7.

Pointer tracing — Program 2 
#include 
void changeValue(int a) {
    a = a + 5;
    printf("Inside changeValue: %d\n", a);
}
void changeReference(int *b) {
    *b = *b + 5;
    printf("Inside changeReference: %d\n", *b);
}
int main() {
    int x = 10;
    changeValue(x);
    printf("After changeValue: %d\n", x);
    changeReference(&x);
    printf("After changeReference: %d\n", x);
    return 0;
}

Find the complete output, and explain why the two functions behave differently.

Output:
Inside changeValue: 15
After changeValue: 10
Inside changeReference: 15
After changeReference: 15
Professor's explanation

changeValue(int a) receives a copy of x (pass-by-value). Changing a inside the function only changes that local copy — x back in main is completely untouched, which is why "After changeValue" still prints 10.

changeReference(int *b) instead receives x's address. *b = *b + 5; writes directly into x's own memory — there is no copy involved — so the change is permanent and visible back in main, which is why "After changeReference" prints 15.

This pair is the cleanest possible illustration of pass-by-value vs pass-by-pointer, and is exactly the kind of contrast final exam tracing questions like to test.

Pointer tracing — Program 3 
#include 
int main() {
    int a = 2;
    int b = 4;
    int c = 6;
    int *p = &a;

    *p = *p + 3;
    p = &b;
    *p = *p + a;
    p = &c;
    c = *p + b;
    *p = a + b + c;

    printf("%d %d %d\n", a, b, c);
    return 0;
}

Find the output. Watch the order of evaluation carefully on the last two lines.

Output:
5 9 29
Professor's explanation
Linep points toabc
init—246
int *p = &a;a246
*p = *p + 3;a546
p = &b;b546
*p = *p + a; (b = b + a = 4+5)b596
p = &c;c596
c = *p + b; (RHS uses OLD c=6, plus b=9, before c changes)c5915
*p = a + b + c; (RHS uses the just-updated c=15)c5929

The trap is in the last two lines. c = *p + b; reads *p (which is c, since p now points to it) before this same statement overwrites c — C always fully evaluates the right-hand side using the old values first, then assigns. So it uses the old c = 6, giving c = 6 + 9 = 15. The very next line, *p = a + b + c;, now sees c as its new value, 15 (statements run strictly in order, so by this point the update has already happened): c = 5 + 9 + 15 = 29. Final output: 5 9 29.

Every problem below is solved in full, with a professor's every-line breakdown (including plain syntax, not just the "interesting" lines) AND an improved "Claude explains" section that teaches the general pattern behind the question — what it's really testing, what variations the real exam might swap in, and how to approach anything in this family from scratch. Part A — Structures

A1. Student registration — department code from the middle digits [12]

A registration number is an 8-digit integer: the first two digits are the admission year, the next three digits are the department code, and the last three digits are the roll serial (e.g. 22101045 → admitted 2022, department 101, serial 045).

Store the given records using an array of structures. Take a department code D as input. Print the name and CGPA of every student in department D. Print how many of those students were admitted before 2023.

NameReg. numberCGPA
Nabila Haque221010453.71
Tanvir Ahmed231021873.25
Sadia Islam211013023.88
Rakib Hasan231032112.95
Mahin Chowdhury221011503.40
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int regNum;
    float cgpa;
} Student;

int main() {
    Student students[5] = {
        {"Nabila Haque", 22101045, 3.71},
        {"Tanvir Ahmed", 23102187, 3.25},
        {"Sadia Islam", 21101302, 3.88},
        {"Rakib Hasan", 23103211, 2.95},
        {"Mahin Chowdhury", 22101150, 3.40}
    };
    int d, i, found = 0, before2023 = 0;

    printf("Enter department code: ");
    scanf("%d", &d);

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        int year = 2000 + students[i].regNum / 1000000;
        int dept = (students[i].regNum / 1000) % 1000;

        if (dept == d) {
            printf("%s CGPA: %.2f (admitted in %d)\n", students[i].name, students[i].cgpa, year);
            found++;
            if (year < 2023) before2023++;
        }
    }

    printf("Students found: %d\n", found);
    printf("Admitted before 2023: %d\n", before2023);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>Brings in the standard I/O library so printf/scanf are available to use.
typedef struct {Begins defining a new structure type. The typedef keyword means the name given at the very end (Student) can be used afterward exactly like a built-in type — no need to keep writing struct Student.
char name[30];A character-array member, 30 bytes wide — enough room for any student's name plus its invisible null terminator.
int regNum;The 8-digit registration number, stored as a plain int so it can later be sliced apart digit-by-digit with / and %.
float cgpa;The CGPA, stored as a decimal (float) since GPAs like 3.71 have a fractional part.
} Student;Closes the structure definition. From this line onward, Student is a real, usable type name.
int main() {Every C program's execution begins at main(); the opening brace starts the function body.
Student students[5] = { {"Nabila Haque", 22101045, 3.71}, {"Tanvir Ahmed", 23102187, 3.25}, {"Sadia Islam", 21101302, 3.88}, {"Rakib Hasan", 23103211, 2.95}, {"Mahin Chowdhury", 22101150, 3.40} };Declares an array of 5 Student structs and initialises all five in one statement. Each {...} is one complete record; the values inside are matched to the struct's members strictly in the order they were declared (name, then regNum, then cgpa).
int d, i, found = 0, before2023 = 0;Four int variables declared together: d will hold the user's input, i is the loop counter, and the two counters are explicitly started at 0 so they only ever count what the loop actually finds.
printf("Enter department code: ");Prints a prompt with no format specifiers — just displays this exact text so the user knows what to type.
scanf("%d", &d);Reads one integer typed by the user and stores it into d. The & is required here because scanf needs the address of the variable to write into, not its value.
for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {The loop header, read as three parts: start i at 0; keep looping only while i < 5 (there are exactly 5 students); after every pass, increase i by 1.
int year = 2000 + students[i].regNum / 1000000;First 2 digits of the 8-digit regNum → dividing by 1,000,000 pushes the remaining 6 digits off the right end, leaving the 2-digit year; adding 2000 turns "22" into the real year 2022.
int dept = (students[i].regNum / 1000) % 1000;The 3 middle digits: dividing by 1000 first drops the 3 unwanted digits on the right, then % 1000 keeps only the 3 digits that remain.
if (dept == d) {Only students whose extracted department code matches the department the user typed in (d) enter this block at all.
printf("%s CGPA: %.2f (admitted in %d)\n", students[i].name, students[i].cgpa, year);Three format specifiers, three arguments, matched left to right: %s prints the name string, %.2f prints the CGPA rounded to exactly 2 decimal places, %d prints the year computed two lines above.
found++;Adds 1 to the running count of matching students — runs once for every student that passed the if above.
if (year < 2023) before2023++;A second, independent test nested inside the first: this counter only increases for students who both matched the department code and were admitted before 2023.
printf("Students found: %d\n", found);Printed once, after the loop has completely finished, using the final accumulated value of found.
printf("Admitted before 2023: %d\n", before2023);Same idea, for the second running counter.
return 0;Tells the operating system the program finished without error — the standard, expected way to end main.
Worked example — watch it happen with real numbers

Take Nabila Haque's record: regNum = 22101045.

What we're findingThe exact expressionWhat integer division/modulo actually does hereValue produced
Extract year22101045 / 1000000Integer division truncates: 22101045 ÷ 1000000 = 22.101045 → kept as 22 (the .101045 is thrown away, not rounded)22 → year = 2000+22 = 2022
Extract dept (step 1)22101045 / 1000Pushes the last 3 digits off: 22101045 ÷ 1000 = 22101.045 → kept as 2210122101
Extract dept (step 2)22101 % 1000Keeps only the remainder after dividing by 1000, i.e. the last 3 digits of 22101101

So for this one record: year = 2022, dept = 101. Since the user typed department 101, this record passes the filter and prints Nabila Haque CGPA: 3.71 (admitted in 2022) — exactly matching the sample run. Try the same two steps on Tanvir Ahmed's regNum = 23102187 yourself: you should get year = 2023, dept = 102 — which is why he does not appear when the user enters department 101.

This is the flagship pattern of the whole practice sheet: an 8-digit code that secretly packs 3 separate fields (year, department, serial) using fixed digit widths. Extracting any middle slice of digits always uses the same two-step recipe: divide to push the unwanted digits on the right off the end, then take % (10 to the power of the field's width) to strip off whatever's left on the "left" side too.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is a digit-split-and-filter question: one struct member is a multi-field integer code, and the whole exercise is (1) correctly slicing that code into its named parts, then (2) using one or more of those parts as a filter condition while looping over the array. The CGPA/name here are just along for the ride once you've cracked the code. The reason this is the flagship question of the whole sheet is that A2–A4 are all variations on exactly this same skeleton — master this one and the rest of Part A becomes much faster.
Likely variations on the exam Expect the exact same shape with a different code layout: different digit widths (e.g. 3+2+3 instead of 2+3+3), a different number of fields (4 fields packed into a 10-digit code), or the filter criterion moved to a different field (filter by roll serial range instead of department). The two derived counts at the end ("how many were admitted before/after some year", "how many scored above some CGPA") are also an easy slot for the examiner to swap — always re-read exactly which field the second count applies to. Also watch for the filter being changed from an equality test (dept == d) to a range test (dept >= d1 && dept <= d2).
How to approach it First write out on paper which digit positions belong to which field and how many digits wide each one is (draw it like YY DDD SSS). Convert each field's width to its own power of 10, then apply (number / rightWidth) % fieldWidthAsPowerOf10 for any middle field, or just number / 10ⁿ for a field flush against the left end, or number % 10ⁿ for a field flush against the right end. Once every field is extracted into its own named variable, the rest is an ordinary array-of-structs filter-and-count loop — never inline the digit arithmetic directly inside the if condition, since a named variable is both easier to trace and easier to re-use for a second condition.
A2. Medicine batch — expiry from the year and month digits [12]

A batch number is a 6-digit integer: first two digits manufacturing year, next two digits manufacturing month, last two digits a serial (e.g. 240718 → made July 2024). Every medicine expires 24 months after manufacture.

Store the records, take the current year and month as input (for September 2026, enter 26 9), print the name/manufacturing date/age in months of every expired medicine, then print how many expired and the total price of the expired stock.

NameBatch numberPrice
Napa Extra2407182.50
Seclo 202305128.00
Monas 1025092618.00
Fexo 1202211059.50
Maxpro 202412037.00
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int batchNum;
    float price;
} Medicine;

int main() {
    Medicine meds[5] = {
        {"Napa Extra", 240718, 2.50},
        {"Seclo 20", 230512, 8.00},
        {"Monas 10", 250926, 18.00},
        {"Fexo 120", 221105, 9.50},
        {"Maxpro 20", 241203, 7.00}
    };
    int curY, curM, i, expiredCount = 0;
    float valueLost = 0;

    printf("Enter current year and month: ");
    scanf("%d %d", &curY, &curM);

    printf("Expired medicines:\n");
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        int mfgYY = meds[i].batchNum / 10000;
        int mfgMM = (meds[i].batchNum / 100) % 100;
        int ageMonths = (curY * 12 + curM) - (mfgYY * 12 + mfgMM);

        if (ageMonths >= 24) {
            printf("%s made 20%02d-%02d age %d months price %.2f\n",
                   meds[i].name, mfgYY, mfgMM, ageMonths, meds[i].price);
            expiredCount++;
            valueLost += meds[i].price;
        }
    }

    printf("Total expired: %d\n", expiredCount);
    printf("Value lost : %.2f Taka\n", valueLost);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>Loads the I/O library for printf/scanf.
typedef struct {Starts a new struct type definition, named at the end via typedef.
char name[30];Holds the medicine's name as a character array.
int batchNum;The 6-digit batch code, kept as a plain integer so it can be digit-sliced.
float price;The unit price, stored as a decimal.
} Medicine;Closes the definition; Medicine is now a usable type.
int main() {Execution starts here.
Medicine meds[5] = { {"Napa Extra", 240718, 2.50}, {"Seclo 20", 230512, 8.00}, {"Monas 10", 250926, 18.00}, {"Fexo 120", 221105, 9.50}, {"Maxpro 20", 241203, 7.00} };Declares and fills an array of 5 Medicine records in one statement, each {...} matching name/batchNum/price in that order.
int curY, curM, i, expiredCount = 0;curY/curM will hold the user's current year/month, i is the loop counter, and expiredCount starts at 0 so it only counts what's actually found.
float valueLost = 0;A running total of the price of every expired medicine, started at 0 before any medicine has been added to it.
printf("Enter current year and month: ");Prompts the user, no format specifiers needed since nothing is being printed back yet.
scanf("%d %d", &curY, &curM);Reads two integers separated by whitespace in one call — the two %ds correspond to the two &-addressed variables, in order.
printf("Expired medicines:\n");A one-time header line, printed before the loop starts, so the list of expired medicines that follows has a heading.
for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {Loop over all 5 medicines: start at 0, run while i<5, advance by 1 each pass.
int mfgYY = meds[i].batchNum / 10000;First 2 digits of the 6-digit batch code → dividing by 10,000 pushes the remaining 4 digits off the right.
int mfgMM = (meds[i].batchNum / 100) % 100;Middle 2 digits: divide by 100 to drop the last 2 (the serial), then % 100 keeps only the 2 digits left after that.
