int ageMonths = (curY * 12 + curM) - (mfgYY * 12 + mfgMM);Converts both the current (year,month) and the manufacture (year,month) into a single "total months" count, then subtracts — this one line replaces what would otherwise be a messy multi-branch year/month subtraction.
if (ageMonths >= 24) {"Expires 24 months after manufacture" means expired the instant 24 full months have passed, so the boundary test is >=, not >.
printf("%s made 20%02d-%02d age %d months price %.2f\n", meds[i].name, mfgYY, mfgMM, ageMonths, meds[i].price);Five values, five specifiers, matched in order. %02d pads a number to at least 2 digits with a leading zero (so month 9 prints as 09) — that's why 20 is written literally right before it, to build "2024" out of the literal "20" plus the 2-digit mfgYY.
expiredCount++;Bumps the count of expired medicines by 1, only for medicines that entered the if block above.
valueLost += meds[i].price;Adds this medicine's price onto the running total — only reached for medicines already confirmed expired.
printf("Total expired: %d\n", expiredCount);Printed once, after the loop, with the final tally.
printf("Value lost : %.2f Taka\n", valueLost);Printed once, with the final accumulated price total, rounded to 2 decimals.
return 0;Normal, successful program end.
Worked example — watch it happen with real numbers

Take Napa Extra's record: batchNum = 240718, with the user entering 26 9 (September 2026) as the current date.

What we're findingThe exact expressionWhat integer division/modulo actually does hereValue produced
Extract mfgYY240718 / 10000240718 ÷ 10000 = 24.0718 → kept as 2424 (year 2024)
Extract mfgMM (step 1)240718 / 100Pushes the last 2 digits (the serial) off: 240718 ÷ 100 = 2407.18 → kept as 24072407
Extract mfgMM (step 2)2407 % 100Keeps only the last 2 digits of 24077 (month 07, July)
Compute ageMonths(26×12+9) − (24×12+7)Converts both dates to total months first: current = 312+9 = 321; manufactured = 288+7 = 295321 − 295 = 26

So this medicine was made in 2024-07 and is now 26 months old. Since 26 >= 24, it counts as expired — exactly matching the sample run's Napa Extra made 2024-07 age 26 months price 2.50. Notice %02d is what turns the plain number 7 into the padded text 07 in the printed output.

The clever bit here is turning two separate (year, month) pairs into a single comparable number of months: year * 12 + month converts any (year,month) pair into "total months since year 0", so subtracting two such totals directly gives the number of months between them — no need to handle year-rollovers (like "March minus November") by hand at all.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is a digit-split-into-a-date question, one level harder than A1 because the two extracted fields (year, month) then feed into a small arithmetic formula (age calculation) before the filter condition can even be checked. It's testing whether you can chain two skills together: digit extraction, followed by a derived calculation, followed by a filter — three stages instead of A1's two.
Likely variations on the exam Age in days instead of months (would need day digits too, and a rougher days-per-month approximation or a lookup table), a warranty/expiry threshold that isn't a round number, or being asked for the opposite filter (medicines that are still safe, or expiring within the next N months, which would need a second, tighter < test in addition to the >=24 one). Also watch for the %02d zero-padding trick being tested on its own as a small formatting question.
How to approach it Whenever a problem gives you two components of a date (or any two-part quantity like hours+minutes) and asks you to compare or subtract them, convert both to a single linear count first (here, total months) using bigUnit * conversionFactor + smallUnit. Do this for both dates, then a plain subtraction gives the true difference with no special-case logic for rollovers. Extract the fields first into named variables exactly as in A1, and only then plug them into the age formula — don't try to combine extraction and the age formula into one giant expression.
A3. Employee code — bonus from the last digit [12]

An employee code is a 6-digit integer: first two digits are the joining year, last digit is a grade (1–5). Yearly bonus = grade × 5% of salary.

Store the records, take a year Y as input (for 2020, enter 20), print name/joining year/grade/bonus of every employee who joined before Y, then print the name of the employee with the highest bonus among them.

NameEmployee codeSalary
Imran Kabir19045345000
Farhana Yasmin21078138000
Shuvo Das17029462000
Nusrat Jahan22061541000
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int code;
    float salary;
} Employee;

int main() {
    Employee emps[4] = {
        {"Imran Kabir", 190453, 45000},
        {"Farhana Yasmin", 210781, 38000},
        {"Shuvo Das", 170294, 62000},
        {"Nusrat Jahan", 220615, 41000}
    };
    int Y, i;
    float maxBonus = -1;
    char maxName[30] = "";

    printf("Enter year Y: ");
    scanf("%d", &Y);

    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        int joinYear = emps[i].code / 10000;
        int grade = emps[i].code % 10;

        if (joinYear < Y) {
            float bonus = grade * 0.05 * emps[i].salary;
            printf("%s joined %d grade %d bonus %.2f\n",
                   emps[i].name, 2000 + joinYear, grade, bonus);

            if (bonus > maxBonus) {
                maxBonus = bonus;
                int k = 0;
                while (emps[i].name[k] != '\0') { maxName[k] = emps[i].name[k]; k++; }
                maxName[k] = '\0';
            }
        }
    }

    printf("Highest bonus: %s (%.2f Taka)\n", maxName, maxBonus);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library for printf/scanf.
typedef struct {Begins the struct definition.
char name[30];Employee's name.
int code;The 6-digit employee code, as a plain int for digit-slicing.
float salary;Monthly salary, as a decimal.
} Employee;Closes the type; Employee is now usable.
int main() {Execution starts here.
Employee emps[4] = { {"Imran Kabir", 190453, 45000}, {"Farhana Yasmin", 210781, 38000}, {"Shuvo Das", 170294, 62000}, {"Nusrat Jahan", 220615, 41000} };Declares and fills an array of 4 Employee records in one statement.
int Y, i;Y will hold the user's input year, i is the loop counter.
float maxBonus = -1;Tracks the highest bonus seen so far. Started at -1, not 0, because a real bonus (grade×5%×salary) is always positive, so any first real bonus is guaranteed to beat -1 and become the initial "best".
char maxName[30] = "";Will hold a manual copy of whichever employee currently has the highest bonus; started as an empty string.
printf("Enter year Y: ");Prompts for input.
scanf("%d", &Y);Reads the year threshold typed by the user into Y.
for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {Loop over all 4 employees.
int joinYear = emps[i].code / 10000;First 2 digits of the 6-digit code → divide by 10,000 to push the remaining 4 digits off.
int grade = emps[i].code % 10;The very last digit of any number is always just % 10 — nothing needs to be pushed away first since there's nothing to its right.
if (joinYear < Y) {Only employees who joined strictly before the input year enter this block. Both joinYear and Y are compared as their raw 2-digit form (19 < 20) — there's no need to add 2000 to either side just to compare them.
float bonus = grade * 0.05 * emps[i].salary;Applies the formula directly: grade × 5% × salary. Because 0.05 is a float literal, the whole expression is computed in floating point even though grade is an int.
printf("%s joined %d grade %d bonus %.2f\n", emps[i].name, 2000 + joinYear, grade, bonus);2000 is added to joinYear only here, for display — this is the one place the full 4-digit year is actually needed.
if (bonus > maxBonus) {Compares this employee's bonus against the best one recorded so far; only employees who beat the current record enter this inner block.
maxBonus = bonus;Updates the record to this new, higher bonus.
int k = 0;A fresh index used only for the manual string-copy loop that follows, reset to 0 for every new "best" found.
while (emps[i].name[k] != '\0') { maxName[k] = emps[i].name[k]; k++; }Manually copies this employee's name into maxName one character at a time, since C cannot assign one whole char array to another with = — this is the course's hand-rolled replacement for strcpy.
maxName[k] = '\0';Terminates the freshly-copied name with a null character, exactly one position past the last character copied — without this, maxName would not be a valid string.
printf("Highest bonus: %s (%.2f Taka)\n", maxName, maxBonus);Printed once, after the loop, using whichever name/bonus pair ended up being the final recorded best.
return 0;Normal program end.
Worked example — watch it happen with real numbers

Take Imran Kabir's record: code = 190453.

What we're findingThe exact expressionWhy it worksValue produced
Extract joinYear190453 / 10000190453 ÷ 10000 = 19.0453 → kept as 1919 (year 2019)
Extract grade190453 % 10The remainder after dividing by 10 is always just the last digit — no division step needed first, since nothing sits to its right3
Compute bonus3 × 0.05 × 45000grade × 5% × salary6750.00

So Imran joined in 2019 with grade 3, earning a bonus of 6750.00 — exactly matching the sample run's Imran Kabir joined 2019 grade 3 bonus 6750.00. Since he joined before the example input year 20 (2020), he passes the filter joinYear < Y (19 < 20).

Two digit-extraction tricks in one code: the year uses the "divide by the width of everything to its right" rule exactly like A1/A2, but the grade uses the simplest possible extraction — a field flush against the very last digit needs only % 10, no division at all.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This combines a digit-split filter (like A1) with a "track-the-best-record-seen-so-far" pattern — but this time the thing being tracked is a computed value (bonus), not a stored member, and the "winner" is a name (a string), which forces a manual string copy since you can't just say maxName = emps[i].name; in C. It's really testing three things at once: digit extraction, running-maximum tracking, and manual string copying — the same three ideas that keep reappearing throughout the sheet.
Likely variations on the exam The grade/multiplier relationship swapped (e.g. a lookup table of 5 different fixed percentages instead of a clean grade×5% formula), the comparison direction reversed (joined after Y instead of before), or the "find the best" requirement dropped/added. Also watch for the field widths changing — if the grade were the first digit instead of the last, you'd need code / 100000 instead of % 10. The maxBonus = -1 starting trick is also worth remembering on its own: any "find the maximum" loop needs a starting value guaranteed to lose to the very first real candidate.
How to approach it Extract every field first (never inline the digit math into a condition directly — store each field in its own clearly-named variable). Then handle the filter and the running "best so far" tracker as two completely separate concerns inside the same loop: the filter decides whether a record is even eligible, and only eligible records get compared against the current best. If the best value carries an associated string field, copy it manually with a while loop the moment a new best is found — and always remember the trailing '\0' after the copy.
A4. Bank account — validating a check digit [12]

The last digit of an 8-digit account number is a check digit: valid when it equals the sum of the first 7 digits, modulo 10 (e.g. 12345678 is valid because 1+2+3+4+5+6+7=28, 28%10=8).

Store the records, print every invalid account (showing expected vs. actual check digit), then print the invalid count and the total balance held in the valid accounts.

NameAccount numberBalance
Rafiul Islam1234567852000.00
Sumaiya Akter2043918378500.50
Jahid Hasan5060708613400.75
Mitu Rani9988776496000.00
Arman Chowdhury3141592045250.25
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int accNum;
    float balance;
} Account;

int main() {
    Account accs[5] = {
        {"Rafiul Islam", 12345678, 52000.00},
        {"Sumaiya Akter", 20439183, 78500.50},
        {"Jahid Hasan", 50607086, 13400.75},
        {"Mitu Rani", 99887764, 96000.00},
        {"Arman Chowdhury", 31415920, 45250.25}
    };
    int i, invalidCount = 0;
    float validBalance = 0;

    printf("Invalid accounts:\n");
    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        int n = accs[i].accNum;
        int lastDigit = n % 10;
        int temp = n, sumAll = 0;

        while (temp > 0) {
            sumAll += temp % 10;
            temp = temp / 10;
        }
        int sumFirst7 = sumAll - lastDigit;
        int expected = sumFirst7 % 10;

        if (expected != lastDigit) {
            printf("%s %d (expected check digit %d, found %d)\n",
                   accs[i].name, accs[i].accNum, expected, lastDigit);
            invalidCount++;
        } else {
            validBalance += accs[i].balance;
        }
    }

    printf("Invalid count : %d\n", invalidCount);
    printf("Valid balance : %.2f Taka\n", validBalance);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
typedef struct {Struct definition begins.
char name[30];Account holder's name.
int accNum;The 8-digit account number, as an int.
float balance;Account balance, as a decimal.
} Account;Closes the type; Account is now usable.
int main() {Execution starts here.
Account accs[5] = { {"Rafiul Islam", 12345678, 52000.00}, {"Sumaiya Akter", 20439183, 78500.50}, {"Jahid Hasan", 50607086, 13400.75}, {"Mitu Rani", 99887764, 96000.00}, {"Arman Chowdhury", 31415920, 45250.25} };Declares and fills the array of 5 Account records in one statement.
int i, invalidCount = 0;Loop counter, and a tally started at 0.
float validBalance = 0;Running total of balances held in valid accounts only, started at 0.
printf("Invalid accounts:\n");One-time header printed before the loop, so the list that follows has a heading.
for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {Loop over all 5 accounts.
int n = accs[i].accNum;Copies this account's number into a plain local variable n, purely so the digit-peeling below can freely modify it without touching the original struct member.
int lastDigit = n % 10;Grabs the check digit itself first, before the peeling loop below consumes and destroys n's digits one by one.
int temp = n, sumAll = 0;temp is a second working copy (so n itself stays intact for reference), and sumAll starts at 0 ready to accumulate a digit sum.
while (temp > 0) {The classic digit-peeling loop condition: keep going as long as there are still digits left in temp.
sumAll += temp % 10;Adds the current last digit of temp onto the running sum.
temp = temp / 10;Removes that last digit by integer division, shortening the number by one digit for the next pass.
int sumFirst7 = sumAll - lastDigit;Since sumAll already includes the check digit itself (all 8 digits were summed), subtracting lastDigit back out leaves exactly the sum of the other 7 — avoiding a second, fiddlier loop that stops one digit early.
int expected = sumFirst7 % 10;Applies the validation rule directly: this is what the check digit should be, if the account number is valid.
if (expected != lastDigit) {True only for accounts where the computed expected check digit doesn't match the actual last digit — i.e. invalid accounts.
printf("%s %d (expected check digit %d, found %d)\n", accs[i].name, accs[i].accNum, expected, lastDigit);Reports both the expected and the actual check digit side by side, so the mismatch is visible.
invalidCount++;Tallies this account as invalid.
} else {The alternative branch: reached only for accounts that passed validation.
validBalance += accs[i].balance;Adds this account's balance to the running total — only ever reached for valid accounts, since the if above already filtered out the invalid ones.
printf("Invalid count : %d\n", invalidCount);Final tally, printed once after the loop.
printf("Valid balance : %.2f Taka\n", validBalance);Final accumulated total, printed once after the loop.
return 0;Normal program end.
Worked example — watch the digit-peeling loop happen, one iteration at a time

Take Rafiul Islam's record: accNum = 12345678. First, lastDigit = 12345678 % 10 = 8 is grabbed and set aside. Then the while (temp > 0) loop peels every digit of temp (which starts as a copy of the full number, 12345678):

Pass #temp at start of this passdigit peeled (temp % 10)sumAll after adding ittemp after (temp / 10)
11234567812345678 % 10 = 80 + 8 = 812345678 / 10 = 1234567
212345671234567 % 10 = 78 + 7 = 151234567 / 10 = 123456
3123456123456 % 10 = 615 + 6 = 21123456 / 10 = 12345
41234512345 % 10 = 521 + 5 = 2612345 / 10 = 1234
512341234 % 10 = 426 + 4 = 301234 / 10 = 123
6123123 % 10 = 330 + 3 = 33123 / 10 = 12
71212 % 10 = 233 + 2 = 3512 / 10 = 1
811 % 10 = 135 + 1 = 361 / 10 = 0 → loop stops, temp is no longer > 0

The loop ends with sumAll = 36 (the sum of ALL 8 digits, including the check digit). Then: sumFirst7 = 36 − 8 = 28 (subtracting the check digit we set aside earlier), and expected = 28 % 10 = 8. Since expected (8) == lastDigit (8), this account is valid — which is exactly why Rafiul Islam's account does not appear in the "Invalid accounts" list in the sample run, and his balance (52000.00) is included in the final valid-balance total.

Unlike A1–A3, here the fields aren't fixed-width slices — you need every individual digit, which means falling back to the digit-peeling while loop from Tab 1/Tab 3 instead of one-shot division. The neat trick: rather than separately summing "the first 7 digits", sum all 8 digits (easy, one loop) and then subtract the last digit back out — algebraically identical, much less code.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is a checksum/validation question: unlike A1–A3 where fields are fixed-width slices, here you must decompose the number into every individual digit and apply a rule across all of them. Recognising "I need every digit, not a fixed slice" is what tells you to reach for a while digit-peeling loop instead of one-shot division/modulo — this is the one problem on the sheet where A1's extraction tricks genuinely don't apply.
Likely variations on the exam The checksum rule itself changes (product instead of sum, alternating add/subtract digit by digit, weighting each digit by its position — this is exactly how real ISBN/credit-card checksums work), a different digit count, or the check digit living at the front instead of the back (in which case you'd peel digits from a version of the number with the first digit removed, rather than subtracting the last digit back out). The "sum everything, then subtract out what you don't want" trick is reusable any time you need "all but one" of a set of values.
How to approach it First decide: can this field be extracted with fixed division/modulo (A1/A2/A3 style), or do you genuinely need to inspect every digit (this question)? If the latter, peel digits with while (n > 0) { digit = n % 10; n = n / 10; }, accumulating whatever running computation the rule needs (sum, alternating sign, weighted sum) as you go. Only after the full peel is done do you compare against whatever isolated field (like the check digit) the rule is validating against. Always grab any digit you'll need before the peeling loop destroys it, as this solution does with lastDigit.

Part B — Dynamic Memory

B1. Taxi fare for several rides [10]

Fare rules: first 5 km → 60 Taka flat; next 10 km (6–15 km) → 22 Taka/km; above 15 km → 18 Taka/km; a fixed 25 Taka booking fee on every ride.

(Optional) write calculateFare(km) for one ride. In main(): take the number of rides N, dynamically allocate memory for N distances, read them, print the fare of each ride and the total collection.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

float calculateFare(int km) {
    float fare;
    if (km <= 5) {
        fare = 60;
    } else if (km <= 15) {
        fare = 60 + (km - 5) * 22;
    } else {
        fare = 60 + 10 * 22 + (km - 15) * 18;
    }
    fare = fare + 25;
    return fare;
}

int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Number of rides: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *distances = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (distances == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("Distance of ride %d (km): ", i + 1);
        scanf("%d", &distances[i]);
    }

    float total = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        float fare = calculateFare(distances[i]);
        printf("Ride %d: %d km -> %.2f Taka\n", i + 1, distances[i], fare);
        total += fare;
    }

    printf("Total collection: %.2f Taka\n", total);
    free(distances);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library for printf/scanf.
#include <stdlib.h>Required for malloc and free, which live in the standard library header.
float calculateFare(int km) {Declares a standalone function taking one ride's distance and returning that ride's fare as a decimal — this is the optional helper the question invites you to write.
float fare;A local variable, declared but not yet given a value, that will hold this ride's fare.
if (km <= 5) {Tests whether the whole trip fits inside the flat-rate band.
fare = 60;Within the flat band, the fare is fixed regardless of the exact distance (3 km and 5 km both cost 60).
} else if (km <= 15) {Only checked if the first condition failed; true for trips in the 6–15 km range.
fare = 60 + (km - 5) * 22;The flat 60 from the first bracket, plus only the km beyond 5 (i.e. km - 5), charged at the second bracket's rate.
} else {Reached only when both earlier conditions failed — trips longer than 15 km.
fare = 60 + 10 * 22 + (km - 15) * 18;Both lower brackets are now full (5 km flat, plus a full 10 km at the second rate), plus only the km beyond 15 charged at the top rate.
fare = fare + 25;The booking fee is unconditional — added once, after the slab calculation, regardless of which branch above ran.
return fare;Sends the fully-computed fare back to whichever line called this function.
int main() {Execution begins here.
int n, i;n will hold the number of rides, i is the loop counter.
printf("Number of rides: ");Prompts the user.
scanf("%d", &n);Reads how many rides there will be into n.
int *distances = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));Allocates a block of heap memory big enough for exactly n integers. sizeof(int) gives the size of one int in bytes, so n * sizeof(int) is the total bytes needed; the (int *) in front tells the compiler to treat the returned raw address as a pointer-to-int.
if (distances == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }If the system couldn't provide the memory, malloc returns NULL instead of a real address; checking for this before using the pointer avoids a crash, and return 1 (a non-zero value) signals to the OS that the program ended in an error state.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {Loop that will run once per ride, to read each distance.
printf("Distance of ride %d (km): ", i + 1);Displays a 1-based ride number in the prompt (i+1) even though the array itself is indexed from 0.
scanf("%d", &distances[i]);Reads one distance directly into slot i of the dynamically-allocated array.
float total = 0;Running total of all fares collected, started at 0 before any ride has been priced.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {A second, separate loop over the same rides — this one computes and prints each fare.
float fare = calculateFare(distances[i]);Calls the helper function on this ride's distance and stores the returned fare.
printf("Ride %d: %d km -> %.2f Taka\n", i + 1, distances[i], fare);Prints the 1-based ride number, its distance, and its computed fare, one line per ride.
total += fare;Adds this ride's fare onto the running total.
printf("Total collection: %.2f Taka\n", total);Printed once, after both loops, with the grand total across every ride.
free(distances);Returns the dynamically-allocated memory back to the system now that the program no longer needs it — required for every successful malloc/calloc/realloc.
return 0;Normal program end.

A slab-rate (a.k.a. tiered) calculation: each bracket only charges for the portion of the distance that falls inside it, and every bracket's fare builds on top of the full charge from the bracket(s) below it — you never re-price the same kilometre twice, and you never skip straight to the top rate for the whole distance.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the slab-rate pattern: a value is charged at different per-unit rates depending on which "bracket" it falls into, with every bracket except the highest one being capped. The DMA part is just the standard "malloc N, fill with a loop, process with a loop, free" skeleton wrapped around it — notice the two separate loops over i < n: one purely for input, one purely for processing/output. Keeping these separate (rather than pricing each ride the instant it's read) is what lets the "Total collection" be printed cleanly at the very end.
Likely variations on the exam Expect the slab boundaries/rates to change completely (this exact shape reappears as B2's income tax), an extra bracket added, the flat fee removed or made conditional (e.g. waived above a certain distance), or the function requirement made non-optional. Also watch for whether the fixed fee is supposed to apply once per ride (as here) or once per whole trip — re-read the wording carefully.
How to approach it Identify the brackets and their boundaries first, then write the branches from the bottom bracket up, each one building on the full charge of every bracket below it: if (x <= b1) charge1; else if (x <= b2) fullCharge1 + rate2*(x-b1); else fullCharge1 + fullCharge2 + rate3*(x-b2);. Add any flat/fixed fee outside and after this ladder, exactly once, so it isn't accidentally duplicated by any branch. For the DMA shell, always check NULL right after malloc, and never forget the matching free at the end.
B2. Yearly income tax [10]

Tax slabs: first 350,000 Taka → 0%; next 100,000 → 5%; next 300,000 → 10%; remaining income → 15%.

(Optional) write calculateTax(income) for one person. In main(): take the number of taxpayers N, dynamically allocate memory for N incomes using calloc, read them, print each person's tax, then print how many pay zero tax and the total tax collected.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

float calculateTax(float income) {
    float tax = 0;
    if (income <= 350000) return 0;

    float slab2 = income - 350000;
    if (slab2 > 100000) slab2 = 100000;
    tax += slab2 * 0.05;
    if (income <= 450000) return tax;

    float slab3 = income - 450000;
    if (slab3 > 300000) slab3 = 300000;
    tax += slab3 * 0.10;
    if (income <= 750000) return tax;

    float slab4 = income - 750000;
    tax += slab4 * 0.15;
    return tax;
}

int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Number of taxpayers: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    float *incomes = (float *)calloc(n, sizeof(float));
    if (incomes == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("Yearly income of person %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%f", &incomes[i]);
    }

    int zeroTaxCount = 0;
    float totalTax = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        float tax = calculateTax(incomes[i]);
        printf("Person %d: income %.2f -> tax %.2f\n", i + 1, incomes[i], tax);
        if (tax == 0) zeroTaxCount++;
        totalTax += tax;
    }

    printf("Taxpayers paying zero tax: %d\n", zeroTaxCount);
    printf("Total tax collected : %.2f Taka\n", totalTax);
    free(incomes);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
#include <stdlib.h>Needed for calloc/free.
float calculateTax(float income) {A helper function taking one person's income and returning their computed tax.
float tax = 0;Running tax total for this one person, started at 0 before any slab has been applied.
if (income <= 350000) return 0;The 0%-tax bracket exits immediately with no further computation — a direct return, not just setting tax, since nothing else in the function needs to run for this case.
float slab2 = income - 350000;How much of this income lies above the first (tax-free) threshold.
if (slab2 > 100000) slab2 = 100000;Caps the amount taxed at 5% to the bracket's own width (100,000), so a very high earner isn't charged 5% on more than that bracket actually holds.
tax += slab2 * 0.05;Only this bracket's (possibly capped) portion is taxed at 5%; the first 350,000 stays untaxed.
if (income <= 450000) return tax;Exits here for anyone who doesn't reach into the third bracket (350,000+100,000) — their tax is already fully and correctly computed.
float slab3 = income - 450000;How much of this income lies above the second threshold.
if (slab3 > 300000) slab3 = 300000;Caps this slice at the third bracket's own width (300,000).
tax += slab3 * 0.10;Adds 10% of this (possibly capped) slice onto the running tax total.
if (income <= 750000) return tax;Exits here for anyone who doesn't reach into the fourth, uncapped bracket.
float slab4 = income - 750000;Everything above 750,000 — this final bracket is not capped, since the question says "remaining income".
tax += slab4 * 0.15;Adds 15% of the entire remaining amount, with no upper limit.
return tax;Returns the fully-accumulated tax for this person.
int main() {Execution begins here.
int n, i;Number of taxpayers, and the loop counter.
printf("Number of taxpayers: ");Prompts the user.
scanf("%d", &n);Reads the taxpayer count into n.
float *incomes = (float *)calloc(n, sizeof(float));Allocates space for n floats. Unlike malloc, calloc takes the count and the per-element size as two separate arguments, and additionally zero-initialises every byte of the new memory.
if (incomes == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }Guards against a failed allocation exactly as in B1.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {Loop to read each person's income.
printf("Yearly income of person %d: ", i + 1);1-based prompt for readability.
scanf("%f", &incomes[i]);Reads a decimal value directly into slot i — %f for float, not %d.
int zeroTaxCount = 0;Tally of people who ended up owing nothing, started at 0.
float totalTax = 0;Running total tax collected across everyone, started at 0.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {A second loop, over the same people, this time computing and reporting tax.
float tax = calculateTax(incomes[i]);Calls the helper on this person's income.
printf("Person %d: income %.2f -> tax %.2f\n", i + 1, incomes[i], tax);Reports the income and computed tax for this one person.
if (tax == 0) zeroTaxCount++;Tallies this person if their computed tax came out to exactly 0.
totalTax += tax;Adds this person's tax onto the running grand total.
printf("Taxpayers paying zero tax: %d\n", zeroTaxCount);Final tally, printed once after the loop.
printf("Total tax collected : %.2f Taka\n", totalTax);Final accumulated total, printed once after the loop.
free(incomes);Releases the dynamically-allocated memory back to the system.
return 0;Normal program end.

Same slab-rate family as B1, but with 4 brackets instead of 3, which is exactly why this version uses early returns instead of if/else if/else: each return exits the moment income doesn't reach into the next bracket, so higher brackets simply never execute for a lower earner.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking The same slab-rate family as B1, scaled up to 4 brackets — this is the question that teaches you the early-return style of writing a slab calculator, which scales far better to many brackets than a long if/else if chain full of repeated subtraction terms. It also tests whether you remember that calloc takes count and size as two separate arguments and zero-fills memory, unlike malloc.
Likely variations on the exam A different number of brackets, different boundaries/rates, or the "cap the slab width" step forgotten as the deliberate bug to find in a tracing/debug-style question (a very high income taxed at 5% on the whole remainder rather than just the capped 100,000 slice is a classic injected error). Also note calloc vs malloc is often tested as a one-line conceptual question on its own.
How to approach it For any slab problem with more than 2–3 brackets, prefer the early-return style: compute how much of the income/quantity falls inside the current bracket (subtract the bracket's starting point, then cap at the bracket's width if it isn't the last/open-ended bracket), tax/charge just that slice, and return immediately if the total doesn't reach into the next bracket. This avoids ever re-deriving "how much of the lower brackets was already charged" by hand.
B3. Marks of an unknown number of students — grow with realloc [12]

Allocate space for 5 marks to begin with. Keep reading marks until the user enters −1 (which itself must not be stored or counted). Whenever the array becomes full, enlarge it by 5 more places using realloc and print a message with the new capacity. Finally print all marks, final capacity, average, highest mark, and how many marks are above the average.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int capacity = 5, count = 0;
    int *marks = (int *)malloc(capacity * sizeof(int));
    if (marks == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    int value;
    printf("Enter marks (-1 to stop):\n");
    scanf("%d", &value);

    while (value != -1) {
        if (count == capacity) {
            capacity = capacity + 5;
            marks = (int *)realloc(marks, capacity * sizeof(int));
            if (marks == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }
            printf("[capacity increased to %d]\n", capacity);
        }
        marks[count] = value;
        count++;
        scanf("%d", &value);
    }

    int i, sum = 0, highest = marks[0];
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        sum += marks[i];
        if (marks[i] > highest) highest = marks[i];
    }
    float avg = (float)sum / count;

    int above = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) if (marks[i] > avg) above++;

    printf("Marks entered (%d): ", count);
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%d ", marks[i]);
    printf("\n");
    printf("Final capacity : %d\n", capacity);
    printf("Average : %.2f\n", avg);
    printf("Highest : %d\n", highest);
    printf("Above average : %d\n", above);

    free(marks);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
#include <stdlib.h>Needed for malloc/realloc/free.
int main() {Execution begins here.
int capacity = 5;The array's current allocated size, starting at the required initial 5.
int count = 0;How many real marks have actually been stored so far — distinct from capacity, which is how much room exists.
int *marks = (int *)malloc(capacity * sizeof(int));The initial allocation, sized to match the starting capacity.
if (marks == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }Guards against a failed initial allocation.
int value;Will hold each mark as it's typed in, one at a time, before deciding whether to store it.
printf("Enter marks (-1 to stop):\n");One-time instruction to the user.
scanf("%d", &value);Reads the very first value before the loop condition is ever checked — this is what makes it a sentinel-controlled loop, since we don't yet know if this first value is real data or the stop signal.
while (value != -1) {Keeps looping as long as the most recently read value isn't the sentinel −1.
if (count == capacity) {Checked at the very start of every pass, before this value is stored — true exactly when the array is completely full.
capacity = capacity + 5;Grows the tracked capacity by the fixed increment of 5.
marks = (int *)realloc(marks, capacity * sizeof(int));Resizes the actual heap block to match the new capacity, preserving every value already stored; the return value is reassigned back into marks because realloc may move the block to a new address.
if (marks == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }Guards against a failed resize the same way the initial allocation was guarded.
printf("[capacity increased to %d]\n", capacity);Announces the new capacity to the user, exactly as the sample run shows.
marks[count] = value;Only reached once we're sure there's room (either it was already there, or growth just happened) — stores the value at the next free slot.
count++;One more real mark has now been stored, so the count increases.
scanf("%d", &value);Reads the next value at the very end of the loop body, so the while condition re-checks this freshly-read value on the next pass — this positioning is what lets the sentinel (-1) get tested without ever being stored.
int i, sum = 0, highest = marks[0];Starts the running sum at 0, and seeds highest with the very first real mark (index 0) rather than an arbitrary guess like 0, which could be wrong if all marks were negative.
for (i = 0; i < count; i++) {Loops only over the count real marks actually stored — not over the full capacity, which may include unused allocated slots.
sum += marks[i];Adds this mark onto the running total.
if (marks[i] > highest) highest = marks[i];Updates the running maximum whenever a bigger mark is found.
float avg = (float)sum / count;Casts sum to float before dividing so the result keeps its decimal part, rather than performing integer division and truncating it.
int above = 0;Tally of marks strictly above the average, started at 0.
for (i = 0; i < count; i++) if (marks[i] > avg) above++;A second pass, needed because the average itself couldn't be known until every mark had already been summed in the first pass.
printf("Marks entered (%d): ", count);Header showing how many real marks were entered.
for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%d ", marks[i]);Prints every stored mark, space-separated, on the same line as the header above.
printf("\n");Moves to a new line after the list of marks.
printf("Final capacity : %d\n", capacity);Reports the array's final allocated size (which may be larger than count if growth over-allocated).
printf("Average : %.2f\n", avg);Reports the average, rounded to 2 decimals.
printf("Highest : %d\n", highest);Reports the highest mark found.
printf("Above average : %d\n", above);Reports the count from the second pass.
free(marks);Releases the final dynamically-allocated block back to the system.
return 0;Normal program end.

The subtle timing point that trips people up: the capacity check and the growth must happen before the new value is stored, checking whether the array is already full (count == capacity) — not after storing, and not by predicting in advance whether a value will be -1.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the sentinel-controlled growing array pattern: you don't know the count in advance, so you start with a small capacity and grow in fixed increments only when you actually run out of room, stopping on a special "stop value" that is itself never stored. It's really testing whether you can get the exact ordering of "read → check/grow → store → read next" right, since a single misplaced line here either stores the sentinel by mistake or overflows the array by one.
Likely variations on the exam The sentinel value changing (0, a negative number, or a specific word for a string-based version), the growth increment changing (double the capacity instead of +5, which is actually the more common real-world strategy), or extra derived statistics tacked onto the end (median, a count below average, standard deviation). The core allocate/grow/read loop stays identical regardless of what changes around it.
How to approach it Structure it as: read one value before the loop starts; loop while (value != sentinel); at the very top of the loop body, check if (count == capacity) and grow first if needed; then store and increment count; then read the next value at the very bottom of the loop body (so the next condition check sees it). Getting the read-before/read-after placement right is the single most common source of off-by-one bugs in this pattern — trace through by hand with a tiny example (capacity 2, 3 real values) if you're ever unsure.
B4. Salaries — more employees join later [10]

Take the number of employees N, dynamically allocate memory for N salaries, read them. Ask how many employees M joined later, resize the array to hold N+M salaries (keeping the ones already entered), read the M new salaries. Print all the salaries, the total and average, then free the memory.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, m, i;
    printf("Number of employees: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    float *salaries = (float *)malloc(n * sizeof(float));
    if (salaries == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("Salary of employee %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%f", &salaries[i]);
    }

    printf("How many employees joined later? ");
    scanf("%d", &m);

    salaries = (float *)realloc(salaries, (n + m) * sizeof(float));
    if (salaries == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = n; i < n + m; i++) {
        printf("Salary of employee %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%f", &salaries[i]);
    }

    int total_n = n + m;
    float total = 0;
    printf("All %d salaries: ", total_n);
    for (i = 0; i < total_n; i++) { printf("%.2f ", salaries[i]); total += salaries[i]; }
    printf("\n");

    printf("Total : %.2f\n", total);
    printf("Average : %.2f\n", total / total_n);
    free(salaries);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
#include <stdlib.h>Needed for malloc/realloc/free.
int main() {Execution begins here.
int n, m, i;n is the initial employee count, m is how many join later, i is the loop counter.
printf("Number of employees: ");Prompts for the initial count.
scanf("%d", &n);Reads n.
float *salaries = (float *)malloc(n * sizeof(float));Allocates space for exactly the first batch of n salaries.
if (salaries == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }Guards against a failed initial allocation.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {Loop to read the first n salaries.
printf("Salary of employee %d: ", i + 1);1-based prompt.
scanf("%f", &salaries[i]);Reads a decimal salary into slot i.
printf("How many employees joined later? ");Prompts for the size of the second batch.
scanf("%d", &m);Reads m — unlike B3, this exact number is simply told to the program directly, no growth loop needed.
salaries = (float *)realloc(salaries, (n + m) * sizeof(float));Grows the block once, straight to the final combined size n+m, instead of repeatedly growing in fixed increments like B3; the first n values already stored are preserved automatically by realloc.
if (salaries == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }Guards against a failed resize.
for (i = n; i < n + m; i++) {Starts at index n (the first previously-unused slot), not 0, so the already-entered salaries are left completely untouched.
printf("Salary of employee %d: ", i + 1);1-based prompt, continuing the numbering from where the first batch left off.
scanf("%f", &salaries[i]);Reads each new salary into its new slot.
int total_n = n + m;The final combined employee count, computed once and reused below instead of repeating n + m every time.
float total = 0;Running sum of all salaries, started at 0.
printf("All %d salaries: ", total_n);Header before printing every salary on one line.
for (i = 0; i < total_n; i++) {This final loop covers all employees, old and new together, from index 0 to the final combined count.
printf("%.2f ", salaries[i]);Prints this salary, rounded to 2 decimals, followed by a space.
total += salaries[i];Adds this salary onto the running grand total.
printf("\n");Newline after the full list of salaries.
printf("Total : %.2f\n", total);Reports the grand total.
printf("Average : %.2f\n", total / total_n);Divides the total by the final combined employee count to get the average; since both are already appropriately typed (total is float), no explicit cast is needed here.
free(salaries);Releases the dynamically-allocated memory.
return 0;Normal program end.

The cleanest of the three DMA-growth styles on this sheet: unlike B3 (unknown count, grow-as-you-go) this one knows exactly how many extra slots it needs (M, told directly by the user) and grows once, straight to the final size.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the known-increment resize pattern: you're told exactly how many new items are coming (M), so you resize once to the exact final size, in contrast to B3 where the final size is unknown and growth happens incrementally in a loop. It's testing whether you default to the simpler one-shot resize when the count is actually known, rather than over-engineering a growth loop that isn't needed here.
Likely variations on the exam The second batch's size depending on a condition instead of being asked outright (e.g. "resize to add 20% more capacity"), a request to only keep the new employees who meet some threshold (turning it into filter+resize), or multiple resize rounds chained together (join now, then join again later). Also watch whether the average should be computed from all employees combined (as here) or the two groups' averages reported separately.
How to approach it Whenever the exact number of additional elements is known up front, resize with realloc(ptr, (oldCount + additionalCount) * sizeof(type)) as a single step, then fill the new region with a loop starting at oldCount and running to oldCount + additionalCount. Only fall back to B3's repeated-growth-in-a-loop style when the total count genuinely isn't known ahead of time (e.g. reading until a sentinel).

Strings are your easiest marks — treat this section as free points

Every single one of C1–C9 is built from the exact same handful of primitives, just recombined: walk the string to '\0', and at each character either (a) classify it (letter? vowel? digit? space?), (b) test whether it's the start of a new word (not a space, and the previous character was a space or this is index 0), or (c) copy/skip it into a second buffer. All 9 problems below are genuinely Core or only lightly Stretch difficulty — if the real bonus question looks like any of these, you should be able to finish it in well under 10 minutes.

Part C — Strings (bonus)

C1. Count the words, even with extra spaces [6]

Count the words in char str[] = " EEE 103 final exam is near "; " — note the leading/trailing spaces and the double/triple spaces between words. " Simply counting spaces and adding one will get this wrong. No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = " EEE 103 final exam is near ";
    int i, wordCount = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' ')) {
            wordCount++;
        }
    }

    printf("String : \"%s\"\n", str);
    printf("Words : %d\n", wordCount);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library for printf.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = " EEE 103 final exam is near ";A character array initialised directly from a string literal; the compiler automatically appends the invisible '\0' terminator, and the array is sized to fit the text exactly.
int i, wordCount = 0;i will walk through the string; wordCount starts at 0 so it only counts what's actually found.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks forward one character at a time, stopping exactly when the terminator is reached — this is the standard way to loop over a string of unknown/variable length.
if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' ')) {Both halves must hold: the current character is not a space, AND (it's the first character OR the previous character was a space). The i == 0 check prevents reading str[-1], which would be invalid memory access.
wordCount++;Only fires once per word, at the exact character where the word begins — every other character of that same word fails the second half of the test (its predecessor isn't a space).
printf("String : \"%s\"\n", str);Prints the whole string, with literal quote characters around it (\" is an escaped quote inside the format string).
printf("Words : %d\n", wordCount);Prints the final tally after the loop has finished.
return 0;Normal program end.

The word-start test: a character is the first letter of a new word exactly when it isn't a space itself, and either it's the very first character of the string, or the character right before it was a space. This correctly ignores any number of consecutive spaces — a run of 3 spaces only ever has one "space → non-space" transition, so it can never be miscounted as multiple words.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This tests whether you can count "things that start" rather than "things that are separated by" — counting separators (spaces) and adding one is the naive approach that breaks the moment spacing isn't perfectly uniform, which is exactly why the sheet deliberately gave you messy spacing (leading, trailing, and doubled spaces all at once).
Likely variations on the exam The separator character changing (commas, tabs), a request to also print each word out separately (tokenising), or a twist where multiple different characters can separate words (space or comma) — in that case the single test str[i]!=' ' becomes a small helper function like isSep(c) checked on both sides.
How to approach it Always default to the word-start test over counting separators. Memorise the exact condition: str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i-1] == ' ') — it handles leading spaces, trailing spaces, and multi-spaces all at once, with no special-casing needed anywhere else in the loop.
C2. Count vowels, consonants, digits, spaces and symbols [6]

Scan char str[] = "BRAC University, EEE-103 Final 2026!"; once and " report how many vowels, consonants, digits, spaces and other characters it contains. " No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "BRAC University, EEE-103 Final 2026!";
    int i, vowels = 0, consonants = 0, digits = 0, spaces = 0, others = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char c = str[i];
        if ((c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')) {
            if (c=='a'||c=='e'||c=='i'||c=='o'||c=='u'||
                c=='A'||c=='E'||c=='I'||c=='O'||c=='U') {
                vowels++;
            } else {
                consonants++;
            }
        } else if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') {
            digits++;
        } else if (c == ' ') {
            spaces++;
        } else {
            others++;
        }
    }

    printf("String : %s\n", str);
    printf("Vowels : %d\n", vowels);
    printf("Consonants : %d\n", consonants);
    printf("Digits : %d\n", digits);
    printf("Spaces : %d\n", spaces);
    printf("Others : %d\n", others);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "BRAC University, EEE-103 Final 2026!";The hard-coded string to analyse.
int i, vowels = 0, consonants = 0, digits = 0, spaces = 0, others = 0;Five independent counters, all started at 0, one per category the question asks for.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once, character by character, until the terminator.
char c = str[i];Copies the current character into a short-named local variable purely for readability in the checks below.
if ((c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')) {Tests whether c is a letter at all, in either case, by checking if it falls in either ASCII range.
if (c=='a'||c=='e'||c=='i'||c=='o'||c=='u'||c=='A'||c=='E'||c=='I'||c=='O'||c=='U') {Nested inside the letter check: tests c against all 10 vowel possibilities (5 lowercase, 5 uppercase).
vowels++;Reached only for letters that matched one of the 10 vowels.
} else {The alternative for the nested check: a letter that matched none of the 10 vowels.
consonants++;Any letter that isn't a vowel must, by elimination, be a consonant.
} else if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') {Only reached once we already know c isn't a letter; tests the digit ASCII range.
digits++;Reached for digit characters.
} else if (c == ' ') {Reached only if c is neither a letter nor a digit; tests for the space character specifically.
spaces++;Reached for space characters.
} else {Whatever is left over after every earlier test has failed.
others++;Catches punctuation and symbols — anything not a letter, digit, or space.
printf("String : %s\n", str);Prints the original string.
printf("Vowels : %d\n", vowels);Prints the vowel tally.
printf("Consonants : %d\n", consonants);Prints the consonant tally.
printf("Digits : %d\n", digits);Prints the digit tally.
printf("Spaces : %d\n", spaces);Prints the space tally.
printf("Others : %d\n", others);Prints the leftover-character tally.
return 0;Normal program end.

A single character can only ever be exactly one of these 5 categories, so the whole problem is just a 5-way if/else if ladder run once per character — letters are further split into vowel/consonant with one small nested check.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This tests the character-classification toolkit directly: recognising that ASCII ranges ('a'..'z', 'A'..'Z', '0'..'9') can be tested with simple range comparisons, and that a clean if/else if ladder (rather than 5 independent ifs) correctly guarantees every character lands in exactly one bucket, never zero and never more than one.
Likely variations on the exam The exact category list changing (uppercase vs lowercase counted separately, punctuation broken into its own sub-categories), or being asked for percentages instead of raw counts (just divide each count by the total length afterwards, casting to float first). The category list rarely exceeds 4–5 buckets.
How to approach it List every category the question asks for, then write one if/else if ladder (not separate independent ifs) so each character only ever increments one counter. Put the most specific test first if any categories could otherwise overlap (here, vowel is nested inside the letter check, not a separate top-level branch, since every vowel is also a letter).
C3. Length and reverse, without strlen [6]

Find the length of char str[] = "Programming"; and print it " backwards. You may not use strlen or strrev.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Programming";
    int len = 0, i;

    while (str[len] != '\0') len++;

    printf("String : %s\n", str);
    printf("Length : %d\n", len);

    printf("Reversed : ");
    for (i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) printf("%c", str[i]);
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "Programming";The hard-coded string.
int len = 0, i;len will count characters, started at 0; i is reserved for the printing loop below.
while (str[len] != '\0') len++;Advances one position per character, stopping exactly at the terminator — len ends up equal to the count of real characters (the terminator itself is never counted).
printf("String : %s\n", str);Prints the original string.
printf("Length : %d\n", len);Prints the length just computed.
printf("Reversed : ");Header before the reversed output, with no newline yet, so the characters that follow appear on the same line.
for (i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) printf("%c", str[i]);Starts at the last real character (len - 1, not len itself, which is the terminator) and walks backward to index 0, printing one character at a time.
printf("\n");Moves to a new line once the reversed printing loop is done.
return 0;Normal program end.

The two most fundamental hand-rolled string primitives in the whole course, back to back: the length-finding while loop (walk until '\0', count steps), and printing backward by simply starting the print loop at the last real index (len-1) and counting down to 0 — no second array needed since we only ever read in reverse, never store.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the single most important pair of primitives in the whole strings topic — nearly every other string question on this sheet (and in the course) either reuses the length-finding loop directly, or reuses the "walk backward from len-1" idea, or both.
Likely variations on the exam This exact pair embedded as a first step inside a bigger question (C4's palindrome check needs the length first too), or a request to reverse in place (swap characters from both ends toward the middle) instead of just printing backward, which needs a second pointer variable and an actual swap rather than only reading.
How to approach it Memorise while (str[len] != '\0') len++; cold — it's the seed for almost every other string algorithm. For printing backward specifically, remember the off-by-one: the last real character is at index len - 1, since str[len] itself is the invisible null terminator, not a printable character.
C4. Palindrome, ignoring case and spaces [6]

Decide whether char str[] = "Never Odd Or Even"; is a palindrome, " ignoring capital letters and spaces. No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Never Odd Or Even";
    char cleaned[50];
    int i, k = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char c = str[i];
        if (c == ' ') continue;
        if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32;
        cleaned[k] = c;
        k++;
    }
    cleaned[k] = '\0';

    int isPalin = 1;
    int left = 0, right = k - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        if (cleaned[left] != cleaned[right]) { isPalin = 0; break; }
        left++;
        right--;
    }

    printf("String : %s\n", str);
    printf("Cleaned : %s\n", cleaned);
    printf("Result : %s\n", isPalin ? "Palindrome" : "Not a palindrome");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "Never Odd Or Even";The hard-coded string to test.
char cleaned[50];A second, separate array that will hold a filtered lowercase copy of str, big enough to comfortably fit it.
int i, k = 0;i walks the original string; k is a separate write-index into cleaned, started at 0.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the original string once, from start to terminator.
char c = str[i];Copies the current character for readability.
if (c == ' ') continue;Spaces are simply skipped: continue jumps straight back to the loop's next iteration without writing anything to cleaned, and without advancing k.
if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32;Normalises this character to lowercase before copying, so the later comparison is automatically case-insensitive.
cleaned[k] = c;Writes the (possibly just-lowercased) character into the next free slot of the cleaned copy.
k++;Advances the write index, since one more character has just been added to cleaned.
cleaned[k] = '\0';Terminates the filtered copy right after the last character actually written — without this, cleaned would not be a valid string.
int isPalin = 1;A flag assumed true (1) until/unless a mismatch is found; using an int as a boolean flag is standard in C.
int left = 0, right = k - 1;Two indices into the cleaned copy: left starts at its beginning, right starts at its last real character (k-1, since k itself is the terminator's position).
while (left < right) {Keeps comparing pairs of characters as long as the two pointers haven't met or crossed in the middle.
if (cleaned[left] != cleaned[right]) { isPalin = 0; break; }The moment any matching pair of characters differs, the string cannot be a palindrome: the flag is set false and break exits the loop immediately, since no further comparison is needed.
left++;Moves the left index one step inward, toward the middle.
right--;Moves the right index one step inward, toward the middle, from the opposite end.
printf("String : %s\n", str);Prints the original, unmodified string.
printf("Cleaned : %s\n", cleaned);Prints the filtered, lowercased copy that the check actually ran on.
printf("Result : %s\n", isPalin ? "Palindrome" : "Not a palindrome");The ?: ternary operator picks one of two literal strings to print, based on the final value of the flag.
return 0;Normal program end.

Two stages, kept deliberately separate: first filter the string down to only what matters (build a brand-new lowercase, space-free copy), then run the ordinary two-pointer palindrome check on that clean copy. Trying to do both at once (filtering while comparing) is where most mistakes happen — build the clean copy first.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This tests whether you instinctively reach for a filter-then-check two-stage design instead of trying to cram the space-skipping and case-folding logic directly into the two-pointer comparison itself (which is possible but much more error-prone under exam pressure, since you'd need to advance each pointer past ignored characters independently while also comparing).
Likely variations on the exam Other characters also needing ignoring (punctuation, digits), the cleaning rule being different (e.g. keep only letters, which folds space-skipping and punctuation-skipping into one single test), or the check requested as an in-place two-pointer walk over the original string (skipping ignored characters on the fly) if the examiner wants to test whether you can avoid the extra buffer.
How to approach it Whenever a problem says "ignoring X", build a clean second copy with X filtered out first, then apply the ordinary algorithm (palindrome check, reversal, whatever's asked) to that clean copy. Only skip the second buffer and filter on-the-fly once the simpler two-stage version is second nature to you.
C5. Toggle the case of every letter [6]

Change every capital letter of char str[] = "Hello World 2026"; to " small, and every small letter to capital, leaving digits and spaces untouched. No " built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Hello World 2026";
    printf("Original : %s\n", str);

    int i;
    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] >= 'a' && str[i] <= 'z') {
            str[i] = str[i] - 32;
        } else if (str[i] >= 'A' && str[i] <= 'Z') {
            str[i] = str[i] + 32;
        }
    }

    printf("Toggled : %s\n", str);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "Hello World 2026";The hard-coded string, modified in place below.
printf("Original : %s\n", str);Prints the string exactly as given, before any changes are made.
int i;Loop counter.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once, from start to terminator.
if (str[i] >= 'a' && str[i] <= 'z') {Tests whether this character is currently lowercase.
str[i] = str[i] - 32;Lowercase → uppercase: subtracting 32 from a lowercase ASCII code gives exactly its uppercase counterpart, and the result is written straight back into the array, mutating it in place.
} else if (str[i] >= 'A' && str[i] <= 'Z') {Only checked if the character wasn't lowercase; tests whether it's currently uppercase.
str[i] = str[i] + 32;Uppercase → lowercase: the mirror-image operation.
printf("Toggled : %s\n", str);Prints the string again — now showing the in-place modifications made by the loop above.
return 0;Normal program end.

Both halves of the ASCII ±32 trick used together: a lowercase letter goes up by subtracting 32, an uppercase letter goes down by adding 32 — and since the two if/else if branches are mutually exclusive, digits and spaces simply fail both checks and pass through untouched.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This tests whether you can apply the ±32 ASCII trick in both directions inside the same loop, and — just as importantly — whether you correctly leave non-letters alone by simply not writing a branch for them, rather than overcomplicating with an explicit "do nothing" else.
Likely variations on the exam A request to toggle only certain characters (e.g. only vowels), toggle case while also doing something else in the same pass (like counting how many characters changed), or the reverse direction only (force everything to one case, which is simpler and only needs one branch, not two).
How to approach it Write the lowercase→uppercase and uppercase→lowercase branches as two separate, mutually exclusive if/else if checks in the same loop. Don't write a third else branch for "leave alone" — simply not matching either condition already achieves that for free, since the character is left exactly as it was read.
C6. How many times does a character appear [6]

Take a character from the user and count how many times it appears in " char str[] = "engineering education";. No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "engineering education";
    char ch;
    int i, count = 0;

    printf("String: %s\n", str);
    printf("Enter a character to search: ");
    scanf(" %c", &ch);

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] == ch) count++;
    }

    printf("'%c' appears %d time(s)\n", ch, count);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "engineering education";The hard-coded string to search within.
char ch;Will hold the single character the user wants to search for.
int i, count = 0;Loop counter, and a match tally started at 0.
printf("String: %s\n", str);Displays the string being searched, for context.
printf("Enter a character to search: ");Prompts the user.
scanf(" %c", &ch);Reads exactly one character into ch. The leading space before %c tells scanf to first skip any whitespace/leftover newline sitting in the input buffer — without it, a stray newline could be read as ch instead of the character the user actually typed.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once, from start to terminator.
if (str[i] == ch) count++;A direct character comparison: every time the current character matches the one being searched for, the tally increases by 1.
printf("'%c' appears %d time(s)\n", ch, count);Prints the searched-for character (inside literal single-quote characters) and the final tally.
return 0;Normal program end.

The simplest possible string-scanning question on the sheet: one loop, one equality check, one counter. The only real trap is the input line — scanf(" %c", ...) with a leading space before %c deliberately skips any leftover whitespace/newline sitting in the input buffer, which a bare %c would otherwise read by mistake.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the simplest string primitive on the sheet, but it's also the one most likely to lose an easy mark on a purely mechanical detail: forgetting the leading space in scanf(" %c", &ch) when a character input follows any earlier scanf call in the same program.
Likely variations on the exam A request to also report the character's positions (print every index it was found at, not just the count), case-insensitive counting (compare after normalising both sides to the same case with the ±32 trick from C5), or counting a whole substring's occurrences instead of a single character.
How to approach it For any input that reads a single character with %c, always use a leading space (" %c") if any earlier scanf call has already happened in the program — it costs nothing and prevents a very common silent bug. The counting logic itself is just one if inside a plain character-walk loop.
C7. Print the string without its vowels [6]

Print char str[] = "Computer Programming"; with every vowel " removed. No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Computer Programming";
    int i;

    printf("Original : %s\n", str);
    printf("Without vowels: ");
    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char c = str[i];
        if (c=='a'||c=='e'||c=='i'||c=='o'||c=='u'||
            c=='A'||c=='E'||c=='I'||c=='O'||c=='U') {
            continue;
        }
        printf("%c", c);
    }
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "Computer Programming";The hard-coded string.
int i;Loop counter.
printf("Original : %s\n", str);Prints the string unmodified, before filtering.
printf("Without vowels: ");Header, with no newline yet, so the filtered characters print on the same line.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once, from start to terminator.
char c = str[i];Copies the current character for readability.
if (c=='a'||c=='e'||c=='i'||c=='o'||c=='u'||c=='A'||c=='E'||c=='I'||c=='O'||c=='U') {The 10-way vowel check, covering both cases.
continue;Matching any vowel skips the rest of this loop iteration entirely — execution jumps straight to the next i, never reaching the printf below.
printf("%c", c);Only reached for characters that aren't vowels, since continue would already have skipped past this line otherwise.
printf("\n");Newline after the filtered output is complete.
return 0;Normal program end.

A "print selectively" question: rather than building a second filtered string (like C4), this one simply skips printing whatever should be removed — continue jumps straight to the next loop iteration without ever reaching the printf for a vowel.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This tests whether you reach for the lighter-weight "skip printing" approach (continue) when the task is just to display a filtered version, rather than unnecessarily building a whole second string buffer like C4 did (which is needed there because the cleaned string is reused afterward for the palindrome check, but isn't needed here since the result is only ever printed once).
Likely variations on the exam The removed category changing (remove digits, remove a specific letter, remove everything except letters), or a request to also store the filtered result in a new array instead of just printing it directly (which would bring back the C4-style buffer-building approach with its own write-index).
How to approach it Ask yourself whether the filtered result needs to be reused later in the program. If it's only ever printed once, skip building a second buffer entirely and just use continue to skip printing what should be removed. If it needs to be reused, build a second buffer with an explicit write index, as in C4.
C8. Build the acronym of a name [6]

Print the acronym of char str[] = "Bangladesh Rural Advancement " "Committee"; — the first letter of every word, in capitals. No built-in " functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Bangladesh Rural Advancement Committee";
    int i;

    printf("Name : %s\n", str);
    printf("Acronym : ");
    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' ')) {
            char c = str[i];
            if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') c = c - 32;
            printf("%c", c);
        }
    }
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "Bangladesh Rural Advancement Committee";The hard-coded name to build an acronym from.
int i;Loop counter.
printf("Name : %s\n", str);Prints the full name.
printf("Acronym : ");Header, with no newline yet.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once, from start to terminator.
if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' ')) {Exactly the C1 word-start test — true only at the first letter of each word.
char c = str[i];Copies this word-starting character for readability.
if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') c = c - 32;Forces this letter to uppercase even if the original word happened to start lowercase, using the same −32 trick from C5.
printf("%c", c);Only the one letter per word is ever printed, giving exactly the acronym, with no spaces or other letters of each word.
printf("\n");Newline after the acronym is fully printed.
return 0;Normal program end.

Two primitives combined in a single pass: C1's word-start test decides which characters to print, and C5's case-conversion trick makes sure every printed letter is uppercase regardless of how it was originally typed.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is a direct test of whether you can combine two primitives you already know (word-start detection from C1, case-forcing from C5) rather than needing a brand new algorithm — the real exam very often builds "new" questions exactly this way, by recombining a small toolkit you've already practiced.
Likely variations on the exam A request for the acronym in lowercase instead, a separator between acronym letters (e.g. "B.R.A.C."), or the acronym built only from words longer than some length (skipping short connector words like "of"/"the", which needs an extra small loop to measure each word's length before deciding to print it).
How to approach it Recognise this as "C1's word-start test, but instead of counting, print (and force-uppercase) the matching character." Whenever a new-looking problem appears, check whether it's really just an old primitive with a different action taken at each matching position — this habit will save significant time under exam pressure.
C9. Count sentences and words per sentence [6]

Count the sentences and words in char str[] = "C is fun. Pointers are " "hard! Do you agree? Practice daily."; and print the average words per " sentence. A sentence ends at ., ! or ?. No " built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "C is fun. Pointers are hard! Do you agree? Practice daily.";
    int i, wordCount = 0, sentenceCount = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' ')) {
            wordCount++;
        }
        if (str[i] == '.' || str[i] == '!' || str[i] == '?') {
            sentenceCount++;
        }
    }

    float avg = (float)wordCount / sentenceCount;

    printf("Text : %s\n", str);
    printf("Sentences : %d\n", sentenceCount);
    printf("Words : %d\n", wordCount);
    printf("Average : %.2f words per sentence\n", avg);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line, including plain syntax
#include <stdio.h>I/O library.
int main() {Execution begins here.
char str[] = "C is fun. Pointers are hard! Do you agree? Practice daily.";The hard-coded passage to analyse.
int i, wordCount = 0, sentenceCount = 0;Two independent counters, both started at 0, plus the loop index.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the passage once, from start to terminator.
if (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' ')) {Exactly the same word-start test as C1/C8, run here alongside the sentence check below rather than on its own.
wordCount++;Tallies one more word every time the word-start test passes.
if (str[i] == '.' || str[i] == '!' || str[i] == '?') {A completely separate, independent test on the very same character: is it one of the three sentence-ending marks?
sentenceCount++;A sentence-ending mark is counted the moment it's seen; note punctuation is attached to the previous word in the string, so this check never interferes with the word-start test above it.
float avg = (float)wordCount / sentenceCount;Casts wordCount to float before dividing, exactly as in every other average calculation in this course, so the result keeps its decimal part instead of being truncated by integer division.
printf("Text : %s\n", str);Prints the original passage.
printf("Sentences : %d\n", sentenceCount);Prints the sentence tally.
printf("Words : %d\n", wordCount);Prints the word tally.
printf("Average : %.2f words per sentence\n", avg);Prints the computed average, rounded to 2 decimals.
return 0;Normal program end.

Both counts run in one single pass, completely independently of each other: the word-start test (from C1) and the sentence-ending test are two separate ifs inside the same loop, neither one affecting the other, then combined into an average only at the very end.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the clearest demonstration on the whole sheet that unrelated counts can be tallied in the same loop without interfering with each other, as long as each one only looks at the current character and (sometimes) the one right before it. It's also a test of whether you remember the cast-before-dividing rule for the final average.
Likely variations on the exam Additional derived statistics (longest sentence, shortest sentence), a different set of sentence-ending characters, or a request for the count of words per individual sentence rather than just the overall average (which would need a per-sentence word counter that resets to 0 every time a sentence-ending mark is seen).
How to approach it When a question asks for two or more independent counts from the same piece of text, always check first whether they can be tallied in a single pass (one loop, several unrelated ifs) before reaching for multiple loops — it's faster to write and less error-prone. Only split into separate loops if one count genuinely depends on the other being finished first.

Part D — Pointer Tracing (new lookalikes)

Part D — Pointer Tracing (new, generated in Assignment 2 Q4's exact style)

These two problems are freshly generated to match Assignment 2 Q4's difficulty and structure exactly: decoy globals, several functions each shadowing the same variable name, and a mix of "changes through a pointer" vs "changes through a captured return value". Each one includes a full line-by-line breakdown, a complete trace table, and a step-by-step guide to tracing this specific table — not just the final answer.

Pointer Tracing Lookalike 1 — count/total across a loop, freshly generated Assignment 2 Q4 style: globals as decoys, pointer accumulation, return-vs-pointer contrast

Trace the program below and give its complete output. Just like Assignment 2 Q4, pay close attention to which count each line actually refers to — there is a global count, main's own local count, and two more functions that each declare their own separate count.

#include <stdio.h>
int count = 100;

void update(int *ptr, int count) {
    int step = 2;
    count = count + step;
    *ptr = *ptr + count;
    step = step + 1;
    printf("A:%d,%d,%d\n", count, *ptr, step);
}

int scale(int count) {
    int result = count * 2;
    return result;
}

void main() {
    int i;
    int count = 5;
    int total = 0;
    int *p = &total;

    for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
        if (i == 1) {
            count = count + 10;
        }
        update(p, count);
        count = scale(count);
        if (i == 2) {
            *p = *p + count;
        }
        printf("i=%d count=%d total=%d\n", i, count, total);
    }
    printf("Final: count=%d total=%d\n", count, total);
}
#include <stdio.h>
int count = 100;

void update(int *ptr, int count) {
    int step = 2;
    count = count + step;
    *ptr = *ptr + count;
    step = step + 1;
    printf("A:%d,%d,%d\n", count, *ptr, step);
}

int scale(int count) {
    int result = count * 2;
    return result;
}

void main() {
    int i;
    int count = 5;
