    int total = 0;
    int *p = &total;

    for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
        if (i == 1) {
            count = count + 10;
        }
        update(p, count);
        count = scale(count);
        if (i == 2) {
            *p = *p + count;
        }
        printf("i=%d count=%d total=%d\n", i, count, total);
    }
    printf("Final: count=%d total=%d\n", count, total);
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line
int count = 100;A global variable, visible everywhere in the file — but only if a function does not declare its own local/parameter of the same name. Watch what happens next: every function below does declare its own count, so this global value of 100 will never actually be read or printed anywhere in the whole program — exactly like Assignment 2 Q4's decoy global x.
void update(int *ptr, int count) {Declares a function with two parameters: ptr (a pointer, so it can reach back into whatever variable the caller passed the address of) and count (an ordinary by-value parameter — a fresh, independent copy every single call, completely unrelated to main's count or the global one).
int step = 2;A local variable, freshly created and reset to 2 on every call to update — it does not remember its value between calls.
count = count + step;Modifies update's own local parameter copy only; this line can never affect main's count, since parameters are passed by value.
*ptr = *ptr + count;This is the one line in the function that reaches outside of update: *ptr means "the variable that ptr points to", which is main's total (since update is always called with p, and p = &total). This permanently changes main's total.
step = step + 1;Modifies the local step one more time; only affects what the printf below prints, has no effect outside this function.
printf("A:%d,%d,%d\n", count, *ptr, step);Prints, in order: update's own local count, the current value of whatever *ptr points to (main's total), and the local step.
int scale(int count) {A separate function with its own separate parameter count — the third distinct "count" variable in this program, unrelated to main's and unrelated to update's.
int result = count * 2;Doubles this function's own local parameter copy and stores it locally.
return result;Sends the doubled value back to whoever called scale — this is the only way any information from inside scale can ever leave it, since it has no pointer parameter.
int i;Main's loop counter.
int count = 5;Main's own local count, starting at 5 — this is the ONLY count that persists across loop iterations, since it's the only one declared in main's scope, which itself doesn't get re-created every iteration (unlike each function's local copies, which reset on every call).
int total = 0;Main's running total, starting at 0.
int *p = &total;Points p at main's total. This binding never changes for the rest of the program — p is never reassigned to point anywhere else.
for (i = 0; i < 3; i++) {Runs the loop body for i = 0, 1, 2 — exactly 3 passes.
if (i == 1) { count = count + 10; }Only on the second pass (i==1) does main bump its own count by 10 before calling anything — this permanently changes main's count from this point onward, since it's assigned directly by name, not through a function.
update(p, count);Calls update, passing p (so update can reach total through *ptr) and the current value of main's count (copied by value into update's own parameter).
count = scale(count);Calls scale with main's current count as input, and immediately overwrites main's own count with whatever scale returns. This is the only line in the whole program that changes main's count based on a function's result — the call to update just above never touches main's count at all, since update's parameter is a separate copy.
if (i == 2) { *p = *p + count; }Only on the third and final pass does main additionally add its own (already-updated-by-scale) count onto *p (i.e. total), on top of whatever update already added to it earlier in this same iteration.
printf("i=%d count=%d total=%d\n", i, count, total);Prints main's own loop counter, main's own count (after this iteration's updates), and main's own total — note this line runs after both function calls and the conditional above it, so it reflects everything that happened this iteration.
printf("Final: count=%d total=%d\n", count, total);Printed once, after the loop has completely finished, showing main's own final values.

Full trace table:

imain's count entering this iterationupdate() prints (A:count,*ptr,step)total after update()main's count after scale()i==2 extra: total += count?Main's printf this iteration
05 (no +10, that only happens when i==1)A:7,7,3 (update's local count=5+2=7; total=0+7=7; step=2+1=3)7scale(5)=5×2=10no (i is 0, not 2)i=0 count=10 total=7
15+10=20 (the i==1 bump fires here)A:22,29,3 (local count=20+2=22; total=7+22=29; step=3)29scale(20)=20×2=40noi=1 count=40 total=29
240 (i==1 bump does not fire again)A:42,71,3 (local count=40+2=42; total=29+42=71; step=3)71scale(40)=40×2=80yes: total = 71+80 = 151i=2 count=80 total=151

How to trace this table yourself, step by step (this is the general method for ANY Assignment-2-style tracing question):

  1. List every variable name first, before tracing any values. Here, the name count appears 4 separate times: the global, main's local, update's parameter, and scale's parameter. Give each one a mental label (count_global, count_main, count_update, count_scale) so you never confuse which one a bare count refers to inside each function's own body.
  2. Identify which variable is genuinely persistent. Only count_main and total survive from one loop iteration to the next, because they live in main's own scope, which isn't re-entered/reset every iteration (a for loop is still just one call to main). Every function-local variable (step, result, and each function's own count parameter) is destroyed and freshly re-created on every single call.
  3. Track the pointer's target once, and reuse it. p is set to &total once, at the very top, and never reassigned — so every *ptr inside update, for the entire program, means "main's total". If a program ever reassigns a pointer partway through (like Tab 3's Program 1), you would need to re-check the target after every reassignment instead.
  4. Step into a function call exactly where it's written, trace the whole function body with its own fresh locals, note its printf output in place, then step back out before continuing the very next line of the caller. Don't skip ahead.
  5. Distinguish "changes through a pointer" from "changes through a return value". update changes total permanently by writing through *ptr — main never has to do anything to receive this change. scale, in contrast, has no pointer parameter at all; the only way its result reaches main is because main explicitly writes count = scale(count);, replacing its own count with the returned value. If main had instead written scale(count); alone (ignoring the return value), main's count would never change from that call at all.
  6. Re-check every if against the CURRENT iteration's i — it's easy to accidentally carry the i==1 bump into i==2 as well if you're not careful; each condition is evaluated fresh, every single pass.

Full program output, in order:

A:7,7,3
i=0 count=10 total=7
A:22,29,3
i=1 count=40 total=29
A:42,71,3
i=2 count=80 total=151
Final: count=80 total=151
Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is deliberately built in Assignment 2 Q4's exact style: a decoy global that's never actually read, several functions each shadowing the same variable name with their own local/parameter copy, and a mix of two different ways information escapes a function — through a pointer write (update) and through a return value that the caller must explicitly capture (scale). The real test is whether you can keep four same-named variables straight without mixing up which one a bare count refers to at each point in the code.
Likely variations on the exam A function that takes a pointer but doesn't use it every time (conditionally writing through it), a return value that the caller ignores (in which case that function's work has no lasting effect at all), or an extra level of nesting where one helper function calls another helper function internally. Also watch for the global actually being used somewhere — if even one function omits its own local of that name, the global suddenly becomes live and must be tracked too.
How to approach it Follow the 6-step method in the box above: name every variable distinctly first, identify what actually persists across loop iterations (only main's own locals do), track any pointer's target, step into every call fully before returning to the caller, and always double check whether a function's effect reaches the caller via a pointer write or via a captured return value — these are the two mechanisms this whole topic is built around.
Pointer Tracing Lookalike 2 — base/score with two helper functions, freshly generated Assignment 2 Q4 style: return-and-capture vs write-through-pointer-only (void function)

Trace the program below and give its complete output. There are three separate variables named base in this program (plus the global) — identify each one before tracing any values.

#include <stdio.h>
int base = 1;

int adjust(int *val, int base) {
    base = base + 1;
    *val = *val - base;
    return base;
}

void grow(int n, int *acc) {
    int base = 10;
    *acc = *acc + n + base;
}

void main() {
    int i;
    int base = 0;
    int score = 20;
    int *ptr = &score;

    for (i = 1; i <= 3; i++) {
        base = base + i;
        if (i % 2 == 0) {
            base = adjust(ptr, base);
        } else {
            grow(i, ptr);
        }
        printf("i=%d base=%d score=%d\n", i, base, score);
    }
    printf("Done: base=%d score=%d\n", base, score);
}
#include <stdio.h>
int base = 1;

int adjust(int *val, int base) {
    base = base + 1;
    *val = *val - base;
    return base;
}

void grow(int n, int *acc) {
    int base = 10;
    *acc = *acc + n + base;
}

void main() {
    int i;
    int base = 0;
    int score = 20;
    int *ptr = &score;

    for (i = 1; i <= 3; i++) {
        base = base + i;
        if (i % 2 == 0) {
            base = adjust(ptr, base);
        } else {
            grow(i, ptr);
        }
        printf("i=%d base=%d score=%d\n", i, base, score);
    }
    printf("Done: base=%d score=%d\n", base, score);
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown — every line
int base = 1;A global variable. Every function below (main, adjust, and grow) declares its own local/parameter also named base, so this global value of 1 is a decoy and is never actually read anywhere in the program.
int adjust(int *val, int base) {Two parameters: val is a pointer (so this function can permanently modify whatever the caller points it at), and base is an ordinary by-value parameter — a fresh copy every call, unrelated to main's base.
base = base + 1;Modifies only this function's own local parameter copy of base; has no effect on main's base by itself.
*val = *val - base;Writes through the pointer: *val means "whatever variable val points to" — since adjust is always called with ptr, and ptr = &score, this line permanently reduces main's score by this function's local (already incremented) base.
return base;Sends this function's own local base value back to the caller — this is the only way adjust's internal base value can ever reach main, since the parameter itself is just a copy.
void grow(int n, int *acc) {Two parameters: n is an ordinary value, acc is a pointer (again always called with ptr, so it points at main's score).
int base = 10;A local variable, freshly created and reset to 10 on every call — the third, completely separate base in this program, unrelated to main's or adjust's.
*acc = *acc + n + base;Writes through the pointer, permanently increasing main's score by n plus this function's own local base (10). Note this function has no return statement at all (it's declared void) — its only way of affecting anything outside itself is this one pointer write.
int i;Main's loop counter.
int base = 0;Main's own local base, starting at 0 — the only base that persists across loop iterations.
int score = 20;Main's own running value, starting at 20.
int *ptr = &score;Points ptr at main's score, permanently — ptr is never reassigned anywhere else in the program.
for (i = 1; i <= 3; i++) {Runs the loop body for i = 1, 2, 3 — note this loop starts at 1, not 0, so the very first pass has i=1.
base = base + i;Main's own base is bumped by the current i, every single iteration, before either branch below runs.
if (i % 2 == 0) {Splits behaviour by whether i is even or odd.
base = adjust(ptr, base);Only on even i: calls adjust, and — critically — assigns whatever adjust returns back into main's own base. This is the only way adjust's internal changes to its local base reach main.
} else {The alternative branch, for odd i.
grow(i, ptr);Only on odd i: calls grow, but does not assign any return value back to main's base (grow is void anyway, it has nothing to return) — so main's own base is completely untouched by this call; only score changes, through the pointer.
printf("i=%d base=%d score=%d\n", i, base, score);Prints main's own current values, after whichever branch above has already run for this iteration.
printf("Done: base=%d score=%d\n", base, score);Printed once, after the loop, showing main's final values.

Full trace table:

ibase after base+=ibranch takenbase after branchscore after branch
1 (odd)0+1=1grow(1, ptr): local base=10; score = 20+1+10 = 31; grow returns nothing1 (grow never touches main's base)31
2 (even)1+2=3adjust(ptr,3): local base=3+1=4; score=31−4=27; returns 4, which main assigns into its own base427
3 (odd)4+3=7grow(3, ptr): local base=10; score=27+3+10=407 (unchanged by grow, again)40

How to trace this table yourself:

  1. Same first step as always: list the distinct variables. There are 4 things called base here (global, main, adjust's parameter, grow's local) — keep them mentally separate before tracing a single value.
  2. Notice the asymmetry between the two functions: adjust returns a value AND main captures it (base = adjust(...)), so adjust's internal base genuinely reaches main. grow is void — it has no return value at all, so main's own base is guaranteed untouched by any call to grow, no matter what grow does internally.
  3. Both functions DO permanently change score, because both receive ptr and write through it. Writing through a pointer and returning a value are two completely separate escape routes out of a function — a function can use either one, both, or neither.
  4. Process one full iteration at a time, top to bottom: first the unconditional base = base + i;, then exactly one of the two branches (never both, since it's if/else), then the printf. Only then move to the next value of i.
  5. Double-check the loop bounds: this loop starts at i=1 and runs through i=3 inclusive (i <= 3), so it's 3 passes total, not 4 — an easy detail to misread under time pressure.

Full program output, in order:

i=1 base=1 score=31
i=2 base=4 score=27
i=3 base=7 score=40
Done: base=7 score=40
Claude explains
What this question is really asking This lookalike specifically drills the difference between a function that returns a value the caller must capture (adjust, whose internal changes only reach main because main writes base = adjust(...)) and a function whose only effect is through a pointer write (grow, which is void and cannot return anything at all). Mixing these two up — assuming grow's call also changes main's base, or forgetting that adjust's return must be captured — is exactly the mistake this question is designed to catch.
Likely variations on the exam A version where main calls adjust(ptr, base); WITHOUT capturing the return value (in which case main's base would never change from that call, only score would), or a version where grow is changed to also return something that main ignores or captures. Also watch for the branch condition changing from odd/even to some other test.
How to approach it Whenever a function call's result is assigned back (x = func(...)), that function's local changes to its own copy of a variable CAN reach the caller — but only through that captured return value, never automatically. Whenever a call is made WITHOUT capturing anything (func(...); alone), the only way it can still affect the caller is through a pointer parameter it was given. Check every function signature for a void return type first — that immediately tells you it has no return-value escape route at all, only (if any) a pointer one.

Master pattern 1: Digit-split structs

Final exam topic

A1–A4 on the practice sheet. This is almost certainly your Structures question.

Every one of A1–A4 stores several logical fields inside one integer member of the struct, using fixed digit widths. The entire question hinges on correctly slicing that integer back into its named parts before any filtering or counting can even begin.

The universal 3-step recipe

  1. Draw the layout on paper first. Write the digit positions out left to right with labels, e.g. Y Y D D D S S S for an 8-digit registration number (2 digits year, 3 digits department, 3 digits serial).
  2. Convert each field's width to a power of 10. A 3-digit field width is 1000 (10³); a 2-digit field width is 100 (10²); and so on.
  3. Apply the extraction formula for that field's position:
    • Field flush against the left end → number / 10(everything to its right)
    • Field flush against the right end → number % 10(the field's own width)
    • Field in the middle → (number / 10(everything to its right)) % 10(the field's own width)
A1's registration number, all three fields extracted
int year = 2000 + regNum / 1000000;      /* leftmost 2 digits  */
int dept  = (regNum / 1000) % 1000;       /* middle 3 digits    */
int roll  = regNum % 1000;                /* rightmost 3 digits */
Professor's explanation

For an 8-digit regNum like 22101045: the year is flush left, so divide by 10⁶ (everything else: 6 digits) → 22. The department is in the middle with 3 digits still to its right, so divide by 10³ first (22101045/1000 = 22101), then % 1000 keeps only its own 3 digits (101). The roll serial is flush right, so it's just % 1000 directly (045).

When you need every digit, not a fixed slice (A4-style checksums)

If the rule involves summing, weighting, or otherwise combining every digit (not a clean fixed-width field), fall back to the digit-peeling loop instead:

int n = accNum, lastDigit = accNum % 10;
int sumAll = 0;
while (n > 0) {
    sumAll += n % 10;
    n = n / 10;
}
int sumFirst7 = sumAll - lastDigit;   /* sum everything, then subtract out what you don't want */

This "sum everything, then subtract the part you don't want" trick is far less error-prone than trying to write a loop that stops one digit early.

Pitfalls that cost marks in this pattern

  • Using the wrong power of 10 — always count the digits physically on paper rather than guessing.
  • Forgetting that the filter input and the extracted field must be compared in the same representation (both as 2-digit years, or both as full 4-digit years, never mixed).
  • Recomputing the same extraction inside every condition instead of storing it once in a clearly-named variable at the top of the loop body.
  • Forgetting the running counters (found-count, before/after-count, running total, running best) that almost every one of these questions asks for in addition to the printed list.

Master pattern 2: Slab-rate & growing-array DMA

Final exam topic

B1–B4 on the practice sheet. Expect one or two of these as your DMA question(s).

Part B splits cleanly into two DMA sub-patterns. Learn to recognise which one a question wants within the first few seconds of reading it.

Sub-pattern 2a: slab-rate (tiered) calculation — B1, B2

A quantity is charged/taxed at different rates depending on which bracket it falls into, with every bracket except the last one capped at its own width.

B1's taxi fare, the slab ladder
float fare;
if (km <= 5) {
    fare = 60;
} else if (km <= 15) {
    fare = 60 + (km - 5) * 22;
} else {
    fare = 60 + 10 * 22 + (km - 15) * 18;
}
Professor's explanation

Each branch builds on the full charge of every bracket below it, adding only the portion of the input that falls inside the current bracket. Write the branches from the bottom bracket upward, and always subtract the bracket's own starting point before multiplying by its rate.

A slab problem with 4+ brackets (like B2's income tax) is cleaner written with a running tax variable and an early return the moment income doesn't reach the next bracket, rather than one giant if/else if chain — see the full worked solution in Tab 4.

Sub-pattern 2b: growing arrays with realloc — B3, B4

B3 style (unknown count)B4 style (known increment)
How much to grow byFixed increment (+5), repeated as neededExact amount M, asked for directly
When to growInside a loop, checked every time before storingOnce, right after asking "how many more?"
Stopping conditionA sentinel value (e.g. -1) that is itself never storedNo sentinel — the count M is exact

Read the question's exact wording carefully to know which of these two you're in: "keeps reading until the user enters X" is always sentinel-style (B3); "ask how many more, then read that many" is always known-increment style (B4).

Pitfalls that cost marks in this pattern

  • Forgetting malloc/calloc/realloc's NULL check — a required line even when you're confident it will succeed.
  • In slab problems: forgetting to cap a middle bracket's width before multiplying by its rate (the classic injected bug — see B2's Claude Explains note in Tab 4).
  • In sentinel-growth problems: checking count == capacity after storing the new value instead of before, which writes one element past the allocated block.
  • In known-increment problems: starting the fill loop for new elements at index 0 instead of at the old count, overwriting data that was already entered.
  • Forgetting free() at the end of any DMA program.

Master pattern 3: The string-walk toolkit

Final exam topic

C1–C9 on the practice sheet. Low mark value, high speed-to-completion — don't overspend time here.

All 9 string problems (worth 6 marks each in practice, likely the exam's bonus slot) reduce to combinations of just four primitives. Master these four cold and every bonus question becomes a fast, easy win.

PrimitiveShapeUsed directly in
Length-findwhile (str[len] != '\0') len++;C3, and as a first step inside C4
Character classifyASCII range checks (>='a' && <='z', etc.) in an if/else-if ladderC2, C5, C7
Word-start teststr[i]!=' ' && (i==0 || str[i-1]==' ')C1, C8, C9
Two-pointer / filter-then-checkBuild a clean copy (skip/convert characters), then compare from both ends inwardC4

How the exam is likely to recombine these

C8 (acronym) is literally "the word-start test, but print-and-uppercase instead of count". C9 (sentences) is literally "the word-start test, plus an unrelated punctuation count, tallied in the same loop". Once you recognise a new-looking bonus question as a recombination of these four primitives, the actual coding is fast.

Pitfalls that cost marks in this pattern

  • Counting separators and adding one, instead of using the word-start test — breaks immediately on double/leading/trailing spaces (the practice sheet's C1 deliberately tests for exactly this mistake).
  • Reading a single character with a bare scanf("%c", &ch) after an earlier scanf call, instead of scanf(" %c", &ch) with the leading space.
  • Building an unnecessary second buffer when the filtered result only needs to be printed once (use continue to skip instead, as in C7).
  • Forgetting that str[len] itself is the null terminator, not a real character — the last real character is always at index len - 1.

Tracing prep — resolving the “practice A2” ambiguity

Final exam topic

Cover all three angles so the ambiguity can't cost you.

Your faculty's syllabus for the final is Lecture-7 through Lecture-11 (Arrays, Sorting, Strings, Pointers, DMA, Structures) and the exam is confirmed to include exactly one tracing question. The practice sheet itself contains no dedicated "trace this program" question — Parts A/B/C are all coding tasks — which strongly suggests the tracing question draws on Pointers (Lecture 9), matching everything already built out in this file.

Resolving the "practice A2" ambiguity

A classmate asked for tracing practice and your faculty pointed to "A2" — which could mean either the practice sheet's own A2 (Medicine batch), or Assignment 2's Q4 (the global/pointer/function tracing question already fully solved in Tab 3). Since there's no way to be 100% certain which was meant, prepare for both, in this order:

  1. Assignment 2, Q4 (Tab 3) — a genuine tracing question: globals shadowed by locals, a pointer parameter, a function with its own shadowed total. This is the closest match to "a tracing question" in the literal sense, and is fully worked with a step-by-step table in Tab 3.
  2. Practice sheet A2 (Tab 4) — not a tracing question by format, but its date-arithmetic logic (year*12+month subtraction) is exactly the kind of multi-step derived-value reasoning a tracing question could adapt into a "what does this print" format. Make sure you can compute the age-in-months formula by hand, quickly, for any two dates.
  3. The dedicated Pointer tracing worked examples (Tab 3) and the new pointer-tracing lookalikes in Part D (Tab 4) — these remain your core preparation for the actual tracing question format: a short program, a line-by-line state table, and an exact printed output.

The tracing method, one more time

  • Draw a table: one column per variable in the program, one row per statement executed, in order.
  • The instant a pointer is reassigned, relabel what *p means from that point on.
  • Any parameter or local variable that shares a name with a global or with a variable in another function is a separate variable unless it was explicitly passed by address.
  • A statement that both reads and writes the same variable (e.g. c = *p + b; where p points at c) always uses the old value on the right-hand side, computed in full before the assignment happens.

Exam-day strategy

Core topic

Suggested time budget (2-hour paper, 11:00 AM–1:00 PM)

  • ~5 min — skim the whole paper, confirm the shape matches what you prepared for.
  • ~35 min — the Structures question (digit-split pattern): highest and most predictable value, do it while fresh.
  • ~35 min — the DMA question(s) (slab-rate or growing-array): second priority, same reasoning.
  • ~20 min — the tracing question: needs focus, not speed — do it once warmed up but before fatigue.
  • ~10–15 min — the string bonus: fast, mechanical, do it whenever you have a spare pocket of time, including right at the end.
  • ~10 min — final check: re-read every printed format string against the sample output character-by-character.

Order-of-attack advice

Do the highest-value, most-predictable questions first — Structures and DMA are now extremely well-characterised by the practice sheet, so there should be very few surprises there. If a question's premise still feels unfamiliar, identify which of the 3 master patterns above it's closest to and adapt from that skeleton rather than starting from scratch.

Common pitfalls across every category

  • Missing a required & in scanf, or adding one where it doesn't belong (arrays/strings never need it; ordinary variables and struct members always do).
  • Wrong power of 10 in a digit-extraction formula — always count digits on paper first.
  • Loop boundaries off by one (<= vs <).
  • Not checking malloc/calloc/realloc against NULL.
  • Skipping free() at the end of a DMA program.
  • Wrong format specifier for a variable's actual type in printf/scanf.
