  • Structures (4)
  • Dynamic Memory (4)
  • Strings (4)
  • Pointer Tracing (4)

Tab 7 — Solved Example Problems: 16 questions (4 categories × 4 variations)

Exactly 4 categories — Structures, Dynamic Memory, Strings, Pointer Tracing — each with the 4 most probable variations of that category's question type, one fresh question per variation. Every one is solved in full with a line-by-line breakdown and a "Claude explains" section; the pointer tracing questions also include a complete trace table. Each variation is explicitly labelled with which official practice-sheet problem (A1–A4, B1–B4, C1/C2/C4/C8) or tracing style it mirrors, so you can see exactly which skill it's testing.

Structures — 4 most probable variations

Structures Variation 1 — digit-split filter [12] mirrors A1

Variation 1 — basic digit-split filter (mirrors A1). A book's 7-digit code packs its category (1st digit: 1=Fiction, 2=Non-fiction, 3=Academic) and its publish year's last 2 digits (last 2 digits of the code).

Store the records, take a category id C as input, print the title and price of every book in category C, and count how many of those were published in 2024 or later.

TitleCodePrice
The Long Walk1042023450.00
Circuit Basics3120025620.00
Quiet Markets2033024380.00
Signals & Systems3098026710.00
Short Fiction Vol.21077025290.00
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char title[30];
    int code;
    float price;
} Book;

int main() {
    Book books[5] = {
        {"The Long Walk", 1042023, 450.00},
        {"Circuit Basics", 3120025, 620.00},
        {"Quiet Markets", 2033024, 380.00},
        {"Signals & Systems", 3098026, 710.00},
        {"Short Fiction Vol.2", 1077025, 290.00}
    };
    int c, i, recent = 0;

    printf("Enter category id: ");
    scanf("%d", &c);

    for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        int category = books[i].code / 1000000;
        int yearSuffix = books[i].code % 100;

        if (category == c) {
            printf("%s - %.2f\n", books[i].title, books[i].price);
            if (yearSuffix >= 24) recent++;
        }
    }
    printf("Published 2024 or later: %d\n", recent);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
typedef struct { char title[30]; int code; float price; } Book;Defines the Book type: a name string, a 7-digit packed code stored as a plain int, and a price.
Book books[5] = { ... };Declares and fills the array of 5 given records in one statement.
int c, i, recent = 0;c holds the user's chosen category; i is the loop counter; recent is a tally started at 0.
scanf("%d", &c);Reads the category filter into c.
for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) {Loops over all 5 books.
int category = books[i].code / 1000000;First digit of the 7-digit code → divide by 106, since six digits remain to its right.
int yearSuffix = books[i].code % 100;Last 2 digits, flush against the right end → plain % 100, no division needed.
if (category == c) {Only books in the requested category enter this block.
printf("%s - %.2f\n", books[i].title, books[i].price);Prints the title and price of this matching book.
if (yearSuffix >= 24) recent++;A second, nested tally: only matching books published in 2024 or later (suffix 24+) increase this count.
printf("Published 2024 or later: %d\n", recent);Printed once, after the loop, with the final tally.
Worked example — watch it happen with real numbers

Take Circuit Basics's record: code = 3120025.

What we're findingThe exact expressionWhat integer division/modulo actually does hereValue produced
Extract category3120025 / 10000003120025 ÷ 1000000 = 3.120025 → kept as 33 (Academic)
Extract yearSuffix3120025 % 100The remainder after dividing by 100 is the last 2 digits25

So this book is category 3 (Academic) and its yearSuffix is 25. If the user enters category 3, this book prints, and since 25 >= 24, it also counts toward "Published 2024 or later".

Identical shape to A1: a packed code with a left-flush field (category) and a right-flush field (year suffix), one equality filter, one nested derived count.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking The simplest member of the digit-split family: one field flush against the left end, one flush against the right end, combined with one equality filter and one nested count. This is the baseline pattern every other Structures variation builds on.
Likely variations on the exam Different digit widths, a 3rd field added in the middle, or the filter changed from equality to a range test.
How to approach it Extract each field with Tab 2's digit-extraction moves (left-flush: divide by 10digits-to-keep; right-flush: mod 10digits-to-keep), then run the ordinary filter-and-count loop.
Structures Variation 2 — digit-split into date arithmetic [12] mirrors A2

Variation 2 — digit-split feeding date arithmetic (mirrors A2). A gym membership code is a 6-digit integer: first 2 digits = start year, next 2 = start month, last 2 = a member serial (e.g. 240815 → started August 2024). Every membership is valid for exactly 12 months from its start date.

Store the records, take the current year and month as input (for September 2026, enter 26 9), print the name/start date/age in months of every expired membership, then the expired count and how many total months of membership fees were lost (assume each expired membership loses (age − 12) months worth of the given monthly fee).

NameCodeMonthly fee
Tania Rahman2408151500.00
Objoy Sarkar2502031800.00
Mahi Noor2306111200.00
Rafid Islam2601072000.00
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int code;
    float fee;
} Member;

int main() {
    Member members[4] = {
        {"Tania Rahman", 240815, 1500.00},
        {"Objoy Sarkar", 250203, 1800.00},
        {"Mahi Noor", 230611, 1200.00},
        {"Rafid Islam", 260107, 2000.00}
    };
    int curY, curM, i, expiredCount = 0;
    float feesLost = 0;

    printf("Enter current year and month: ");
    scanf("%d %d", &curY, &curM);

    printf("Expired memberships:\n");
    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        int startYY = members[i].code / 10000;
        int startMM = (members[i].code / 100) % 100;
        int ageMonths = (curY * 12 + curM) - (startYY * 12 + startMM);

        if (ageMonths > 12) {
            printf("%s started 20%02d-%02d age %d months\n",
                   members[i].name, startYY, startMM, ageMonths);
            expiredCount++;
            feesLost += (ageMonths - 12) * members[i].fee;
        }
    }

    printf("Total expired: %d\n", expiredCount);
    printf("Fees lost : %.2f Taka\n", feesLost);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
typedef struct { char name[30]; int code; float fee; } Member;Defines the Member type: name, packed 6-digit code, monthly fee.
Member members[4] = { ... };Declares and fills the given array in one statement.
int curY, curM, i, expiredCount = 0;Current year/month inputs, loop counter, and a tally started at 0.
float feesLost = 0;Running total of lost fees, started at 0.
scanf("%d %d", &curY, &curM);Reads two integers in one call.
for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {Loops over all 4 members.
int startYY = members[i].code / 10000;First 2 digits of the 6-digit code (left-flush) → divide by 104.
int startMM = (members[i].code / 100) % 100;Middle 2 digits: divide by 100 to drop the serial, then % 100 keeps only the month.
int ageMonths = (curY*12+curM) - (startYY*12+startMM);Converts both dates to total months and subtracts — the standard date-difference trick.
if (ageMonths > 12) {Expired only once membership age exceeds the 12-month validity — strictly greater than, since exactly 12 months is still (just barely) valid.
printf("%s started 20%02d-%02d age %d months\n", ...);Reports the member, their formatted start date (%02d zero-pads to 2 digits), and their age.
expiredCount++;Tallies this expired membership.
feesLost += (ageMonths - 12) * members[i].fee;Only the months beyond the valid 12 are counted as "lost" — multiplied by the monthly fee to get a Taka amount.
printf("Total expired: %d\n", expiredCount);Final tally.
printf("Fees lost : %.2f Taka\n", feesLost);Final accumulated loss.
Worked example — watch it happen with real numbers

Take Tania Rahman's record: code = 240815, with the user entering 26 9 (September 2026) as the current date.

What we're findingThe exact expressionWhat integer division/modulo actually does hereValue produced
Extract startYY240815 / 10000240815 ÷ 10000 = 24.0815 → kept as 2424 (year 2024)
Extract startMM (step 1)240815 / 100240815 ÷ 100 = 2408.15 → kept as 24082408
Extract startMM (step 2)2408 % 100Keeps only the last 2 digits of 24088 (month 08, August)
Compute ageMonths(26×12+9) − (24×12+8)current = 312+9 = 321; start = 288+8 = 296321 − 296 = 25

So this membership started in 2024-08 and is now 25 months old. Since 25 > 12 (the 12-month validity period), it has expired, and the "lost fee" is charged for the 25 − 12 = 13 overdue months, not the full 25.

Same two-stage shape as A2: extract fields, feed them into the year*12+month date-difference formula, then filter/accumulate. The only new twist is the "lost fees" calculation multiplying the overdue months (not the full age) by the fee.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests the date-arithmetic chain (digit-split → age formula → filter) plus one extra step: computing a value proportional to how much the threshold was exceeded, not just whether it was exceeded.
Likely variations on the exam The validity period changing, the "lost fee" formula using the full age instead of just the overdue portion, or being asked for still-valid memberships instead of expired ones.
How to approach it Extract fields, compute total months for both dates, subtract, then compare against the threshold. For any "amount by which a limit was exceeded" calculation, subtract the threshold from the actual value first, then multiply by whatever per-unit rate applies.
Structures Variation 3 — running-best + manual string copy [12] mirrors A3

Variation 3 — running-best tracking + manual string copy (mirrors A3). A student ID is a 5-digit integer: first digit is year of joining (relative, e.g. 1 = first year), last digit is the student's club membership level (1–4). Attendance bonus points = level × 10% of the student's total quiz score.

Store the records, take a year Y as input, print name/level/bonus for every student who joined on or after year Y, then print the name of the student with the highest bonus among them.

NameIDQuiz score
Priya Das2052188
Kabir Chowdhury1034295
Farin Alam3041170
Tahmid Reza2015382
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int id;
    float score;
} Student;

int main() {
    Student students[4] = {
        {"Priya Das", 20521, 88},
        {"Kabir Chowdhury", 10342, 95},
        {"Farin Alam", 30411, 70},
        {"Tahmid Reza", 20153, 82}
    };
    int Y, i;
    float maxBonus = -1;
    char maxName[30] = "";

    printf("Enter year Y: ");
    scanf("%d", &Y);

    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        int joinYear = students[i].id / 10000;
        int level = students[i].id % 10;

        if (joinYear >= Y) {
            float bonus = level * 0.10 * students[i].score;
            printf("%s level %d bonus %.2f\n", students[i].name, level, bonus);

            if (bonus > maxBonus) {
                maxBonus = bonus;
                int k = 0;
                while (students[i].name[k] != '\0') { maxName[k] = students[i].name[k]; k++; }
                maxName[k] = '\0';
            }
        }
    }

    printf("Highest bonus: %s (%.2f)\n", maxName, maxBonus);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
typedef struct { char name[30]; int id; float score; } Student;Defines the Student type.
Student students[4] = { ... };Declares and fills the given array.
int Y, i;Input year threshold, and the loop counter.
float maxBonus = -1;Tracks the highest bonus so far, started at -1 (not 0) so any real, non-negative bonus is guaranteed to beat it on the first eligible student.
char maxName[30] = "";Will hold a manual copy of the current best student's name, started empty.
scanf("%d", &Y);Reads the year threshold.
for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {Loops over all 4 students.
int joinYear = students[i].id / 10000;First digit of the 5-digit ID (left-flush, 1 digit wide) → divide by 104.
int level = students[i].id % 10;Last digit (right-flush, 1 digit) → plain % 10.
if (joinYear >= Y) {Only students who joined on or after Y enter this block — note >=, not >, since "on or after" includes equality.
float bonus = level * 0.10 * students[i].score;Applies the bonus formula directly.
printf("%s level %d bonus %.2f\n", ...);Reports this student's details.
if (bonus > maxBonus) {Only students who beat the current recorded best enter this inner block.
maxBonus = bonus;Updates the record.
int k = 0; while (students[i].name[k] != '\0') { maxName[k]=students[i].name[k]; k++; } maxName[k]='\0';Manual character-by-character copy of the new best student's name into maxName, ending with the required '\0' terminator.
printf("Highest bonus: %s (%.2f)\n", maxName, maxBonus);Printed once, after the loop, using the final recorded best.
Worked example — watch it happen with real numbers

Take Priya Das's record: id = 20521.

What we're findingThe exact expressionWhy it worksValue produced
Extract joinYear20521 / 1000020521 ÷ 10000 = 2.0521 → kept as 22
Extract level20521 % 10The remainder after dividing by 10 is the last digit1
Compute bonus1 × 0.10 × 88level × 10% × quiz score8.80

So Priya joined in year 2 with club level 1, earning a bonus of 8.80. With input Y = 2, she passes the filter joinYear >= Y (2 >= 2), exactly matching the sample output Priya Das level 1 bonus 8.80.

Same shape as A3: digit-split filter combined with a running-maximum tracker, where the "winner" is a string field that must be copied manually since C cannot assign one char array to another with =.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests the combination of digit-split filtering with "track the best record seen so far", specifically when the tracked "best" needs an associated string field carried along with it — which is why the manual copy loop is unavoidable here.
Likely variations on the exam The filter direction reversed (before Y instead of on/after), the field widths changed, or the "best" criterion changed to a minimum instead of a maximum (in which case the starting sentinel would need to be a very large number instead of -1).
How to approach it Always seed a running-maximum tracker with a value guaranteed to lose to the first real candidate (a bonus/score is never negative here, so -1 is safe). Whenever the tracked best carries a string, copy it manually the instant a new best is confirmed.
Structures Variation 4 — checksum validation [12] mirrors A4

Variation 4 — checksum/validation via digit-peel (mirrors A4). The last digit of a 7-digit library card number is a check digit: valid when it equals the sum of the first 6 digits, modulo 10 (e.g. 1234561 is valid because 1+2+3+4+5+6=21, 21%10=1).

Store the records, print every invalid card (showing expected vs actual check digit), then the invalid count and the total number of books currently borrowed on valid cards only.

NameCard numberBooks borrowed
Nabil Hasan12345612
Ayesha Siddiqua20457813
Rezwan Karim50607081
Mitu Akter99887744
#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct {
    char name[30];
    int cardNum;
    int booksBorrowed;
} Card;

int main() {
    Card cards[4] = {
        {"Nabil Hasan", 1234561, 2},
        {"Ayesha Siddiqua", 2045781, 3},
        {"Rezwan Karim", 5060708, 1},
        {"Mitu Akter", 9988774, 4}
    };
    int i, invalidCount = 0, validBooks = 0;

    printf("Invalid cards:\n");
    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        int n = cards[i].cardNum;
        int lastDigit = n % 10;
        int temp = n, sumAll = 0;

        while (temp > 0) {
            sumAll += temp % 10;
            temp = temp / 10;
        }
        int sumFirst6 = sumAll - lastDigit;
        int expected = sumFirst6 % 10;

        if (expected != lastDigit) {
            printf("%s %d (expected %d, found %d)\n",
                   cards[i].name, cards[i].cardNum, expected, lastDigit);
            invalidCount++;
        } else {
            validBooks += cards[i].booksBorrowed;
        }
    }

    printf("Invalid count : %d\n", invalidCount);
    printf("Valid-card books borrowed : %d\n", validBooks);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
typedef struct { char name[30]; int cardNum; int booksBorrowed; } Card;Defines the Card type.
Card cards[4] = { ... };Declares and fills the given array.
int i, invalidCount = 0, validBooks = 0;Loop counter and two tallies, both started at 0.
for (i = 0; i < 4; i++) {Loops over all 4 cards.
int n = cards[i].cardNum;Working copy of this card's number, so the digit-peeling below can freely consume it.
int lastDigit = n % 10;Grabs the check digit before the peeling loop destroys it.
int temp = n, sumAll = 0;A second working copy plus a sum accumulator, started at 0.
while (temp > 0) { sumAll += temp % 10; temp = temp / 10; }Digit-peeling loop: adds the current last digit onto the sum, then removes it — sums all 7 digits, including the check digit itself.
int sumFirst6 = sumAll - lastDigit;Subtracts the check digit back out of the total, leaving exactly the sum of the first 6 — avoids a fiddlier loop that stops one digit early.
int expected = sumFirst6 % 10;Applies the validation rule: what the check digit should be.
if (expected != lastDigit) {True only for invalid cards.
printf("%s %d (expected %d, found %d)\n", ...);Reports the mismatch.
invalidCount++;Tallies this invalid card.
} else { validBooks += cards[i].booksBorrowed; }For valid cards only, adds this card's borrowed-book count onto the running total.
printf("Invalid count : %d\n", invalidCount);Final tally.
printf("Valid-card books borrowed : %d\n", validBooks);Final accumulated total, from valid cards only.
Worked example — watch the digit-peeling loop happen, one iteration at a time

Take Nabil Hasan's record: cardNum = 1234561. First, lastDigit = 1234561 % 10 = 1 is grabbed and set aside. Then the while (temp > 0) loop peels every digit of temp (starting as a copy of the full number, 1234561):

Pass #temp at start of this passdigit peeled (temp % 10)sumAll after adding ittemp after (temp / 10)
112345611234561 % 10 = 10 + 1 = 11234561 / 10 = 123456
2123456123456 % 10 = 61 + 6 = 7123456 / 10 = 12345
31234512345 % 10 = 57 + 5 = 1212345 / 10 = 1234
412341234 % 10 = 412 + 4 = 161234 / 10 = 123
5123123 % 10 = 316 + 3 = 19123 / 10 = 12
61212 % 10 = 219 + 2 = 2112 / 10 = 1
711 % 10 = 121 + 1 = 221 / 10 = 0 → loop stops

The loop ends with sumAll = 22 (sum of all 7 digits, including the check digit). Then: sumFirst6 = 22 − 1 = 21, and expected = 21 % 10 = 1. Since expected (1) == lastDigit (1), this card is valid — matching the sample output, where Nabil Hasan's card does not appear in the "Invalid cards" list.

Identical shape to A4: the check digit forces a full digit-peel (every digit needed, not a fixed slice), and the same "sum everything, subtract the one digit you don't want" trick avoids writing a second, fiddlier loop.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Confirms whether you recognise a checksum question purely from the rule description ("the check digit equals some function of the other digits") and reach immediately for digit-peeling rather than trying to force a fixed-width extraction onto it.
Likely variations on the exam A different checksum rule (weighted sum, alternating sign), a different digit count, or the check digit at the front instead of the back.
How to approach it Peel every digit with a while loop, sum them all (including the check digit), then subtract the check digit back out to isolate the sum of the rest — always grab any digit you need to keep before the peeling loop consumes it.

Dynamic Memory — 4 most probable variations

DMA Variation 1 — slab-rate with malloc [10] mirrors B1

Variation 1 — slab-rate with malloc (mirrors B1). Electricity bill slabs (per unit): first 100 units → 4 Taka/unit; next 200 units (101–300) → 6 Taka/unit; above 300 units → 9 Taka/unit. A fixed service charge of 50 Taka is added to every bill.

Write calculateBill(units). In main(): take the number of households N, malloc N unit-readings, read them, print each bill, then the total revenue and how many households paid more than 1000 Taka.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

float calculateBill(int units) {
    float bill;
    if (units <= 100) {
        bill = units * 4;
    } else if (units <= 300) {
        bill = 100 * 4 + (units - 100) * 6;
    } else {
        bill = 100 * 4 + 200 * 6 + (units - 300) * 9;
    }
    bill = bill + 50;
    return bill;
}

int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Number of households: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *units = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (units == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("Units for household %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%d", &units[i]);
    }

    float total = 0;
    int highCount = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        float bill = calculateBill(units[i]);
        printf("Household %d: %d units -> %.2f Taka\n", i + 1, units[i], bill);
        total += bill;
        if (bill > 1000) highCount++;
    }

    printf("Total revenue: %.2f Taka\n", total);
    printf("Households above 1000 Taka: %d\n", highCount);
    free(units);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float calculateBill(int units) {A helper function, mirroring B1's calculateFare exactly — one ride/unit reading in, one price out.
if (units <= 100) bill = units * 4;Flat-rate band: every unit inside the first 100 costs 4 Taka.
else if (units <= 300) bill = 100*4 + (units-100)*6;Full first-band charge, plus only units beyond 100 at the second rate.
else bill = 100*4 + 200*6 + (units-300)*9;Both lower bands full, plus only units beyond 300 at the top rate.
bill = bill + 50;The fixed service charge, added once, unconditionally, after the ladder.
return bill;Sends the fully-computed bill back to the caller.
int *units = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));Standard DMA-array allocation, sized for exactly n households.
if (units == NULL) { ... return 1; }Guards against a failed allocation.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) { scanf("%d", &units[i]); }Reads each household's unit reading directly into the dynamically-allocated array.
float bill = calculateBill(units[i]);Calls the helper on this household's reading.
total += bill; if (bill > 1000) highCount++;Accumulates the grand total, and separately tallies bills exceeding 1000 Taka — two independent pieces of bookkeeping in the same pass.
free(units);Releases the allocated memory at the end.

The exact B1 slab-rate skeleton, wrapped in the exact malloc-fill-process-free DMA skeleton — nothing new here beyond combining two patterns you already know.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Confirms you can build the slab ladder from scratch for a brand-new set of rates, and wrap it in the standard DMA skeleton without needing either pattern re-explained.
Likely variations on the exam Extra bands, different per-unit rates, or the service charge waived for very low usage (an extra condition before adding it).
How to approach it Build the slab ladder bottom-up first, then wrap the standard malloc/read/process/free DMA shell around it.
DMA Variation 2 — many-bracket slab-rate with calloc [10] mirrors B2

Variation 2 — many-bracket slab-rate with calloc + early return (mirrors B2). Sales commission slabs: first 50,000 Taka in sales → 0% commission; next 100,000 (50,001–150,000) → 3%; next 200,000 (150,001–350,000) → 6%; remaining sales above 350,000 → 10%.

Write calculateCommission(sales). In main(): take the number of salespeople N, calloc N sales figures, read them, print each commission, then how many earned zero commission and the total commission paid out.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

float calculateCommission(float sales) {
    float comm = 0;
    if (sales <= 50000) return 0;

    float slab2 = sales - 50000;
    if (slab2 > 100000) slab2 = 100000;
    comm += slab2 * 0.03;
    if (sales <= 150000) return comm;

    float slab3 = sales - 150000;
    if (slab3 > 200000) slab3 = 200000;
    comm += slab3 * 0.06;
    if (sales <= 350000) return comm;

    float slab4 = sales - 350000;
    comm += slab4 * 0.10;
    return comm;
}

int main() {
    int n, i;
    printf("Number of salespeople: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    float *sales = (float *)calloc(n, sizeof(float));
    if (sales == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("Sales of person %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%f", &sales[i]);
    }

    int zeroCount = 0;
    float totalComm = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        float comm = calculateCommission(sales[i]);
        printf("Person %d: sales %.2f -> commission %.2f\n", i + 1, sales[i], comm);
        if (comm == 0) zeroCount++;
        totalComm += comm;
    }

    printf("Zero-commission salespeople: %d\n", zeroCount);
    printf("Total commission paid : %.2f Taka\n", totalComm);
    free(sales);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
float calculateCommission(float sales) {A helper function mirroring B2's calculateTax.
if (sales <= 50000) return 0;The 0% bracket exits immediately with a direct return.
slab2 = sales-50000; if (slab2>100000) slab2=100000;How much falls in bracket 2, capped at that bracket's own width (100,000).
comm += slab2 * 0.03; if (sales<=150000) return comm;Adds 3% of the (capped) slice, then exits if sales don't reach bracket 3.
slab3 = sales-150000; if (slab3>200000) slab3=200000;How much falls in bracket 3, capped at its own width (200,000).
comm += slab3 * 0.06; if (sales<=350000) return comm;Adds 6% of the (capped) slice, then exits if sales don't reach the final bracket.
slab4 = sales-350000; comm += slab4 * 0.10;The final, uncapped bracket: everything above 350,000 taxed at 10% with no upper limit.
float *sales = (float *)calloc(n, sizeof(float));Allocates n floats, zero-initialised, with count and size as two separate calloc arguments.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) { scanf("%f", &sales[i]); }Reads each salesperson's figure into the array.
float comm = calculateCommission(sales[i]); if (comm==0) zeroCount++; totalComm += comm;Computes each commission, tallies zero-commission earners, and accumulates the grand total — all in one loop pass.
free(sales);Releases the allocated memory.

The exact B2 four-bracket, early-return slab shape, applied to a fresh set of boundaries and rates — the calloc + zero-commission-counting wrapper is also identical in spirit to B2's zero-tax counting.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests whether the early-return slab style (needed once there are more than 2–3 brackets) generalises cleanly to a completely different set of numbers, without the pattern being re-taught.
Likely variations on the exam A different number of brackets, or the cap on a middle bracket forgotten as a deliberately injected bug in a tracing-style question.
How to approach it Subtract the bracket's own starting point, cap at the bracket's width unless it's the final open-ended one, add that slice's charge, and return immediately if the value doesn't reach the next bracket.
DMA Variation 3 — sentinel-controlled growing array [12] mirrors B3

Variation 3 — sentinel-controlled growing array (mirrors B3). A weather station logs temperature readings (in °C) throughout the day. Allocate space for 4 readings to begin with. Keep reading temperatures until the user enters -999 (a sentinel, not stored or counted). Whenever the array becomes full, enlarge it by 4 more places using realloc, printing the new capacity. Finally print all readings, final capacity, average, highest reading, and how many readings were below the average.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int capacity = 4, count = 0;
    float *temps = (float *)malloc(capacity * sizeof(float));
    if (temps == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    float value;
    printf("Enter temperatures (-999 to stop):\n");
    scanf("%f", &value);

    while (value != -999) {
        if (count == capacity) {
            capacity = capacity + 4;
            temps = (float *)realloc(temps, capacity * sizeof(float));
            if (temps == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }
            printf("[capacity increased to %d]\n", capacity);
        }
        temps[count] = value;
        count++;
        scanf("%f", &value);
    }

    int i, below = 0;
    float sum = 0, highest = temps[0];
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        sum += temps[i];
        if (temps[i] > highest) highest = temps[i];
    }
    float avg = sum / count;

    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) if (temps[i] < avg) below++;

    printf("Readings entered (%d): ", count);
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%.1f ", temps[i]);
    printf("\n");
    printf("Final capacity : %d\n", capacity);
    printf("Average : %.2f\n", avg);
    printf("Highest : %.1f\n", highest);
    printf("Below average : %d\n", below);

    free(temps);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int capacity = 4, count = 0;Starting capacity as required, and the real count of stored readings, distinct from capacity.
float *temps = (float *)malloc(capacity * sizeof(float));Initial allocation sized to match the starting capacity.
scanf("%f", &value); while (value != -999) {Reads the first value before the condition is checked at all — a sentinel-controlled loop, exactly like B3.
if (count == capacity) { capacity += 4; temps = realloc(...); print message; }Checked at the very start of every pass, before storing — grows by the fixed increment of 4 the instant the array is completely full.
temps[count] = value; count++;Stores the value once room is guaranteed, then advances the real count.
scanf("%f", &value);Reads the next value at the very end of the loop body, so the sentinel gets tested without ever being stored.
float sum = 0, highest = temps[0];Seeds the running maximum with the first real reading (not an arbitrary guess), since temperatures could be negative.
for (i = 0; i < count; i++) { sum += temps[i]; if (temps[i]>highest) highest=temps[i]; }Loops only over the real readings (count, not capacity), accumulating the sum and tracking the maximum in one pass.
float avg = sum / count;Computes the average; no explicit cast needed here since sum is already a float.
for (i = 0; i < count; i++) if (temps[i] < avg) below++;A second pass, needed because the average couldn't be known until every reading was already summed.
free(temps);Releases the final allocated block.

Identical shape to B3, just with a different sentinel (-999 instead of -1) and a different growth increment (+4 instead of +5) — and using float throughout since temperatures aren't whole numbers.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests whether you can adapt B3's exact skeleton to a different data type (float instead of int) and a different sentinel value, without getting the read-before/check-grow-store/read-after ordering wrong.
Likely variations on the exam A different sentinel or growth increment, doubling capacity instead of a fixed increment, or extra derived statistics.
How to approach it Read one value before the loop; loop while it isn't the sentinel; check/grow capacity at the top of the loop body before storing; store and increment; read the next value at the very bottom.
DMA Variation 4 — known-increment resize [10] mirrors B4

Variation 4 — known-increment resize (mirrors B4). A classroom has N students already seated. Take N, malloc N seat-scores (a participation score per student), read them. Ask how many more students M are joining the class, resize to hold N+M scores (keeping the existing ones), read the M new scores. Print all scores, the total, and the average, then free the memory.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    int n, m, i;
    printf("Number of students already seated: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    int *scores = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    if (scores == NULL) { printf("Allocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("Score of student %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%d", &scores[i]);
    }

    printf("How many more students are joining? ");
    scanf("%d", &m);

    scores = (int *)realloc(scores, (n + m) * sizeof(int));
    if (scores == NULL) { printf("Reallocation failed\n"); return 1; }

    for (i = n; i < n + m; i++) {
        printf("Score of student %d: ", i + 1);
        scanf("%d", &scores[i]);
    }

    int total_n = n + m, total = 0;
    printf("All %d scores: ", total_n);
    for (i = 0; i < total_n; i++) { printf("%d ", scores[i]); total += scores[i]; }
    printf("\n");

    printf("Total : %d\n", total);
    printf("Average : %.2f\n", (float)total / total_n);
    free(scores);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int *scores = (int *)malloc(n * sizeof(int));Initial allocation for exactly the first n students.
for (i = 0; i < n; i++) { scanf("%d", &scores[i]); }Reads the first batch of scores.
scanf("%d", &m);Reads exactly how many new students are joining — known directly, unlike D3's sentinel loop.
scores = (int *)realloc(scores, (n + m) * sizeof(int));Grows the block once, straight to the final combined size, preserving the first n scores automatically.
for (i = n; i < n + m; i++) { scanf("%d", &scores[i]); }Fills only the newly added slots, starting at index n so the existing scores are left untouched.
int total_n = n + m, total = 0;Final combined count, computed once and reused; total starts at 0.
for (i = 0; i < total_n; i++) { printf(...); total += scores[i]; }A single final loop over all students, old and new together, printing each and accumulating the total.
printf("Average : %.2f\n", (float)total / total_n);Casts total to float before dividing, since total is an int here (unlike B4 where salaries were already floats).
free(scores);Releases the memory.

Identical shape to B4: the exact number of new items is known up front, so the array is resized exactly once to its final size, rather than growing incrementally like D3.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Confirms whether you default to the simpler one-shot resize whenever the exact new count is known, rather than reaching for a sentinel-and-grow loop that isn't needed here — and whether you remember to cast before dividing when the accumulator is an int (unlike B4, where the accumulator was already a float).
Likely variations on the exam Filtering the new students by some threshold before adding them, multiple resize rounds chained together, or being asked for the two groups' averages separately instead of combined.
How to approach it When the exact extra count is known, realloc once to (oldCount+extra) * sizeof(type), then fill only the new region starting at oldCount. Watch the data type of your running total — cast to float before dividing whenever the total itself is an int.

Strings — 4 most probable variations

Strings Variation 1 — word-start test, mixed delimiters [6] mirrors C1

Variation 1 — word-start test with mixed delimiters (mirrors C1). Count the words in char str[] = "rice, dal, egg , milk"; where a word can be separated by a comma or a space, possibly several in a row. No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int isSep(char c) {
    return (c == ' ' || c == ',');
}

int main() {
    char str[] = "rice, dal,  egg , milk";
    int i, wordCount = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (!isSep(str[i]) && (i == 0 || isSep(str[i - 1]))) {
            wordCount++;
        }
    }

    printf("String : %s\n", str);
    printf("Words : %d\n", wordCount);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int isSep(char c) { return (c == ' ' || c == ','); }A small helper function: returns true (1) if c is either kind of separator, false (0) otherwise — keeps the main loop's condition readable.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once, from start to terminator.
if (!isSep(str[i]) && (i == 0 || isSep(str[i - 1]))) {The exact same word-start test as C1, just built on isSep(c) instead of a single c==' ' test: current character is not a separator, AND (it's the first character OR the previous one was a separator).
wordCount++;Fires exactly once per word, at the character where it begins.

Only the separator test changes from C1 — the word-start logic itself is completely unchanged, just built on a small helper function instead of a single character comparison.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests whether you can generalise the word-start test to "any of several possible separators" by wrapping the separator check in its own small function, rather than hard-coding a single character comparison.
Likely variations on the exam A different pair/set of separator characters, or a request to also print each word individually (tokenising) instead of just counting.
How to approach it Write a tiny isSep(c) (or inline || chain) that returns true for any valid separator, then plug it into the unchanged word-start test: !isSep(str[i]) && (i==0 || isSep(str[i-1])).
Strings Variation 2 — character classification [6] mirrors C2

Variation 2 — character classification (mirrors C2). Scan char str[] = "Dhaka-2026: BRAC Univ!"; once and report how many uppercase letters, lowercase letters, digits, and other characters (including spaces) it contains. No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Dhaka-2026: BRAC Univ!";
    int i, upper = 0, lower = 0, digits = 0, others = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char c = str[i];
        if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') {
            upper++;
        } else if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') {
            lower++;
        } else if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') {
            digits++;
        } else {
            others++;
        }
    }

    printf("String : %s\n", str);
    printf("Upper: %d Lower: %d Digits: %d Others: %d\n", upper, lower, digits, others);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int i, upper=0, lower=0, digits=0, others=0;Four independent counters, all started at 0.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the string once.
char c = str[i];Copies the current character for readability.
if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') upper++;Tests the uppercase ASCII range.
else if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') lower++;Only checked if not uppercase; tests the lowercase range.
else if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') digits++;Only checked if not a letter at all; tests the digit range.
else others++;Everything left over — spaces, punctuation, symbols — falls here.

Same 4-way if/else-if ladder shape as C2, just with uppercase and lowercase reported as two separate categories instead of combined into "letters" split into vowel/consonant.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests the same ASCII range-comparison toolkit as C2, with a slightly different category list — confirming you rebuild the ladder from the category list given, rather than memorising C2's specific categories.
Likely variations on the exam The category list changing again (vowels vs consonants within letters, spaces separated out from other punctuation), or percentages requested instead of raw counts.
How to approach it List the exact categories asked for, then write one if/else-if ladder testing each ASCII range in turn, ending with a catch-all else for whatever's left.
Strings Variation 3 — filter-then-check, ignoring digits too [6] mirrors C4

Variation 3 — filter-then-check palindrome, ignoring digits too (mirrors C4). Decide whether char str[] = "12 A man a plan a canal Panama 21"; is a palindrome once spaces, case, and digits are all ignored (so only the letters matter). No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "12 A man a plan a canal Panama 21";
    char cleaned[50];
    int i, k = 0;

    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char c = str[i];
        int isLetter = (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z');
        if (!isLetter) continue;
        if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32;
        cleaned[k] = c;
        k++;
    }
    cleaned[k] = '\0';

    int isPalin = 1;
    int left = 0, right = k - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        if (cleaned[left] != cleaned[right]) { isPalin = 0; break; }
        left++;
        right--;
    }

    printf("String : %s\n", str);
    printf("Cleaned : %s\n", cleaned);
    printf("Result : %s\n", isPalin ? "Palindrome" : "Not a palindrome");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
char cleaned[50]; int i, k = 0;A second array for the filtered copy, plus its own write index started at 0.
for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {Walks the original string once.
int isLetter = (c>='a'&&c<='z') || (c>='A'&&c<='Z');A single combined test: is this character a letter at all, in either case?
if (!isLetter) continue;Anything that ISN'T a letter — spaces, digits, punctuation — is skipped entirely; this one test replaces C4's separate space-only check, automatically dropping digits too.
if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') c = c + 32;Normalises to lowercase, exactly as in C4, before copying.
cleaned[k] = c; k++;Writes the normalised letter into the next free slot.
cleaned[k] = '\0';Terminates the filtered copy.
int left = 0, right = k - 1; while (left < right) { ... }The standard two-pointer palindrome check, unchanged from C4, run on the newly-cleaned letters-only copy.

Only the filter condition changed from C4: instead of "skip only spaces", it's "keep only letters" (isLetter), which automatically drops spaces AND digits in one single test — the two-pointer check itself is completely unchanged.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests whether you can widen the filter condition in the filter-then-check design (from "ignore spaces" to "keep only letters") without needing to touch the two-pointer comparison logic at all — the two stages stay cleanly separated.
Likely variations on the exam The filter narrowing even further (letters and digits both kept, only punctuation dropped), or being asked to also report the count of characters that were ignored.
How to approach it When a filter rule needs to widen or narrow, change only the if condition that decides what gets copied into the cleaned buffer — the copy loop and the two-pointer check underneath never need to change.
Strings Variation 4 — combining 3 primitives [6] mirrors C8

Variation 4 — combining primitives (mirrors C8). Print a dotted acronym of char str[] = "Bangladesh University of Engineering and Technology"; — the first letter of every word with 4 or more letters, in capitals, separated by dots, skipping short connector words like "of" and "and". No built-in functions.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    char str[] = "Bangladesh University of Engineering and Technology";
    int i;

    printf("Acronym : ");
    for (i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        int isStart = (str[i] != ' ' && (i == 0 || str[i - 1] == ' '));
        if (!isStart) continue;

        int len = 0;
        while (str[i + len] != '\0' && str[i + len] != ' ') len++;
        if (len < 4) continue;

        char c = str[i];
        if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') c = c - 32;
        printf("%c.", c);
    }
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int isStart = (str[i]!=' ' && (i==0||str[i-1]==' '));Exactly the C1 word-start test, stored in a named variable for clarity.
if (!isStart) continue;Skip everything except the first character of each word.
int len = 0; while (str[i+len]!='\0' && str[i+len]!=' ') len++;A small inner loop that measures how many characters this specific word has, without disturbing the outer index i — it looks ahead using i+len and only ever changes len.
if (len < 4) continue;Skips short connector words ("of" has length 2, "and" has length 3) before printing anything for them.
char c = str[i]; if (c>='a'&&c<='z') c = c-32;Forces this word-starting letter to uppercase, exactly as in C8.
printf("%c.", c);Prints the letter followed by a literal dot separator.

Three primitives stacked together: C1's word-start test decides where to look, a small length-measuring loop (borrowed from C3's counting idea) decides whether to skip short words, and C5's case-forcing trick makes sure every printed letter is uppercase.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the clearest test of whether you can chain three primitives together (word-start, length-measuring, case-forcing) rather than just two, which is exactly the direction "combine known primitives" questions escalate toward on the real exam.
Likely variations on the exam The length threshold changing, a different separator (space, comma, or none at all), or being asked to keep the connector words but lowercase them instead of dropping them entirely.
How to approach it Break any "new-looking" string question into the primitives it's built from: identify which parts are word-start detection, which are measuring/counting, and which are character transformation, then combine them in the order the problem describes.

Pointer Tracing — 4 most probable variations

Pointer Tracing Variation 1 — reassigning a pointer mid-program trap: p redirected partway through

Variation 1 — reassigning a pointer mid-program. Trace the program and give its complete output.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a = 4, b = 9;
    int *p = &a;

    *p = *p + 6;
    p = &b;
    *p = *p - 2;
    a = a + *p;

    printf("%d %d\n", a, b);
    return 0;
}
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a = 4, b = 9;
    int *p = &a;

    *p = *p + 6;
    p = &b;
    *p = *p - 2;
    a = a + *p;

    printf("%d %d\n", a, b);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int a = 4, b = 9;Two ordinary integers.
int *p = &a;p is set to point at a.
*p = *p + 6;*p currently means "a" — so this line is really a = a + 6.
p = &b;p is redirected to point at b instead. From this line onward, *p means "b", not "a", even though the variable is still called p.
*p = *p - 2;*p now means "b" — so this is really b = b - 2.
a = a + *p;*p still means "b" (unchanged since the reassignment) — so this is really a = a + b, using b's current (already-updated) value.

Full trace table:

Lineabp points to
init49—
int *p=&a;49a
*p=*p+6; (a=4+6)109a
p=&b;109b
*p=*p-2; (b=9-2)107b
a=a+*p; (a=10+7)177b

Output: 17 7. The trap: once p = &b; runs, every *p after that means "b", not "a" — the variable name p never changes, only what it points to does.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking The most fundamental pointer-tracing trap: a pointer being reassigned partway through the program, so its meaning silently changes for every line after the reassignment.
Likely variations on the exam More reassignments in sequence, or a reassignment inside a loop/conditional so it only sometimes happens.
How to approach it Track a running "p currently points to ___" column separately from the value columns. Update it the instant a reassignment line runs, and use whatever it currently says for every *p that follows.
Pointer Tracing Variation 2 — pass-by-value vs pass-by-pointer the core contrast

Variation 2 — pass-by-value vs pass-by-pointer. Trace the program and give its complete output, and explain why the two calls behave differently.

#include <stdio.h>
void doubleValue(int x) {
    x = x * 2;
    printf("Inside doubleValue: %d\n", x);
}
void doubleReference(int *y) {
    *y = *y * 2;
    printf("Inside doubleReference: %d\n", *y);
}
int main() {
    int n = 7;
    doubleValue(n);
    printf("After doubleValue: %d\n", n);
    doubleReference(&n);
    printf("After doubleReference: %d\n", n);
    return 0;
}
#include <stdio.h>
void doubleValue(int x) {
    x = x * 2;
    printf("Inside doubleValue: %d\n", x);
}
void doubleReference(int *y) {
    *y = *y * 2;
    printf("Inside doubleReference: %d\n", *y);
}
int main() {
    int n = 7;
    doubleValue(n);
    printf("After doubleValue: %d\n", n);
    doubleReference(&n);
    printf("After doubleReference: %d\n", n);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
void doubleValue(int x) {Takes an ordinary int parameter — C always copies the argument's value into this parameter.
x = x * 2;Modifies only the local copy x; the caller's original variable is completely untouched by this line.
void doubleReference(int *y) {Takes a pointer parameter — the caller's address is copied in, not a copy of the value.
*y = *y * 2;Dereferences y to reach the caller's actual variable and writes a new value directly into it — there is no separate copy involved.
int n = 7;The variable both functions will be called on.
doubleValue(n);Passes a copy of n's value (7) into doubleValue.
doubleReference(&n);Passes n's address, allowing doubleReference to modify n itself.

Full trace table:

CallWhat happensn in main afterward
doubleValue(n)local x=7×2=14 printed; n untouched (pass-by-value)7
doubleReference(&n)*y writes directly into n: n=7×2=1414

Full output:

Inside doubleValue: 14
After doubleValue: 7
Inside doubleReference: 14
After doubleReference: 14
Claude explains
What this question is really asking The cleanest possible contrast between pass-by-value (a copy, so changes never escape the function) and pass-by-pointer (the real address, so changes are permanent) — this exact pairing is one of the most reliably tested tracing formats.
Likely variations on the exam The value-parameter function being called a second time to show the copy resets each call, or a third function that takes a pointer but conditionally chooses not to write through it.
How to approach it Ask of every function parameter: is it a plain type (a copy, changes are local and temporary) or a pointer (the real address, changes are permanent and visible to the caller)? Trace accordingly.
Pointer Tracing Variation 3 — order of evaluation trap: RHS evaluated with OLD values first

Variation 3 — order of evaluation. Trace the program and give its complete output. Watch the order of evaluation carefully on the last two lines.

#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int x = 3, y = 5, z = 8;
    int *p = &x;

    *p = *p + 2;
    p = &y;
    *p = *p + x;
    p = &z;
    z = *p + y;
    *p = x + y + z;

    printf("%d %d %d\n", x, y, z);
    return 0;
}
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int x = 3, y = 5, z = 8;
    int *p = &x;

    *p = *p + 2;
    p = &y;
    *p = *p + x;
    p = &z;
    z = *p + y;
    *p = x + y + z;

    printf("%d %d %d\n", x, y, z);
    return 0;
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int x = 3, y = 5, z = 8;Three ordinary integers.
int *p = &x;p points at x.
*p = *p + 2;*p means x: x = 3+2 = 5.
p = &y;p redirected to y.
*p = *p + x;*p now means y: y = 5 + 5 (x's current value) = 10.
p = &z;p redirected to z.
z = *p + y;The trap: *p means z, but this reads z's old value (8), BEFORE this same line overwrites it — C fully evaluates the right-hand side with old values first, then assigns. So z = 8 (old z) + 10 (y) = 18.
*p = x + y + z;*p still means z, and this line sees z's just-updated value (18) from the line immediately above, since statements run strictly in order: z = 5 + 10 + 18 = 33.

Full trace table:

Linep points toxyz
init—358
int *p=&x;x358
*p=*p+2;x558
p=&y;y558
*p=*p+x; (y=5+5)y5108
p=&z;z5108
z=*p+y; (uses OLD z=8)z51018
*p=x+y+z; (uses NEW z=18)z51033

Output: 5 10 33.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking Tests whether you know that C fully evaluates a statement's right-hand side using old values before performing the assignment — even when the assignment target is the same variable being read on the right-hand side through a pointer.
Likely variations on the exam The self-referencing assignment appearing earlier in the trace instead of at the very end, or two consecutive self-referencing lines back to back.
How to approach it Whenever a line assigns to a variable that also appears (directly or through *p) on its own right-hand side, evaluate the right-hand side completely first using the value that variable had before this line runs, then perform the assignment. The next line then sees the brand-new value.
Pointer Tracing Variation 4 — combined scoping + pointer + function (Assignment 2 Q4 standard) the hardest variety — everything combined

Variation 4 — combined scoping + pointer + function trace (Assignment 2 Q4 standard, hardest variety). Trace the program and give its complete output. Identify which rate each line refers to before tracing any values.

#include <stdio.h>
int rate = 2;

void apply(int *pool, int rate) {
    int bonus = 3;
    rate = rate + bonus;
    *pool = *pool + rate;
    bonus = bonus + 1;
    printf("B:%d,%d,%d\n", rate, *pool, bonus);
}

int boost(int rate) {
    int extra = rate + 5;
    return extra;
}

void main() {
    int j;
    int rate = 1;
    int pool = 10;
    int *pp = &pool;

    for (j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
        if (j == 2) {
            rate = rate + 4;
        }
        apply(pp, rate);
        rate = boost(rate);
        printf("j=%d rate=%d pool=%d\n", j, rate, pool);
    }
    printf("Final: rate=%d pool=%d\n", rate, pool);
}
#include <stdio.h>
int rate = 2;

void apply(int *pool, int rate) {
    int bonus = 3;
    rate = rate + bonus;
    *pool = *pool + rate;
    bonus = bonus + 1;
    printf("B:%d,%d,%d\n", rate, *pool, bonus);
}

int boost(int rate) {
    int extra = rate + 5;
    return extra;
}

void main() {
    int j;
    int rate = 1;
    int pool = 10;
    int *pp = &pool;

    for (j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
        if (j == 2) {
            rate = rate + 4;
        }
        apply(pp, rate);
        rate = boost(rate);
        printf("j=%d rate=%d pool=%d\n", j, rate, pool);
    }
    printf("Final: rate=%d pool=%d\n", rate, pool);
}
Professor's explanation
Line-by-line breakdown
int rate = 2;A global rate. Every function below (main, apply, boost) declares its own local/parameter also named rate, so this global value of 2 is a decoy, never actually read anywhere in this program.
void apply(int *pool, int rate) {pool is a pointer (always called with pp, which points at main's pool); rate is an ordinary by-value parameter — a fresh copy every call.
int bonus = 3;A local, reset to 3 on every single call to apply.
rate = rate + bonus;Modifies only apply's own local parameter copy of rate; cannot affect main's rate.
*pool = *pool + rate;Writes through the pointer: permanently increases main's pool, using apply's own (just-updated) local rate.
bonus = bonus + 1;Only affects the local bonus, which only matters for the printf immediately below.
printf("B:%d,%d,%d\n", rate, *pool, bonus);Prints apply's own local rate, the current value pointed to by pool (main's pool), and the local bonus.
int boost(int rate) {A separate function with its own separate parameter rate — the third distinct "rate" in this program.
int extra = rate + 5;Computes a value from this function's own local parameter copy.
return extra;The only way any information from inside boost reaches its caller, since boost has no pointer parameter at all.
int rate = 1;Main's own local rate, starting at 1 — the only rate that persists across loop iterations.
int pool = 10;Main's running pool value, starting at 10.
int *pp = &pool;Points permanently at main's pool; never reassigned.
for (j = 0; j < 3; j++) {Runs for j = 0, 1, 2 — 3 passes.
if (j == 2) { rate = rate + 4; }Only on the third pass does main directly bump its own rate by 4, before calling anything.
apply(pp, rate);Calls apply with the pointer pp and the current value of main's rate, copied by value into apply's own parameter.
rate = boost(rate);Calls boost with main's current rate, and immediately overwrites main's own rate with whatever boost returns — the only line that changes main's rate based on a function result.
printf("j=%d rate=%d pool=%d\n", j, rate, pool);Prints main's own values, after both calls above have already run this iteration.
printf("Final: rate=%d pool=%d\n", rate, pool);Printed once, after the loop, with main's final values.

Full trace table:

jmain's rate entering apply()apply() prints (B:rate,*pool,bonus)pool after apply()main's rate after boost()
01B:4,14,4 (local rate=1+3=4; pool=10+4=14; bonus=3+1=4)14boost(1)=1+5=6
16B:9,23,4 (local rate=6+3=9; pool=14+9=23; bonus=4)23boost(6)=6+5=11
211+4=15 (the j==2 bump fires first)B:18,41,4 (local rate=15+3=18; pool=23+18=41; bonus=4)41boost(15)=15+5=20

Full output, in order:

B:4,14,4
j=0 rate=6 pool=14
B:9,23,4
j=1 rate=11 pool=23
B:18,41,4
j=2 rate=20 pool=41
Final: rate=20 pool=41

How to trace this specific table: exactly the same method as Assignment 2 Q4: (1) list every rate separately before touching values — there are 4 here (global, main, apply's parameter, boost's parameter); (2) only main's own rate and pool persist across iterations, since every function-local variable is recreated fresh on each call; (3) pp is set once and never reassigned, so every *pool inside apply means main's pool for the whole program; (4) apply changes pool permanently through the pointer, but its own rate changes are thrown away the instant the function returns; (5) the only way boost's work reaches main is because main explicitly writes rate = boost(rate); — if that line instead just said boost(rate);, main's rate would never change from it at all; (6) re-check every if against the current loop's own j value, fresh, every single pass.

Claude explains
What this question is really asking This is the hardest, most exam-realistic tracing variety: it combines everything from the other three variations at once — a decoy global, several functions each shadowing the same variable name, a pointer write that permanently changes the caller's variable, and a return value that the caller must explicitly capture to have any lasting effect. This is exactly Assignment 2 Q4's format and difficulty.
Likely variations on the exam An extra function, a pointer that gets reassigned partway through (combining this with Variation 1's trap), or a self-referencing assignment inside one of the functions (combining this with Variation 3's trap). The real exam often layers 2–3 of these tricks into one single tracing question.
How to approach it Follow the 6-step method spelled out above: separate every same-named variable first, identify what persists across loop iterations (only the caller's own locals), fix any pointer's target, and carefully distinguish a function's two possible escape routes — writing through a pointer (immediate and automatic) versus returning a value (only takes effect if the caller captures it).
